\mathcal{L}^{(D)}_{ij}(\rho):=a_{ij}\Big(P_{i}\rho P_{j}-\tfrac{1}{2}P_{j}P_{i}\rho-\tfrac{1}{2}\rho P_{j}P_{i}\Big)

is a linear combination of three maps of diamond norm
1
, so

\|\mathcal{L}^{(D)}_{ij}\|_{\diamond}\leq 2|a_{ij}|\leq 2
. Thus

\|\mathcal{L}_{D}\|_{\diamond}:=\Big\|\sum_{i,j}\mathcal{L}^{(D)}_{ij}\Big\|_{\diamond}\leq\sum_{i,j}\|\mathcal{L}^{(D)}_{ij}\|_{\diamond}\leq 2M_{D}^{2}.

(34)

Finally,
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}_{H}+\mathcal{L}_{D}
implies

\norm{\Lindblad}_{\diamond}\leq\|\mathcal{L}_{H}\|_{\diamond}+\|\mathcal{L}_{D}\|_{\diamond}\leq 2M_{H}+2M_{D}^{2}=2M.\qed

Corollary B.3

(Diamond norm bound for a diagonal Pauli dissipator)
.

Assume
\mathcal{L}
has no Hamiltonian part (
h_{i}=0
for all
i
) and has only
diagonal Kossakowski entries, i.e.
a_{ij}=0
for all
i\neq j
, with at most

M_{D}
nonzero diagonal coefficients
a_{ii}
satisfying
|a_{ii}|\leq 1
. Then

\|\mathcal{L}\|_{\diamond}\;\leq\;2M_{D}.

(35)

Proof.

This is Lemma
B.3
with
M_{H}=0
and with at most

M_{D}
nonzero dissipative terms (rather than
M_{D}^{2}
) since only the diagonal
coefficients
a_{ii}
are present. Hence
\|\mathcal{L}\|_{\diamond}\leq 2M_{D}
.
∎

B.4
Stabilizer QEC Preliminaries

B.4.1
Stabilizers, code space, and projector

A stabilizer group
S\subseteq\mathbb{P}_{n}
is an abelian subgroup with
-I\notin S
.
Fix a (possibly redundant) generating set
G=\{G_{\ell}\}_{\ell=1}^{m}
.
The code space
\mathcal{H}_{c}
is the joint
+1
eigenspace of all
G_{\ell}
, with orthogonal projector

\Pi=\frac{1}{|S|}\sum_{\sigma\in S}\sigma=\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}\frac{I+G_{\ell}}{2},\qquad\sigma\Pi=\Pi\sigma=\Pi\ \ \forall\sigma\in S.

(36)

We will repeatedly work on the corner algebra:

\mathfrak{A}_{c}:=\Pi\,\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})\,\Pi\cong\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{c}),\quad\text{so that }\ \Pi X\Pi=X\ \text{ for }X\in\mathfrak{A}_{c}.

(37)

Define the corresponding corner projection map
\mathcal{C}_{\Pi}:\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})\to\mathfrak{A}_{c}
:

\mathcal{C}_{\Pi}(X)\coloneq\Pi X\Pi,\qquad\forall X\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})

(38)

Definition B.4

(Detectability)
.

A Pauli
Q\in\mathbb{P}_{n}
is
detectable
by the stabilizer code if it
anticommutes with at least one generator in
G
(equivalently, with some
\sigma\in S
).

Proposition B.5

(Detectability
\Longleftrightarrow
compression to zero)
.

For a Pauli
Q\in\mathbb{P}_{n}
and stabilizer projector
\Pi
, the following are equivalent:

(i)

Q
is detectable, i.e., there exists
g\in G
with
\{g,Q\}=0
;

(ii)

\Pi Q\Pi=0
.

Consequently, for all
X\in\mathfrak{A}_{c}
,
\Pi QX\Pi=0
and
\Pi\{Q,X\}\Pi=0
.

Proof.

(i)
\Rightarrow
(ii): Pick
g\in G
with
\{g,Q\}=0
. Then

g(\Pi Q\Pi)=(\Pi g)Q\Pi=\Pi(gQ)\Pi=-\Pi(Qg)\Pi=-(\Pi Q\Pi)g.

Since
g
acts as the identity on
\mathcal{H}_{c}
, the only operator on the code
that anticommutes with
g
is
0
, hence
\Pi Q\Pi=0
.

(ii)
\Rightarrow
(i): Suppose
\Pi Q\Pi=0
and, for contradiction, that
Q
commutes
with every generator
G_{\ell}
. Then
Q
preserves the code space:
for any
\psi\in\mathcal{H}_{c}
we have
G_{\ell}(Q\psi)=Q(G_{\ell}\psi)=Q\psi
, so
Q\psi\in\mathcal{H}_{c}
.
Therefore
\Pi Q\Pi\,\psi=\Pi(Q\psi)=Q\psi
. But
\Pi Q\Pi=0
forces
Q\psi=0

for all
\psi\in\mathcal{H}_{c}
, which is impossible since
Q
is unitary and

\mathcal{H}_{c}\neq\{0\}
. Hence some generator must anticommute with
Q
.
∎

B.4.2
Normalizer and logical Paulis

The
normalizer
(same as centralizer in
\mathbb{P}_{n}
) of
S
in the Pauli group is

N(S):=\{P\in\mathbb{P}_{n}:\ [P,\sigma]=0\ \forall\,\sigma\in S\}
.
For
Q\in N(S)
we define its logical action on the code by

\overline{Q}\;:=\;\Pi Q\Pi\in\mathfrak{A}_{c}.

(39)

If
Q\in S
then
\overline{Q}=\Pi
(identity on the code);
if
Q\in N(S)\setminus S
then
\overline{Q}
is a nontrivial unitary on

\mathcal{H}_{c}
(a
logical Pauli
).

B.4.3
Syndromes and syndrome projectors

Syndrome (commutation) map.

For a Pauli
P\in\mathbb{P}_{n}
define its
syndrome
with respect to the
generators
G=\{G_{\ell}\}_{\ell=1}^{m}
by

r(P)\in\{0,1\}^{m},\qquad r_{\ell}(P)=\begin{cases}0,&[P,G_{\ell}]=0,\\[2.0pt]
1,&\{P,G_{\ell}\}=0.\end{cases}

(40)

Equivalently,
G_{\ell}P=(-1)^{r_{\ell}(P)}\,PG_{\ell}
.
Then:

(a)

r(PQ)=r(P)\oplus r(Q)
for all
P,Q\in\mathbb{P}_{n}
.

(b)

r(P)=\mathbf{0}
iff
P\in N(S)
(i.e.
P
commutes with the stabilizer group).

Syndrome projectors.

Given
G=\{G_{\ell}\}_{\ell=1}^{m}
, define syndrome projectors

\Pi_{s}\;:=\;\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}\frac{I+(-1)^{s_{\ell}}\,G_{\ell}}{2},\qquad s\in\{0,1\}^{m},\qquad\sum_{s}\Pi_{s}=I,\ \ \Pi_{s}\Pi_{s^{\prime}}=\delta_{s,s^{\prime}}\Pi_{s},

(41)

with
\Pi_{(0,\ldots,0)}=\Pi
.

Shift / block identities.

For every
s,t\in\{0,1\}^{m}
and
P\in\mathbb{P}_{n}
,

\displaystyle\Pi_{s}\,P

\displaystyle=P\,\Pi_{\,s\oplus r(P)},\qquad\qquad P\,\Pi_{s}=\Pi_{\,s\oplus r(P)}\,P,

(42)

\displaystyle\Pi_{s}\,P\,\Pi_{t}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}0,&t\neq s\oplus r(P),\\[2.0pt]
\Pi_{s}\,P=P\,\Pi_{t},&t=s\oplus r(P).\end{cases}

(43)

Proof sketch.
Using (
41
),

\Pi_{s}=\prod_{\ell=1}^{m}\frac{I+(-1)^{s_{\ell}}G_{\ell}}{2}.

Move
P
through each factor with
G_{\ell}P=(-1)^{r_{\ell}(P)}PG_{\ell}
; each factor gains
the bit flip
s_{\ell}\mapsto s_{\ell}\oplus r_{\ell}(P)
, yielding (
42
).
Multiplying on the right by
\Pi_{t}
and using orthogonality

\Pi_{u}\Pi_{v}=\delta_{u,v}\Pi_{u}
gives (
43
).
∎

Detectability in terms of
r(\cdot)
.

A Pauli
Q
is detectable iff
r(Q)\neq\mathbf{0}
(rephrasing of
Section

B.4.1
).
Conversely, if
r(Q)=\mathbf{0}
then
Q
preserves the code and

\overline{Q}=\Pi Q\Pi
is its logical action (see (
39
)).

B.4.4
Binary symplectic representation

We use the standard binary symplectic picture

61
;
102
;
62
.
In particular, we use the fact that there is an isomorphism:

\mathcal{P}_{n}\ \cong\ \mathbb{F}_{2}^{2n},\qquad P\ \longleftrightarrow\ v_{P}=(x_{P}\,|\,z_{P}),\ \ P=\bigotimes_{j=1}^{n}X_{j}^{x_{P,j}}Z_{j}^{z_{P,j}},

with the symplectic product

\langle v,w\rangle_{s}\;:=\;x\!\cdot z^{\prime}\,+\,z\!\cdot x^{\prime}\ \in\ \mathbb{F}_{2}\quad\text{for }v=(x|z),\ w=(x^{\prime}|z^{\prime}).

Then
[P,Q]=0
iff
\langle v_{P},v_{Q}\rangle_{s}=0
, and

\{P,Q\}=0
iff
\langle v_{P},v_{Q}\rangle_{s}=1
.

Syndrome matrix and normalizer as a nullspace.

Let
S
be generated by
G=\{G_{\ell}\}_{\ell=1}^{s}
with binary rows

h_{\ell}:=v_{G_{\ell}}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2n}
. Form the
s\times 2n
matrix

H
with rows
h_{\ell}
. Let

\Lambda\;:=\;\begin{pmatrix}0&I_{n}\\
I_{n}&0\end{pmatrix},\qquad\langle u,v\rangle_{s}\;:=\;u^{\top}\Lambda v

be the standard symplectic form on
\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2n}
.
For any
P\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
with binary vector
v_{P}
, the syndrome is

r(P)\;=\;H\,\Lambda\,v_{P}\pmod{2},

(44)

so
r(P)=\mathbf{0}
iff
v_{P}\in\ker(H\Lambda)
.
Hence

N(S)\ \cong\ \{\,v\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2n}:H\Lambda v=0\,\}\;=\;(\mathrm{rowspan}(H))^{\perp_{s}},

where
(\cdot)^{\perp_{s}}
denotes orthogonality with respect to

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{s}
.
If the
s
generators are independent, then
\mathrm{rank}(H)=\mathrm{rank}(H\Lambda)=s
and

\dim_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}N(S)=2n-s
.

Logical Paulis and their count.

Two normalizer Paulis
P,Q\in N(S)
induce the same logical operator iff

PQ\in S
, i.e. iff
v_{P}-v_{Q}\in\mathrm{rowspan}(H)
.
Thus the space of logical Paulis (including identity) is the quotient

N(S)/S\ \cong\ (\ker(H\Lambda))/(\mathrm{rowspan}(H)),

which has

\dim_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}\bigl(N(S)/S\bigr)\;=\;(2n-s)-s\;=\;2(n-s).

Equivalently, if
k:=n-s
is the number of logical qubits, then there are

2^{2k}=4^{k}=4^{\,n-s}
distinct logical Paulis (including the logical identity).

Lemma B.6

(Linear algebra lemma (Gottesman
(
62
, Lem. 3.15)
))
.

Let
\{P_{1},\dots,P_{m}\}\subset\mathcal{P}_{n}
map to linearly independent

v_{P_{i}}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2n}
. For any choice
s\in\{0,1\}^{m}
there exists

Q\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
such that

\langle v_{P_{i}},v_{Q}\rangle_{s}=s_{i}

for all
i
. Moreover, the set of such
Q
has size
2^{\,2n-m}
.

Corollary B.7

(Every nontrivial logical has an anticommuting partner)
.

Let
P\in N(S)\setminus S
be a nontrivial logical Pauli. Then there exists

Q\in N(S)\setminus S
such that
\{P,Q\}=0
.

Proof.

Apply Lemma
B.4.4
to the independent set consisting of the

s
generators
G_{1},\dots,G_{s}
together with
P
. Choose the commutation
pattern
s=(0,\dots,0,1)
, i.e. commuting with each
G_{\ell}
and
anticommuting with
P
. The resulting
Q
commutes with all
G_{\ell}
,
hence
Q\in N(S)
, and anticommutes with
P
. Since every
S
-element
commutes with
P
, this
Q\notin S
.
∎

Corollary B.8

(Half of all logical Paulis anticommute with a fixed nontrivial logical)
.

Let
P\in N(S)\setminus S
be a nontrivial logical Pauli.
Then among the distinct logical Paulis
N(S)/S

exactly half anticommute with
\overline{P}
and
exactly half commute with
\overline{P}
.

Proof.

Apply Lemma
B.4.4
to the independent set consisting of the

m
generators
G_{1},\dots,G_{m}
together with
P
.
Choose the commutation pattern

s=(\underbrace{0,\ldots,0}_{m\ \text{entries}},1),

i.e., commute with each
G_{\ell}
and anticommute with
P
.
By Lemma
B.4.4
there are exactly

2^{\,2n-(m+1)}=2^{\,2n-m-1}
physical Paulis
Q
satisfying these
constraints. Every stabilizer element commutes with
P
, so none of these

Q
lies in
S
.

Passing to logical operators amounts to quotienting by
S
:
each logical class in
N(S)/S
has exactly
|S|=2^{\,m}
representatives
in
N(S)
. Hence the number of
distinct
logical Paulis that
anticommute with
\overline{P}
is

\frac{2^{\,2n-m-1}}{2^{\,m}}\;=\;2^{\,2n-2m-1}\;=\;2^{\,2(n-m)-1}.

Repeating the argument with the pattern

(0,\ldots,0,0)
(commuting also with
P
) yields the same count for the
logical Paulis that commute with
\overline{P}
.
Since the total number of logical Paulis is
2^{\,2(n-m)}
,
exactly half anticommute with
\overline{P}
and half commute.
∎

Below, we cite a crucial lemma for Lindbladian reshaping (Appendix
C
):

Lemma B.9

(Logical Pauli twirling
46
)
.

Let
\overline{P}
be a logical Pauli string of some code and let
\overline{\mathcal{K}}_{\overline{P}_{a}}
be the commuting logical set of a target non-identity logical Pauli
{\overline{P}_{a}}
:

\overline{\mathcal{K}}_{\overline{P}_{a}}\;:=\;\{\overline{Q}\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}:\ [\overline{Q},\overline{P}_{a}]=0\}.

Then

\frac{1}{|\overline{\mathcal{K}}_{\overline{P}_{a}}|}\sum_{\overline{Q}\in\overline{\mathcal{K}}_{\overline{P}_{a}}}\overline{Q}\,\overline{P}\,\overline{Q}\;=\;\begin{cases}\;\overline{P},&\overline{P}\in\{\overline{P}_{a},I\},\\[2.0pt]
\;0,&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Proof.

If
\overline{P}\in\{\overline{P}_{a},I\}
, then by construction,
\overline{P}
commutes with all
\overline{Q}
. Hence,
\overline{Q}\,\overline{P}\,\overline{Q}=\overline{P}
and the entire sum is
\overline{P}
.

If
\overline{P}\notin\{\overline{P}_{a},I\}
, then we prove that half of logical Paulis in
\overline{\mathcal{K}}_{\overline{P}_{a}}
anti-commute with
\overline{P}
and half commute, hence averaging to zero. For that, apply Lemma
B.4.4
to the independent set consisting of the

m
Stabilizer generators
G_{1},\dots,G_{m}
together with
\overline{P}_{a},\overline{P}
. The anti-commuting logical Paulis have the following commutation pattern:

s=(\underbrace{0,\ldots,0}_{m\ \text{entries}},0,1),

By Lemma
B.4.4
there are exactly

2^{\,2n-(m+2)}=2^{\,2n-m-2}
physical Paulis, and hence
2^{\,2n-m-2}/2^{m}
logical Paulis (taking quotient by
S
) satisfying these constraints. This constitutes exactly one half of
\overline{\mathcal{K}}_{\overline{P}_{a}}
(by
Section

B.4.4
). Since Paulis either commute or anti-commute, the other half of
\overline{\mathcal{K}}_{\overline{P}_{a}}
commutes with
\overline{P}
, concluding the proof.
∎

B.5
Polynomial interpolation

Here we introduce the polynomial-interpolation primitive used for derivative estimation. This primitive is used in Appendix
F.3
, where Lindbladian coefficients are obtained from first derivatives at
t=0
of scalar functions of the form
f(t)\coloneq\tr\!\left(O\,(e^{t\mathcal{L}}\otimes I_{R})(\rho_{0})\right)
. In this subsection, however, we keep the discussion general.

Let
f:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
be a sufficiently smooth scalar function. Suppose we have access to noisy estimates
\widehat{f}(t)
of
f(t)
. The idea is to esitmate
f
at a set of carefully chosen nodes on the interval
[0,\tau_{\max}]
, fit a degree-
r
polynomial interpolant through those values, and then take the analytical derivative of the fitted polynomial to obtain an estimate for the derivative of
f(t)
. This is a standard approach in numerical analysis, and multiple choices of nodes are possible. We use Chebyshev–Gauss nodes because they provide a stable interpolation rule with controlled derivative bias and controlled amplification of nodewise sampling noise. The following numerical guarantees are adapted from
(
74
, Appendix C)
, which contains a more detailed treatment of Chebyshev interpolation and also treats second-derivative estimation.

Fix an interpolation degree
r\geq 2
and a maximum evolution time
\tau_{\max}>0
. Let
T_{j}
denote the Chebyshev polynomial of the first kind, defined equivalently by

T_{j}(\cos\theta)=\cos(j\theta).

(45)

We use the
r+1
Chebyshev–Gauss nodes

z_{q}=-\cos\!\left(\frac{(2q-1)\pi}{2(r+1)}\right),\qquad q=1,\ldots,r+1,

(46)

which are the roots of
T_{r+1}
. Mapping the standard interval
[-1,1]
to the physical time interval
[0,\tau_{\max}]
, we set

t_{q}=\frac{\tau_{\max}}{2}(z_{q}+1),\qquad z(t)=\frac{2t}{\tau_{\max}}-1.

(47)

Given noisy estimates
\{\widehat{f}(t_{q})\}_{q=1}^{r+1}
, define the degree-
r
Chebyshev interpolant

\widehat{p}(t)=\sum_{j=0}^{r}\widehat{c}_{j}T_{j}(z(t)).

(48)

The coefficients are computed from the discrete orthogonality relations at the Chebyshev–Gauss nodes:

\widehat{c}_{0}=\frac{1}{r+1}\sum_{q=1}^{r+1}\widehat{f}(t_{q}),\qquad\widehat{c}_{j}=\frac{2}{r+1}\sum_{q=1}^{r+1}\widehat{f}(t_{q})T_{j}(z_{q}),\quad j=1,\ldots,r.

(49)

The first-derivative estimator is obtained by differentiating
\widehat{p}
at
t=0
:

\widehat{p}^{\,\prime}(0)=\frac{2}{\tau_{\max}}\sum_{j=1}^{r}(-1)^{j+1}j^{2}\widehat{c}_{j}.

(50)

Equivalently, substituting Eq. (
49
), the estimator is a fixed linear combination of the (noisy) function values:

\widehat{p}^{\,\prime}(0)=\sum_{q=1}^{r+1}\alpha_{q}\,\widehat{f}(t_{q}),\qquad\alpha_{q}=\frac{4}{\tau_{\max}(r+1)}\sum_{j=1}^{r}(-1)^{j+1}j^{2}T_{j}(z_{q}).

(51)

The weights
\{\alpha_{q}\}_{q=1}^{r+1}
depend only on
r
and
\tau_{\max}
, and can therefore be precomputed once and reused for every function sampled on the same grid.

Theorem B.10

(Noisy first-derivative Chebyshev estimator; Theorem C.1 in
74
)
.

Let
\tau_{\max}>0
, and let
f
be
(r+1)
-times continuously differentiable on
[0,\tau_{\max}]
. Suppose the available noisy samples
\{\widehat{f}(t_{q})\}_{q=1}^{r+1}
at the Chebyshev–Gauss nodes from Eq. (
47
) satisfy the nodewise noise bound

|\widehat{f}(t_{q})-f(t_{q})|\leq\epsilon_{s},\qquad q=1,\ldots,r+1.

(52)

Then the estimator
\widehat{p}^{\,\prime}(0)
defined in Eq. (
50
) satisfies

\bigl|\widehat{p}^{\,\prime}(0)-f^{\prime}(0)\bigr|\leq\underbrace{\frac{5r^{3}}{2\tau_{\max}}\,\epsilon_{s}}_{\text{noise term}}+\underbrace{2(r+1)^{2}\tau_{\max}^{r}\frac{\norm{f^{(r+1)}}_{\infty,[0,\tau_{\max}]}}{r!}}_{\text{bias term}},

(53)

where

\norm{f^{(r+1)}}_{\infty,[0,\tau_{\max}]}\coloneq\sup_{t\in[0,\tau_{\max}]}|f^{(r+1)}(t)|.

(54)

The theorem separates the error into two contributions: the noise term, which quantifies the cost of differentiating noisy node values, and the bias term, which is the deterministic interpolation error and is controlled by the
(r+1)
-st derivative of
f
on the sampled interval. The following corollary provides a convenient parameter choice when the derivatives of
f
grow at most exponentially with order.

Corollary B.11

(First derivative: sufficient
\tau_{\max}
,
r
, and
\epsilon_{s}
; Corollary C.3 in
74
)
.

Suppose that for some
B,\Lambda>0
, the derivatives of
f
obey

\norm{f^{(k)}}_{\infty,[0,1/(2\Lambda)]}\leq B\Lambda^{k},\qquad k\geq 1.

(55)

Fix a target accuracy
\epsilon>0
, and set

\tau_{\max}=\frac{1}{2\Lambda},\qquad r=\max\left\{2,\left\lceil\log_{2}\!\left(\frac{18B\Lambda}{\epsilon}\right)\right\rceil\right\},\qquad\epsilon_{s}=\frac{\epsilon}{10\Lambda r^{3}}.

(56)

Then the derivative estimator
\widehat{p}^{\,\prime}(0)
in Eq. (
50
) satisfies

\bigl|\widehat{p}^{\,\prime}(0)-f^{\prime}(0)\bigr|\leq\epsilon.

(57)

Finally, we note that the spacing between the Gauss–Chebyshev is not too small, which guarantees that the interpolation procedure does not require arbitrarily fine time control as the target accuracy increases.

Lemma B.12

(Time resolution of Chebyshev–Gauss nodes; Lemma C.6 in
74
)
.

Let
r\geq 1
, and let
\{t_{q}\}_{q=1}^{r+1}
be the sampling times defined in Eq. (
47
). Then

\min_{1\leq q\leq r+1}t_{q}\geq\frac{\tau_{\max}}{4(r+1)^{2}},\qquad\min_{1\leq q\leq r}|t_{q+1}-t_{q}|\geq\frac{2\tau_{\max}}{(r+1)^{2}}.

(58)

In particular, if
\tau_{\max}=\Theta(1/\Lambda)
and
r=\Theta(\log(\Lambda/\epsilon))
, then the smallest relevant time scale is

t_{\mathrm{res}}=\Omega\!\left(\frac{1}{\Lambda\log^{2}(\Lambda/\epsilon)}\right),

(59)

up to absolute constants.

Appendix C
QEC Lindbladian Reshaping

In this section, we introduce Lindbladian reshaping, the main subroutine behind our dissipator and Hamiltonian learning algorithms. Our scheme combines the quantum Zeno effect
98
;
14
;
26
;
109
with stabilizer quantum error correction (QEC)
148
;
40
;
78
to selectively suppress specified Lindblad terms. Under the appropriate detectability and correctability conditions, Lindbladian reshaping suppresses the targeted Lindblad terms and thereby enables longer effective evolution times. This primitive is instrumental to our Heisenberg-limited and standard-quantum-limited algorithms for learning Lindbladian dynamics in the setting of unknown structure.

We now summarize the notation used in this section. The
n
-qubit probe system has Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}_{n}\cong(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{\otimes n}
, and its dynamics is generated by a Lindbladian
\mathcal{L}:\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})\to\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n})
, which we write in the Pauli basis as

\mathcal{L}(\rho)=-i\sum_{P_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{H}}h_{k}\commutator{P_k}{\rho}+\sum_{P_{k},P_{j}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}}a_{kj}\left(P_{k}\rho P_{j}-\tfrac{1}{2}\anticommutator{P_j P_k}{\rho}\right).

(60)

To realize a stabilizer code, we append
n^{\prime}-n
ancilla qubits, thereby enlarging the Hilbert space to
\mathcal{H}_{n^{\prime}}\cong\mathcal{H}_{n}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{n^{\prime}-n}
. Let
\Pi
denote the projector onto the code space
\mathcal{H}_{c}\coloneq\Pi\mathcal{H}_{n^{\prime}}
. The associated corner algebra (defined in Eq. (
37
)) is

\mathfrak{A}_{c}\coloneq\Pi\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n^{\prime}})\Pi\cong\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{c}).

(61)

The corresponding corner projection is
\mathcal{C}_{\Pi}(X)\coloneq\Pi X\Pi
for any
X\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n^{\prime}})
. We also fix a completely positive and trace-preserving (CPTP) recovery channel
\mathcal{R}:\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n^{\prime}})\to\mathfrak{A}_{c}
which implements syndrome extraction followed by a corrective operation and returns states to the code space. A precise definition of
\mathcal{R}
is given in Appendix
C.1
.

To perform targeted QEC, we maintain the following two sets of Paulis:

V_{\mathrm{det}}\subset\mathcal{P}_{n},\qquad V_{\mathrm{corr}}\subset\mathcal{P}_{n}.

(62)

Operationally,
V_{\mathrm{det}}
keeps track of previously learned Hamiltonian Pauli terms, for which we enforce detectability, and
V_{\mathrm{corr}}
contains previously learned dissipator Pauli terms corresponding to nonzero diagonal Kossakowski matrix elements, for which we enforce correctability.

The sets
V_{\mathrm{det}}
and
V_{\mathrm{corr}}
consist of probe-system Paulis in
\mathcal{P}_{n}
. Whenever such a Pauli is used together with code-space objects on
\mathcal{H}_{n^{\prime}}
, we implicitly view it via the canonical embedding
P\mapsto P\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}
. In particular, we write
r(P)
as shorthand for
r(P\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})
, and
\Pi P\Pi
as shorthand for
\Pi(P\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})\Pi
.

Since the Lindbladian structure is not known a priori, we use randomly chosen quantum error-correcting codes. The codes are selected to suppress all previously learned terms via detectability and correctability conditions, while ensuring that the remaining unknown terms survive and induce nontrivial logical dynamics with non-negligible probability. We formally treat the random QEC construction in Appendix
C.3
.

In addition to QEC reshaping, we employ randomized Pauli twirling
46
, namely, we interleave the evolution with conjugation by randomly chosen Pauli operators drawn from a specified set. We use twirling in two forms: physical twirling over
n
-qubit Paulis acting on the probe subsystem, and partial logical twirling over a subset of logical Paulis.

Definition C.1

(Unitary conjugation and twirl of a superoperator)
.

For a unitary
U
, define the conjugation channel
\mathcal{U}_{U}(X):=UXU^{\dagger}
. Given a finite set
\mathcal{G}
of unitaries, define the uniform twirl of a linear map
\mathcal{M}:\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n^{\prime}})\to\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n^{\prime}})
by

\mathbb{T}_{\mathcal{G}}(\mathcal{M})\coloneq\frac{1}{|\mathcal{G}|}\sum_{U\in\mathcal{G}}\mathcal{U}_{U}\circ\mathcal{M}\circ\mathcal{U}_{U^{\dagger}}.

(63)

Definition C.2

(Partial logical twirl set)
.

Let
\overline{\mathcal{P}}
denote the logical Pauli group on the code space
\Pi\mathcal{H}_{n^{\prime}}
(for example,
N(S)/S
for a stabilizer code with stabilizer group
S
), and fix a nonidentity target logical Pauli
\overline{P}_{a}\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}
. Define the commuting logical subset

\overline{\mathcal{K}}_{\overline{P}_{a}}\coloneq\{\overline{Q}\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}:\ [\overline{Q},\overline{P}_{a}]=0\}.

(64)

For
\overline{Q}\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}
, let
\mathcal{U}_{\overline{Q}}
denote conjugation on the corner algebra
\mathfrak{A}_{c}
induced by any stabilizer coset representative of
\overline{Q}
.

By slight abuse of notation, throughout this section we sometimes write
e^{\mathcal{L}\tau}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}
and
\mathcal{L}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}
for the channel and generator acting as
e^{\mathcal{L}\tau}
and
\mathcal{L}
on the probe subsystem and trivially on the ancillas.

Having set up the notation, we now define the three single-step maps used, via repeated interleaving, to achieve Lindbladian reshaping. Note that the initial application of
\mathcal{C}_{\Pi}
is added to all of them for simplified notation and analysis. Since
\mathcal{C}_{\Pi}
acts as identity on
\mathfrak{A}_{c}
, once we get into the code-space, the physically realized reshaping maps can just omit this projection.

•

QEC reshaping map
(used in HDD Hamiltonian structure learning):

A_{\tau,H}^{I}\coloneq\mathcal{R}\circ\bigl(e^{\mathcal{L}\tau}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}\bigr)\circ\mathcal{C}_{\Pi}.

(65)

Repeated QEC is used to suppress most of the dissipator (specified via
V_{\mathrm{corr}}
) and some Hamiltonian (specified via
V_{\mathrm{det}}
) terms after they have been learned.

•

Physical twirling + partial QEC reshaping
(used in dissipator structure learning
\mathcal{A}^{I}_{D}
):

A_{\tau,D}\coloneq\mathcal{R}\circ\frac{1}{4^{n}}\sum_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}\bigl(\mathcal{U}_{P}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}\bigr)\circ\bigl(e^{\mathcal{L}\tau}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}\bigr)\circ\bigl(\mathcal{U}_{P}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}\bigr)\circ\mathcal{C}_{\Pi}.

(66)

This map is used in a setting where Hamiltonian is fully unknown and the dissipator is partially known. Physical Pauli twirling suppresses Hamiltonian contributions and off-diagonal Kossakowski terms, while QEC reshaping suppresses the previously learned diagonal dissipator terms contained in
V_{\mathrm{corr}}
.

•

QEC reshaping + partial logical twirling
(used in HDD Hamiltonian coefficient learning):

A_{\tau,H}^{II}\coloneq\frac{1}{|\overline{\mathcal{K}}_{\overline{P}_{a}}|}\sum_{\overline{Q}\in\overline{\mathcal{K}}_{\overline{P}_{a}}}\Bigl(\mathcal{U}_{\overline{Q}}\circ\mathcal{R}\circ\bigl(e^{\mathcal{L}\tau}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}\bigr)\circ\mathcal{U}_{\overline{Q}}\Bigr)\circ\mathcal{C}_{\Pi}.

(67)

Here QEC reshaping suppresses the previously learned part of the dissipator, while the partial logical twirl isolates a specified surviving logical Hamiltonian term
\overline{P}_{a}\in\mathfrak{A}_{c}
.

This section proceeds as follows:

•

QEC recovery channel.
In Appendix
C.1
, we summarize a standard quantum error correction recovery channel
\mathcal{R}:\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n^{\prime}})\to\mathfrak{A}_{c}
that implements syndrome measurement, decoding, and correction.

•

Effective generators.
In Appendix
C.2
, we derive the effective generators corresponding to the three reshaping maps Eq. (
65
), Eq. (
66
), and Eq. (
67
) under suitable detectability and correctability conditions.

•

Random QEC construction.
In Appendix
C.3
, we present the random stabilizer code construction tailored for Lindbladian reshaping. This construction ensures that the previously learned terms are suppressed, while the unknown terms survive as distinct nontrivial logical operators with non-negligible probability.

•

Effective evolution under QEC reshaping.
In Appendix
C.4
, we compare the evolution under repeated applications of the one-step maps from Eq. (
65
), Eq. (
66
), and Eq. (
67
) with the desired effective evolution
e^{t\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}}
and quantify the resulting diamond-norm errors.

•

Deferred proofs.

In Apppendix
C.5
, we provide the deferred proofs related to the effective generators of QEC reshaping.

C.1
QEC recovery channel (decoding + correction)

We assume a stabilizer code
C
that is non-degenerate with respect to the relevant collection of Pauli errors. We construct an explicit completely positive and trace-preserving (CPTP) recovery channel
\mathcal{R}
implementing syndrome measurement, decoding, and correction via a syndrome lookup table. The construction follows the standard recovery map for non-degenerate stabilizer codes
102
, except that we allow certain Pauli errors to be treated in a
detection-only
mode.

Concretely, let
G=\{G_{\ell}\}_{\ell=1}^{m}
be a generating set of an abelian stabilizer
group
S
of code
C
, and let
\{\Pi_{s}\}_{s\in\{0,1\}^{m}}
be the associated syndrome projectors in
\mathcal{H}_{n^{\prime}}
from
Eq. (
41
). Let
\Pi\coloneq\Pi_{0}
. Assume
non-degeneracy
on
V_{\mathrm{corr}}
:
all
P\in V_{\mathrm{corr}}
have mutually distinguishable nonzero syndromes
r(P)\in\{0,1\}^{m}
. Additionally, assume
detectability
on
V_{\mathrm{det}}
: each
P\in V_{\mathrm{det}}
has nonzero syndrome
r(P)\in\{0,1\}^{m}
.

Pauli–KL relations on
V_{\mathrm{corr}}
.

Under the above assumption, one can check that the Knill–Laflamme equalities
hold for the target set of Paulis (
82
;
61
;
102
):
for all
P_{k},P_{j}\in V_{\mathrm{corr}}
,

\Pi(P_{k})\Pi=0,\qquad\Pi(P_{k}P_{j})\Pi=\delta_{kj}\,\Pi.

(68)

The first equation follows from the nonzero-syndrome assumption of terms in
V_{\mathrm{corr}}
. The second equation follows from the requirement of non-degeneracy on
V_{\mathrm{corr}}
(see
Section

C.2
). We only invoke Eq. (
68
) as a sanity check: the core of our analysis is that with frequent correction rounds, the
stroboscopic evolution
\mathcal{A}_{\tau}
(referring to any of Eq. (
65
), Eq. (
66
) and Eq. (
67
))
removes all specified Pauli terms to first order
and approximates
e^{t\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}}
with an explicit norm error bound as shown in
Section

C.4
.

We now construct the recovery channel. For each possible error syndrome

s\in\{0,1\}^{m}
, choose a Hermitian Pauli correction

R_{s}\in\mathcal{P}_{n^{\prime}}
as follows:

R_{r(P\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})}\coloneqq P\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}\quad(P\in V_{\mathrm{corr}}),\qquad R_{0}\coloneqq I_{n^{\prime}}.

(69)

For every remaining syndrome
s\notin r(V_{\mathrm{corr}}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})\cup\{0\}
,
choose any Hermitian Pauli
R_{s}\in\mathcal{P}_{n^{\prime}}
with syndrome
r(R_{s})=s
.
Whenever such a choice exists with support only on the probe subsystem, we take

R_{s}=\widetilde{R}_{s}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}
for some Hermitian
\widetilde{R}_{s}\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
. For syndromes
for which no probe-supported representative exists, the correction may act on the
ancilla qubits.

Given a density operator
\rho
and a realized syndrome
s
, the corrected
state is

\rho_{\mathrm{out}\mid s}\;=\;R_{s}\,\frac{\Pi_{s}\rho\Pi_{s}}{\tr(\Pi_s\rho)}\,R_{s}.

Averaging over all outcomes (the operational channel actually implemented)
yields

\sum_{s}\tr(\Pi_s\rho)\,\rho_{\mathrm{out}\mid s}\;=\;\sum_{s}R_{s}\Pi_{s}\rho\Pi_{s}R_{s}.

Definition C.3

(Recovery channel)
.

With the recovery operators from Eq. (
69
) and with the corresponding Kraus operators
K_{s}\coloneq R_{s}\Pi_{s}
, define the recovery channel
\mathcal{R}:\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n^{\prime}})\to\mathfrak{A}_{c}
:

\mathcal{R}(\rho)\coloneq\sum_{s\in\{0,1\}^{m}}K_{s}\,\rho\,K_{s}^{\dagger}\;=\;\sum_{s}R_{s}\Pi_{s}\rho\Pi_{s}R_{s}.

Remark C.4

(Well-definedness, range, and identity on the code space)
.

The recovery channel
\mathcal{R}
is completely positive and trace preserving (CPTP) and has range in the corner algebra
\mathfrak{A}_{c}
(Eq. (
61
)). Moreover,
\mathcal{R}
acts as the identity on
\mathfrak{A}_{c}
, i.e.,
\mathcal{R}(X)=X
for all
X\in\mathfrak{A}_{c}
.

C.2
Effective generators of QEC reshaping

In this section, we derive the effective generators corresponding to the three reshaping maps Eq. (
65
), Eq. (
66
), and Eq. (
67
). First, consider the QEC reshaping map defined in Eq. (
65
):

A_{\tau,H}^{I}\coloneq\mathcal{R}\circ(e^{\mathcal{L}\tau}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})\circ\mathcal{C}_{\Pi}=\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{C}_{\Pi}+\tau\underbrace{\mathcal{R}\circ(\mathcal{L}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})}_{\to\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}}\circ\,\mathcal{C}_{\Pi}+\mathcal{O}(\tau^{2}).

In
Section

C.2
, we derive the effective generator of this map under the detectability and correctability assumptions on
V_{\mathrm{det}}
and
V_{\mathrm{corr}}
. Intuitively, when
V_{\mathrm{corr}}
contains most of the dissipator, this approximately reduces the problem to logical Hamiltonian learning. Hence, this map is used for the Hamiltonian-Disjoint-from-Dissipator structure learning in Appendix
E.2
.

Lemma C.5

(Effective generator under QEC reshaping)
.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be an
n
-qubit GKSL generator as in Eq. (
60
), and let
\mathcal{L}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}
denote its trivial extension to the ancilla register. Let
C
be a stabilizer code on
n^{\prime}\geq n
qubits with code projector
\Pi
, corner algebra
\mathfrak{A}_{c}\coloneq\Pi\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n^{\prime}})\Pi
and syndrome map
r(\cdot)
(as in Eq. (
40
)). Suppose
V_{\mathrm{det}}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{n}
and
V_{\mathrm{corr}}\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{D}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{n}
are specified sets, and that all Paulis in
V_{\mathrm{det}}
have non-zero syndromes. Assume furthermore that all elements of
V_{\mathrm{corr}}
, have pairwise distinct nonzero syndromes. Let
\mathcal{R}:\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n^{\prime}})\to\mathfrak{A}_{c}
be the associated recovery map from
Section

C.1
based on the specified sets
V_{\mathrm{det}}
and
V_{\mathrm{corr}}
.

Then, for any
X\in\mathfrak{A}_{c}
, the effective generator of the QEC reshaping map from Eq. (
65
) is

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}(X)\coloneq\mathcal{R}\circ(\mathcal{L}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})(X)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P_{k},P_{j}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}\setminus V_{\mathrm{corr}},\\
r(P_{k})=r(P_{j})\end{subarray}}a_{kj}\Bigl((Q_{P_{k}}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})X(Q_{P_{j}}^{\dagger}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})-\frac{1}{2}\{Q_{P_{j}}^{\dagger}Q_{P_{k}}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n},X\}\Bigr)

(70)

\displaystyle-i\underbrace{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{H},\\
r(P_{k})=0\end{subarray}}h_{k}[P_{k}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n},X]}_{[H_{\mathrm{tar}},X]}\,-\,i\underbrace{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P_{k}\in V_{\mathrm{corr}},\\
P_{j}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}\setminus V_{\mathrm{corr}},\\
r(P_{k})=r(P_{j})\end{subarray}}\left({\operatorname{Re}(a_{kj})\left[\frac{i[P_{k},P_{j}]}{2}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}\,,\,X\right]+\operatorname{Im}(a_{kj})\left[\frac{\{P_{k},P_{j}\}}{2}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}\,,\,X\right]}\right)}_{[H_{\mathrm{bias}},X]}

where
Q_{P}\coloneqq\widetilde{R}_{r(P)}P\in\mathbb{P}_{n}
are Pauli operators composed of
P
and the corresponding recovery actions
\widetilde{R}_{r(P)}
as specified in
Eq. (
69
) with
R_{r(P)}=\widetilde{R}_{r(P)}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}
. Notably, the resulting Hamiltonian part of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}
contains only zero-syndrome Paulis. In particular, no Pauli term from

V_{\mathrm{det}}\cup V_{\mathrm{corr}}\cup\{\operatorname{Herm}(PP^{\prime}):P,P^{\prime}\in V_{\mathrm{corr}},\ P\neq P^{\prime}\}

is present in the Pauli decompositions of
H_{\mathrm{tar}}
and
H_{\mathrm{bias}}
.

Proof.

See Apppendix
C.5
.
∎

Corollary C.6

(Effective QEC-reshaped generator under distinct dissipator syndromes)
.

Assuming additionally that all Paulis
P\in\mathcal{S}_{D}
have pairwise distinct nonzero syndromes, one can significantly simplify the
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}
from Eq. (
70
) to:

\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}(X)=\sum_{P_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}\setminus V_{\mathrm{corr}}}a_{kk}\Bigl((Q_{P_{k}}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})X(Q_{P_{k}}^{\dagger}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})-X\Bigr)-i\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{H},\\
r(P_{k})=0\end{subarray}}h_{k}[P_{k}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n},X]

(71)

where the resulting Hamiltonian part contains no Paulis from

V_{\mathrm{det}}\cup\mathcal{S}_{D}\cup\{\operatorname{Herm}(PP^{\prime}):P,P^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}_{D},\ P\neq P^{\prime}\}.

The second relevant map is the physical twirling combined with partial QEC reshaping (Eq. (
66
)):

\displaystyle A_{\tau,D}\coloneq

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}\circ\frac{1}{4^{n}}\sum_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}(\mathcal{U}_{P}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})\circ(e^{\mathcal{L}\tau}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})\circ(\mathcal{U}_{P}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})\circ\mathcal{C}_{\Pi}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{C}_{\Pi}+\tau\underbrace{\mathcal{R}\circ\frac{1}{4^{n}}\sum_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}(\mathcal{U}_{P}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})\circ(\mathcal{L}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})\circ(\mathcal{U}_{P}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})}_{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}}\circ\,\mathcal{C}_{\Pi}+\mathcal{O}(\tau^{2}).

The effective generator of this map is presented in
Section

C.2
. This map is used for dissipator structure learning in Appendix
D
.

Lemma C.7

(Effective generator of Physical twirling + QEC reshaping)
.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be an
n
-qubit GKSL generator as in Eq. (
60
), and let
\mathcal{L}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}
denote its trivial extension to the ancilla register. Let
C
be a stabilizer code on
n^{\prime}\geq n
qubits with code projector
\Pi
and corner algebra
\mathfrak{A}_{c}=\Pi\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n^{\prime}})\Pi
. Assume that the target
V_{\mathrm{det}}=\emptyset
and that
V_{\mathrm{corr}}
is a subset of the dissipator structure, i.e.
V_{\mathrm{corr}}\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{D}
. Suppose all Paulis in
V_{\mathrm{corr}}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{n}
have nonzero mutually distinguishable syndromes. Let
\mathcal{R}:\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n^{\prime}})\to\mathfrak{A}_{c}
be the associated recovery map from
Section

C.1
.

Then for any
X\in\mathfrak{A}_{c}
, the effective generator of the physical twirling + QEC reshaping map is

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}(X)\coloneq

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}\circ\frac{1}{4^{n}}\sum_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}\bigl(\mathcal{U}_{P}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}\bigr)\circ\bigl(\mathcal{L}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}\bigr)\circ\bigl(\mathcal{U}_{P}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})(X)=

(72)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{P_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}\setminus V_{\mathrm{corr}}}a_{kk}\Bigl((Q_{P_{k}}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})X(Q_{P_{k}}^{\dagger}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})-X\Bigr)

where
Q_{P}\coloneqq\widetilde{R}_{r(P)}P\in\mathbb{P}_{n}
are Pauli operators composed of
P
and the corresponding recovery actions
\widetilde{R}_{r(P)}
as specified in
Eq. (
69
) with
R_{r(P)}=\widetilde{R}_{r(P)}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}
. The resulting
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}
is purely dissipative and excludes all terms corresponding to
P_{k}\in V_{\mathrm{corr}}
.

Proof.

First, the standard Pauli twirling
46
(which we call physical twirling) reshapes the generator by removing its Hamiltonian part as well as off-diagonal Kossakowski elements

\frac{1}{4^{n}}\sum_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}\bigl(\mathcal{U}_{P}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}\bigr)\circ\bigl(\mathcal{L}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}\bigr)\circ\bigl(\mathcal{U}_{P}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})(X)=\sum_{P_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}}a_{kk}\Bigl((P_{k}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})X(P_{k}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})-X\Bigr)

Acting on
X\in\mathfrak{A}_{c}
and composing with the recovery channel
\mathcal{R}
, similarly to Eq. (
89
) we get:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}(X)

\displaystyle=\sum_{P_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}}a_{kk}\Bigl(R_{r(P_{k})}(P_{k}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})X(P_{k}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})R_{r(P_{k})}-X\Bigr)=

(73)

\displaystyle=\sum_{P_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}\setminus V_{\mathrm{corr}}}a_{kk}\Bigl((Q_{P_{k}}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})X(Q_{P_{k}}^{\dagger}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})-X\Bigr)

where the second equality follows directly from the fact that Paulis from
V_{\mathrm{corr}}
are correctable by design of
\mathcal{R}
.

Finally, since
r(PQ)=r(P)\oplus r(Q)
, the reshaped operators
Q_{P_{k}}\coloneq R_{r(P_{k})}P_{k}
all have
r(Q_{P_{k}})=0
by construction of
\mathcal{R}
in
Section

C.1
. Since elements of
V_{\mathrm{corr}}
have non-zero syndromes, they are absent from the expansion of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}
for inputs
X\in\mathfrak{A}_{c}
.
∎

The third relevant map combines QEC reshaping with partial logical twirling (Eq. (
67
)). In Appendix
E.3
, this map is used to approximately isolate evolution under a single logical Pauli, thereby reducing the problem to single-parameter metrology. Assume that the target Pauli
P_{a}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}=\overline{P}_{a}
is a nontrivial logical operator for the chosen code, and take any
Y\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n^{\prime}})
. Using the partial logical twirling set
\overline{\mathcal{K}}_{\overline{P}_{a}}
defined in Eq. (
64
), we have

\displaystyle A_{\tau,H}^{II}(Y)\coloneq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\overline{\mathcal{K}}_{\overline{P}_{a}}|}\sum_{\overline{Q}\in\overline{\mathcal{K}}_{\overline{P}_{a}}}\Bigl(\mathcal{U}_{\overline{Q}}\circ\mathcal{R}\circ(e^{\mathcal{L}\tau}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})\circ\mathcal{U}_{\overline{Q}}\circ\mathcal{C}_{\Pi}\Bigr)(Y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{\Pi}(Y)+\tau\underbrace{\frac{1}{|\overline{\mathcal{K}}_{\overline{P}_{a}}|}\sum_{\overline{Q}\in\overline{\mathcal{K}}_{\overline{P}_{a}}}\mathcal{U}_{\overline{Q}}\circ\mathcal{R}\circ(\mathcal{L}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})\circ\mathcal{U}_{\overline{Q}}}_{\to\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}}\circ\,\mathcal{C}_{\Pi}(Y)+\mathcal{O}(\tau^{2}).

The effective generator of the “QEC reshaping combined with partial logical twirling” map is presented in
Section

C.2
.

Lemma C.8

(Effective generator of QEC reshaping + partial logical twirl)
.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be an
n
-qubit GKSL generator as in Eq. (
60
), and let
\mathcal{L}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}
denote its trivial extension to the ancilla register. Let
C
be a stabilizer code on
n^{\prime}\geq n
qubits with code projector
\Pi
and corner algebra
\mathfrak{A}_{c}\coloneq\Pi\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n^{\prime}})\Pi
. Let
\overline{P}_{a}\coloneq P_{a}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}
be the nontrivial logical Pauli to be isolated. Suppose
V_{\mathrm{corr}}\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{D}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{n}
is a specified set and let
\mathcal{R}:\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n^{\prime}})\to\mathfrak{A}_{c}
be the associated recovery map from
Section

C.1
based on
V_{\mathrm{corr}}
. Assume furthermore that all elements of
V_{\mathrm{corr}}
have pairwise distinct nonzero syndromes. Assume also that
P_{a}
is the unique
n
-qubit Pauli whose restriction to the code space contributes to the logical Pauli
\overline{P}_{a}
. Equivalently, for every
P_{k}\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
with
P_{k}\neq P_{a}
, the compressed operator
\Pi(P_{k}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})\Pi
has zero component along
\Pi\overline{P}_{a}\Pi
. Assume moreover that no nontrivial product of zero-syndrome Pauli operators appearing in the QEC-reshaped generator of
\mathcal{L}
from
Section

C.2
is proportional to a stabilizer.

Then for any
X\in\mathfrak{A}_{c}
, the effective generator of QEC reshaping + the partial logical twirl over
\overline{\mathcal{K}}_{\overline{P}_{a}}
is

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}(

\displaystyle X)\coloneq\frac{1}{|\overline{\mathcal{K}}_{\overline{P}_{a}}|}\sum_{\overline{Q}\in\overline{\mathcal{K}}_{\overline{P}_{a}}}\mathcal{U}_{\overline{Q}}\circ\mathcal{R}\circ(\mathcal{L}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})\circ\mathcal{U}_{\overline{Q}}(X)=

(74)

\displaystyle=-ih_{a}[\overline{P}_{a},X]\,-\,i\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P_{k}\in V_{\mathrm{corr}},\\
P_{j}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}\setminus V_{\mathrm{corr}},\\
P_{k}P_{j}\propto P_{a}\end{subarray}}\left({\operatorname{Re}(a_{kj})\left[\frac{i[P_{k},P_{j}]}{2}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}\,,\,X\right]+\operatorname{Im}(a_{kj})\left[\frac{\{P_{k},P_{j}\}}{2}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}\,,\,X\right]}\right)+

\displaystyle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}\setminus V_{\mathrm{corr}},\\
\operatorname{Herm}(P_{k}P_{a})\in\mathcal{S}_{D}\setminus V_{\mathrm{corr}};\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P_{j}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}\setminus V_{\mathrm{corr}},\\
\operatorname{Herm}(P_{k}P_{j})\in\{I_{n},P_{a}\}\end{subarray}}a_{kj}\Bigl((Q_{P_{k}}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})X(Q_{P_{j}}^{\dagger}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})-\frac{1}{2}\{Q_{P_{j}}^{\dagger}Q_{P_{k}}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n},X\}\Bigr)

\displaystyle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}\setminus V_{\mathrm{corr}},\\
\operatorname{Herm}(P_{k}P_{a})\notin\mathcal{S}_{D}\setminus V_{\mathrm{corr}}\end{subarray}}a_{kk}\Bigl((Q_{P_{k}}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})X(Q_{P_{k}}^{\dagger}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})-X\Bigr).

where
Q_{P}\coloneqq\widetilde{R}_{r(P)}P\in\mathbb{P}_{n}
are Pauli operators composed of
P
and the corresponding recovery actions
\widetilde{R}_{r(P)}
as specified in
Eq. (
69
) with
R_{r(P)}=\widetilde{R}_{r(P)}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}
. Here
h_{a}
is understood to be zero if
P_{a}\notin\mathcal{S}_{H}
.

Proof.

See Apppendix
C.5
.
∎

Corollary C.9

(Effective generator of QEC reshaping + partial logical twirl under distinct dissipator syndromes)
.

Assuming additionally that all Paulis
P\in\mathcal{S}_{D}
have pairwise distinct nonzero syndromes, one can significantly simplify the
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}
from Eq. (
74
) to:

\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}(X)=-ih_{a}[\overline{P}_{a},X]+\sum_{P_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}\setminus V_{\mathrm{corr}}}a_{kk}\Bigl((Q_{P_{k}}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})X(Q_{P_{k}}^{\dagger}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})-X\Bigr)

(75)

Remark C.10

(Full-space realization of the effective generators)
.

Although
Sections

C.2
,
C.2
and
C.2
derive
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}
only for inputs
X\in\mathfrak{A}_{c}
, in each case we may use the same explicit expression to define a linear map on the full operator algebra
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n^{\prime}})
. In all three cases, the resulting full-space map has the form of a valid GKSL generator on
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n^{\prime}})
, and we continue to denote it by
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}
. Therefore, for every
t\geq 0
, the semigroup
e^{t\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}}
is CPTP on
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n^{\prime}})
, and thus

\|e^{t\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}}\|_{\diamond}=1.

Moreover, by construction, each
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}
inherits its coefficients from the original Lindbladian Eq. (
60
) and contains no more Pauli terms than
\mathcal{L}
. In particular, if
\mathcal{L}
is
M
-sparse, then so is
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}
. Hence, by
Section

B.3
,

\|\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}\|_{\diamond}\leq 2M.

C.3
Random stabilizer code construction

Within our learning framework, the reshaping maps Eq. (
65
), Eq. (
66
), and Eq. (
67
) are used to selectively suppress previously learned terms. To make this useful algorithmically, we must also ensure that terms not yet learned survive in the effective dynamics with non-negligible probability over the random code draw. A second, independent issue is
injectivity of logical action
: two Pauli strings that commute with all stabilizers, hence are undetectable, but differ by a stabilizer element act as the same logical operator on the code space. If such terms, for example, enter the Hamiltonian with opposite signs, they can cancel in
H_{\mathrm{eff}}
and hide the signal we wish to learn.

In this section, we present a random stabilizer code construction that addresses these issues while satisfying the detectability and correctability assumptions used in Appendix
C.2
. Recall from Eq. (
62
) that
V_{\mathrm{corr}}\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{D}
keeps track of the learned dissipator terms and
V_{\mathrm{det}}\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{H}
keeps track of the learned Hamiltonian terms. We work with the associated target suppression set

\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{tar}}\coloneq V_{\mathrm{det}}\cup V_{\mathrm{corr}}\cup\{\operatorname{Herm}(P^{\prime}P^{\prime\prime}):P^{\prime},P^{\prime\prime}\in V_{\mathrm{corr}},\ P^{\prime}\neq P^{\prime\prime}\},

(76)

For a Pauli
P\in\mathcal{P}_{n}\setminus\{I\}
, let
A_{P}
denote the event that
P
survives reshaping, namely that it has zero syndrome under the sampled checks. Using the syndrome map
r()
from Eq. (
40
), we also define the target suppression event

E_{\mathrm{tar}}\coloneq\{r(Q)\neq 0\text{ for all }Q\in\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{tar}}\},

(77)

The random-code construction will be used to guarantee the following:

•

Targeted detectability/correctability.
Every Pauli in
V_{\mathrm{det}}
is detectable and all Paulis in
V_{\mathrm{corr}}
have nonzero mutually distinguishable syndromes. This is equivalent to event
E_{\mathrm{tar}}
since
r(P^{\prime}P^{\prime\prime})=r(P^{\prime})\oplus r(P^{\prime\prime})
.

•

Survival probability.
Any Pauli operator
P
outside the target set
\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{tar}}
survives reshaping with non-negligible probability over the random code draw.

•

Injective logical action.
Distinct
n
-qubit probe Pauli terms that survive reshaping induce distinct logical Paulis on the code space, so no two survivors are equal modulo the stabilizer.

These guarantees are recorded formally in
Theorem

C.14
.

Let us proceed to the code construction. As a starting point in
Algorithm

1
, we will draw a set of random
n
-qubit Pauli strings
G=\{G_{1},\dots,G_{m}\}
that don’t necessarily commute. To abelianize them and hence form a valid stabilizer code, one may append ancillas and use the standard
X/Z
-padding
25
;
139
;
this preserves commutation/anticommutation on the data:

\operatorname{sgn}(\tilde{G}_{i},P\otimes I_{\rm anc})=\operatorname{sgn}(G_{i},P)
, where
\operatorname{sgn}()
denotes a commutation sign from Eq. (
31
). Thus, we import the following lemma:

Lemma C.11

(ZX padding to enforce commutation
25
;
139
)
.

Let
G=\{G_{1},\ldots,G_{m}\}\subset\mathcal{P}_{n}
be
m
Hermitian Pauli checks (not necessarily commuting).
There exists an integer
c\leq\lfloor m/2\rfloor
, a register of
c
ancillary qubits, and Hermitian Pauli
operators
A_{1},\ldots,A_{m}\in\mathcal{P}_{c}
acting on the ancillas such that the padded checks

\widetilde{G}_{k}\;:=\;G_{k}\otimes A_{k}

pairwise commute:
[\widetilde{G}_{i},\widetilde{G}_{j}]=0
for all
i,j
.

Proof sketch.

Even though the elements of
G
do not necessarily commute, we may represent them in the binary symplectic
formalism (Appendix
B.4.4
). Let
H\in\{0,1\}^{m\times 2n}
be the check matrix whose
k
th row is
the binary vector of
G_{k}
. Then the syndrome map
r(P)=H\Lambda v_{P}
(Eq. (
44
)) encodes the
commutation pattern of any
P\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
with
G
, and the mutual commutation of the checks is encoded by

\Omega_{H}\coloneq H\Lambda H^{T}
.

By symplectic Gram–Schmidt orthogonalization (SGSOP)
139
, there exists an
invertible
m\times m
matrix
Y
that encodes symplectic row operations such that
H^{\prime}=YH
satisfies

\Omega_{H^{\prime}}\;=\;H^{\prime}\Lambda H^{\prime T}\;=\;Y\Omega_{H}Y^{T}\;=\;\bigoplus_{i=1}^{c}\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\[2.0pt]
1&0\end{pmatrix}\;\oplus\;\bigoplus_{j=1}^{\,m-2c}[0],

(78)

where
[0]
denotes the zero-matrix. Thus the transformed check set can be written as
\{Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{m}\}
where
(Q_{2i-1},Q_{2i})
form
c

disjoint anticommuting pairs,
\{Q_{2i-1},Q_{2i}\}=0
, and all other pairs commute. By Eq. (
78
),

c=\tfrac{1}{2}\mathrm{rank}(\Omega_{H})\leq\lfloor m/2\rfloor
. Introduce
c
ancillas
a_{1},\ldots,a_{c}
and pad each
anticommuting pair by

\widetilde{Q}_{2i-1}\coloneq Q_{2i-1}\otimes Z_{a_{i}},\qquad\widetilde{Q}_{2i}\coloneq Q_{2i}\otimes X_{a_{i}},

while padding all remaining
Q_{j}
by identity on the ancillas; the resulting
\{\widetilde{Q}_{j}\}
commute.
Finally, applying the inverse row transformation
Y^{-1}
to
\{\widetilde{Q}_{j}\}
(i.e., multiplying and reordering generators
according to
Y^{-1}
) yields a commuting padded generating set
\widetilde{G}
corresponding to the original
list
G
. For details, see
139
.
∎

In Lemmas (
C.3
)-(
C.3
), we analyze the commutation properties (and thus detectability/correctabilitity) of randomly chosen Pauli strings.

Lemma C.12

(Probability of nonzero syndromes on a finite Pauli set.)
.

Let
\mathcal{W}\subset\mathcal{P}_{n}\setminus\{I\}
be a target set and let

\mathcal{T}=\{P^{(t)}\}_{t=1}^{m}
be a multiset of
m
Paulis sampled i.i.d. uniformly (with replacement) from
\mathcal{P}_{n}
. Let
E
denote the event that each
Q\in\mathcal{W}
anti-commutes with at least one element of
\mathcal{T}
. Then

\Pr_{\mathcal{T}}(E)\geq 1-|\mathcal{W}|\,2^{-m}.

(79)

Proof.

For each
\widetilde{Q}\in\mathcal{W}
, let
E_{\widetilde{Q}}
denote the event that all
m
checks in
\mathcal{T}
commute with
\widetilde{Q}
. For a fixed nonidentity Pauli
\widetilde{Q}
and a single uniformly random check
P^{(t)}
, we have
\Pr(\{\widetilde Q,P^{(t)}\}=0)=\tfrac{1}{2}
. Since the
m
draws are independent,

\Pr(E_{\widetilde Q})=2^{-m}\qquad\text{for all }\widetilde{Q}\in\mathcal{W}.

(80)

By the union bound,

\Pr_{\mathcal{T}}(E^{c})=\Pr_{\mathcal{T}}\!\Bigl[\exists\,\widetilde{Q}\in\mathcal{W}:\widetilde{Q}\text{ commutes with all checks in }\mathcal{T}\Bigr]\leq|\mathcal{W}|\,2^{-m}.

(81)

Hence

\Pr_{\mathcal{T}}(E)\geq 1-|\mathcal{W}|\,2^{-m}.

(82)

proving the claim.
∎

Lemma C.13

(Probability of survival of non-target set member)
.

Let
\mathcal{W}\subset\mathcal{P}_{n}\setminus\{I\}
and let

\mathcal{T}=\{P^{(t)}\}_{t=1}^{m}
be a multiset of
m
Paulis sampled i.i.d. uniformly (with replacement) from
\mathcal{P}_{n}
. Let
E
be the event that each
Q\in\mathcal{W}
anti-commutes with at least one element of
\mathcal{T}
. Let
A_{P}
be the event that a given non-identity Pauli string
P\notin\mathcal{W}
commutes with all elements in
\mathcal{T}
. Then conditioned on
E
, the probability that
P
is undetectable by checks in
\mathcal{T}
obeys:

\frac{2^{-m}}{(1-\absolutevalue{\mathcal W}2^{-m})}\geq\Pr_{\mathcal{T}}\Bigl[\,A_{P}\,\Big|\,E\,\Bigr]\ \geq\ 2^{-m}(1-|\mathcal{W}|2^{-m})

where the upper bound is only meaningful when
|\mathcal{W}|2^{-m}<1
.

Proof.

Using Bayes’ theorem,

\Pr(\,A_P\, | \,E\,)=\frac{\Pr(\,E\, | A_P)\Pr(A_P)}{\Pr(E)}.

Therefore,

\frac{\Pr(A_P)}{\Pr(E)}\geq\Pr(\,A_P\, | \,E\,)\geq\Pr(\,E\, | A_P)\Pr(A_P)

(83)

For a uniformly random sample
P^{(t)}
, the probability
\Pr([P, P^{(t)}] = 0)=\frac{1}{2}
. Since the
m
samples of
\mathcal{T}
are independent:

\Pr(A_P)=2^{-m}

Combining this with
\Pr(E)\geq 1-|\mathcal{W}|2^{-m}
(from
Section

C.3
), we obtain:

\frac{2^{-m}}{(1-\absolutevalue{\mathcal W}2^{-m})}\geq\frac{\Pr(A_P)}{\Pr(E)}\geq\Pr(\,A_P\, | \,E\,)

It remains to lower bound
\Pr(\,A_P\, | \,E\,)
. To do that, we use the lower bound on
\Pr(\,E\, | A_P)
. Fix
Q\in\mathcal{W}
. Since
Q\neq P
, using
Section

B.4.4
,

\Pr_{T\sim\mathcal{P}_{n}}([Q,T]=0\mid[P,T]=0)=\frac{\text{\# of Paulis that commute w both $Q,P$}}{\text{\# of Paulis that commute w $P$}}=\frac{2^{2n-2}}{2^{2n-1}}=\frac{1}{2}

By independence of the
m
draws,

\Pr\bigl(\,[Q,T]=0\ \forall T\in\mathcal{T}\,\big|\,[P,T]=0\ \forall T\in\mathcal{T}\,\bigr)\;=\;2^{-m}.

A union bound over
|\mathcal{W}|
gives

\Pr(E^c\mid A_P)\ \leq\ |\mathcal{W}|\,2^{-m}\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad\Pr(E\mid A_P)\ \geq\ 1-|\mathcal{W}|\,2^{-m}.

Plugging into Eq. (
83
) with
\Pr(A_P)=2^{-m}
concludes the proof:

\Pr(\,A_P\,|\,E\,)\;\geq\;\Pr(E\mid A_P)\Pr(A_P)\;\geq\;\bigl(1-|\mathcal{W}|\,2^{-m}\bigr)\,2^{-m},

∎

Inputs :

[label=(0), leftmargin=*, nosep]

1.

Target detectable set
V_{\mathrm{det}}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{n}\setminus\{I\}
. We require that each term in
V_{\mathrm{det}}
has a non-zero syndrome.

2.

Target correctable set
V_{\mathrm{corr}}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{n}\setminus\{I\}
. We require that all terms in
V_{\mathrm{corr}}
have mutually distinguishable non-zero syndromes.

3.

Detectability-size bound
M_{\mathrm{det}}\geq|\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{tar}}|
(Eq. (
76
)). This upper bound bounds the number of Pauli terms that we want to have non-zero syndromes under the code with non-negligible probability. For example, for dissipator structure learning one may take
M_{\mathrm{det}}=|\mathcal{S}_{D}|^{2}
, while for Hamiltonian learning one may take
M_{\mathrm{det}}=M
.

4.

Failure probability
\delta\in(0,1)
.

5.

Arbitrary positive constant
c>1
.

6.

Access to ancilla qubits.

Output :
Commuting stabilizer checks
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}=\{\widetilde{G}_{\ell}\}_{\ell=1}^{m}
and a recovery channel
\mathcal{R}
. Upon return, the code satisfies
E_{\mathrm{tar}}
(
77
), and distinct zero-syndrome probe Paulis induce distinct logical Paulis on the code space.

Schedule

\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{tar}}\leftarrow V_{\mathrm{det}}\cup V_{\mathrm{corr}}\cup\{\operatorname{Herm}(P^{\prime}P^{\prime\prime}):P^{\prime},P^{\prime\prime}\in V_{\mathrm{corr}},\ P^{\prime}\neq P^{\prime\prime}\}

//
target suppression set

m\leftarrow\left\lceil\log_{2}(cM_{\mathrm{det}})\right\rceil

//
number of random checks

N_{\mathrm{attempts}}\leftarrow\left\lceil\log_{c}\!\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\right\rceil

//
repeat-until-success budget

N_{\mathrm{ancillas}}\leftarrow\frac{5}{2}\left\lceil\log_{2}(cM_{\mathrm{det}})\right\rceil

//
\tfrac{m}{2}
- abelianity,
m
- logical injectivity,
m
- syndrome meas.

for

r\leftarrow 1

to

N_{\mathrm{attempts}}

do

/*
Each indep. draw of
\mathcal{G}
satisfies the target event
E_{\mathrm{tar}}
with probability
>1-1/c
. */

\mathcal{G}\leftarrow\{\,m\text{ random Pauli strings on }n\text{ qubits}\,\}

//
Baseline generators

\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}\leftarrow
Do ZX padding to ensure abelianity

//
\leq 1/2\log_{2}(cM_{\mathrm{det}})
ancillas

\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}\leftarrow
Do Z padding for each check to ensure injective logical action

//
\log_{2}(cM_{\mathrm{det}})
ancillas

Compute the syndromes
r(P)
for all
P\in\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{tar}}

if

r(P)\neq 0
for all
P\in\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{tar}}

then

Construct recovery channel
\mathcal{R}

//
based on
V_{\mathrm{det}},V_{\mathrm{corr}}
as in
Section

C.1

return

\widetilde{\mathcal{G}},\mathcal{R}

return
FAIL

Algorithm 1

Random stabilizer code construction for targeted reshaping

Theorem C.14

(Random stabilizer code for targeted reshaping)
.

Let
V_{\mathrm{det}},V_{\mathrm{corr}}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{n}\setminus\{I\}
, and define the corresponding target suppression set

\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{tar}}\coloneq V_{\mathrm{det}}\cup V_{\mathrm{corr}}\cup\{\operatorname{Herm}(P^{\prime}P^{\prime\prime}):P^{\prime},P^{\prime\prime}\in V_{\mathrm{corr}},\ P^{\prime}\neq P^{\prime\prime}\}.

Let
M_{\mathrm{det}}\geq|\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{tar}}|
be the detectability-size bound supplied to
Algorithm

1
, and let
c>1
be a positive constant. Use

m\coloneq\left\lceil\log_{2}(cM_{\mathrm{det}})\right\rceil

to denote the stabilizer generator cardinality. In this setting, each code draw in
Algorithm

1
succeeds with probability

p_{\mathrm{succ}}\coloneq\Pr(E_{\mathrm{tar}})\geq 1-1/c,

where success means that the target event
E_{\mathrm{tar}}
holds, namely that for the constructed code (with stabilizer subgroup
\mathcal{S}
), every element of
\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{tar}}
has nonzero syndrome. Equivalently, all Paulis in
V_{\mathrm{det}}
have nonzero syndromes and all Paulis in
V_{\mathrm{corr}}
have nonzero mutually distinguishable syndromes. Additionally, the random code satisfies:

(a)

Injective logical action of surviving terms.
If
P_{i},P_{j}\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
with
P_{i}\neq P_{j}
are such that
\Pi P_{i}\Pi\neq 0
and
\Pi P_{j}\Pi\neq 0
, then
\Pi P_{i}\Pi\neq\pm\,\Pi P_{j}\Pi
.

(b)

Survival probability for non-target Paulis.

Any Pauli
P\notin\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{tar}}
satisfies:

\Pr\!\Bigl[\,P\ \text{is undetected by the $m$ checks}\,\big|\,E_{\mathrm{tar}}\,\Bigr]\ \geq\ 2^{-m}\bigl(1-M_{\mathrm{det}}2^{-m}\bigr)\geq\frac{1}{2cM_{\mathrm{det}}}\left(1-\frac{1}{c}\right).

(c)

The ancilla cost of
Algorithm

1
is at most
5/2\,m=5/2\left\lceil\log_{2}(cM_{\mathrm{det}})\right\rceil
.

Proof.

Consider a single code draw within
Algorithm

1
. Let
G_{1},\dots,G_{m}\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
be the corresponding i.i.d. uniformly sampled checks. To ensure abelianity, we perform standard
ZX
padding (
Section

C.3
) using at most
\lfloor m/2\rfloor
ancillas and obtain commuting checks

\widehat{G}_{1},\dots,\widehat{G}_{m}.

We then use additional
Z
padding to ensure the logical action is injective on data Paulis. In particular, we append
m
fresh ancillas (one per check)
b_{1},\dots,b_{m}
and define our final stabilizer checks:

\widetilde{G}_{\ell}\coloneq\widehat{G}_{\ell}\otimes Z_{b_{\ell}},\qquad\ell=1,\dots,m.

Since the resulting checks
\{\widetilde{G}_{\ell}\}_{\ell=1}^{m}
can only generate operators that are non-trivial on the padded ancillas, no two distinct probe Paulis can differ by a stabilizer. This ensures injectivity of logical action upon survival, proving (a).

Importantly, both
ZX
and
Z
padding steps preserve the commutativity of any data Pauli
P\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
with the original random checks
\{G_{\ell}\}_{\ell=1}^{m}
. Concretely, using the commutation sign
\operatorname{sgn}(\cdot,\cdot)
defined in Eq. (
31
), we have

\operatorname{sgn}(\widetilde{G}_{\ell},P\otimes I)=\operatorname{sgn}(\widehat{G}_{\ell},P\otimes I)=\operatorname{sgn}(G_{\ell},P).

Hence the
\{\widetilde{G}_{\ell}\}_{\ell=1}^{m}
checks inherit the syndrome properties from
\{G_{\ell}\}_{\ell=1}^{m}
.

Recall that
E_{\mathrm{tar}}
denotes the event that every element of
\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{tar}}
has nonzero syndrome. By
Section

C.3
,

\Pr(E_{\mathrm{tar}})\geq 1-|\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{tar}}|2^{-m}\geq 1-M_{\mathrm{det}}2^{-m}.

(84)

Since
m=\lceil\log_{2}(cM_{\mathrm{det}})\rceil
, we get
\Pr(E_{\mathrm{tar}})\geq 1-1/c
. This proves the claimed success probability.

For the survival bound (b), apply
Section

C.3
with
\mathcal{W}=\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{tar}}
. For every fixed Pauli
P\notin\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{tar}}
we obtain

\Pr\!\bigl[P\text{ is undetectable by the }m\text{ checks}\mid E_{\mathrm{tar}}\bigr]\geq 2^{-m}\bigl(1-|\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{tar}}|2^{-m}\bigr)\geq 2^{-m}\bigl(1-M_{\mathrm{det}}2^{-m}\bigr)\geq\frac{1}{2cM_{\mathrm{det}}}\left(1-\frac{1}{c}\right).

where the last inequality follows from
m=\lceil\log_{2}(cM_{\mathrm{det}})\rceil
.
This concludes the proof of part (b).

Finally, ZX padding uses at most
\lfloor m/2\rfloor
ancillas, the additional
Z
padding uses
m
ancillas, and syndrome measurement can be implemented using
m
ancillas. Thus the total ancilla budget is at most

\left\lfloor\frac{m}{2}\right\rfloor+m+m\leq\frac{5m}{2}\leq\frac{5}{2}\left\lceil\log_{2}(cM_{\mathrm{det}})\right\rceil.

(85)

which proves (c).
∎

Corollary C.15

(Amplified success probability)
.

Theorem

C.14
proves that a single code draw from
Algorithm

1
succeeds with probability
p_{\mathrm{succ}}\geq 1-1/c
. Simply repeating this step
N_{\mathrm{attempts}}=\left\lceil\log_{c}\!\left(\frac{1}{\delta}\right)\right\rceil
times ensures that
Algorithm

1
succeeds with probability at least
1-\delta
.

C.4
Effective evolution under QEC reshaping

In this section, we quantify, how well the repeated QEC–based reshaping maps (Eq. (
65
), Eq. (
66
), and Eq. (
67
)) approximate the intended effective evolution.
Our analysis has two parts:
(i) a one–step comparison between QEC–reshaped evolution and the target
e^{\tau\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}}
with an
O(\tau^{2})
remainder (
Section

C.4
);
(ii) an
N
–step bound showing
\|A_{\tau}^{N}-(e^{t\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}}\circ\,\mathcal{C}_{\Pi})\|_{\diamond}=O(t^{2}/N)
(
Section

C.4
).

Lemma C.16

(One–step reshaping error)
.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be an
n
-qubit GKSL generator as in Eq. (
60
). Let
C
be a stabilizer code on
n^{\prime}\geq n
qubits with code projector
\Pi
, corner algebra
\mathfrak{A}_{c}\coloneq\Pi\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n^{\prime}})\Pi
and corner projection
\mathcal{C}_{\Pi}(X)\coloneq\Pi X\Pi
for any
X\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n^{\prime}})
. Let
A_{\tau}:\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n^{\prime}})\to\mathfrak{A}_{c}
be any of the three Lindbladian (
\mathcal{L}
) reshaping maps from Eq. (
65
), Eq. (
66
), and Eq. eqrefeq:logical-twirling-QEC-reshaping. Let
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}
be the corresponding effective generator of
A_{\tau}
(from
Sections

C.2
,
C.2
and
C.2
) defined on the full operator algebra
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n^{\prime}})
in
Section

C.2
.

Then the following holds for the diamond norm error:

\|A_{\tau}-(e^{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}\tau}\,\circ\mathcal{C}_{\Pi})\|_{\diamond}\leq\frac{(\|\mathcal{L}\|_{\diamond}^{2}+\|\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}\|_{\diamond}^{2})\tau^{2}}{2}

(86)

Proof.

Assume
A_{\tau}
is the physical twirling + QEC reshaping map from Eq. (
66
). The proof for the other two maps (Eq. (
65
) and Eq. (
67
)) is identical. Thus, we analyze the following map:

A_{\tau}=\mathcal{R}\circ\frac{1}{4^{n}}\sum_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}\bigl(\mathcal{U}_{P}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}\bigr)\circ\bigl(e^{\mathcal{L}\tau}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}\bigr)\circ\bigl(\mathcal{U}_{P}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}\bigr)\circ\mathcal{C}_{\Pi}.

where
\mathcal{R}
is the recovery channel of the stabilizer code
C
(
Section

C.1
) and
\mathcal{U}_{P}(X)\coloneq P(X)P^{\dagger}
is the unitary conjugation map .

Use
Section

B.2
:

e^{\tau\mathcal{L}}=I+\tau\mathcal{L}+\int_{0}^{\tau}(\tau-s)\,\mathcal{L}^{2}e^{s\mathcal{L}}\,ds
and plug it into
A_{\tau}
:

\displaystyle A_{\tau}=

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}\circ C_{\Pi}+\frac{\tau}{4^{n}}\sum_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}\mathcal{R}\circ\bigl(\mathcal{U}_{P}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}\bigr)\circ\bigl(\mathcal{L}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}\bigr)\circ\bigl(\mathcal{U}_{P}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}\bigr)\circ\mathcal{C}_{\Pi}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{4^{n}}\sum_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}\mathcal{R}\circ\bigl(\mathcal{U}_{P}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}\bigr)\circ\int_{0}^{\tau}\biggl[(\tau-s)\bigl(\mathcal{L}^{2}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}\bigr)\circ\bigl(e^{s\mathcal{L}}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}\bigr)\,ds\biggr]\circ\bigl(\mathcal{U}_{P}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}\bigr)\circ\mathcal{C}_{\Pi}

Since
\mathcal{R}
acts as an identity on
\mathfrak{A}_{c}
and using
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}
from
Section

C.2
, this simplifies to:

A_{\tau}=C_{\Pi}+\tau\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}\circ\mathcal{C}_{\Pi}+\frac{1}{4^{n}}\sum_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}\mathcal{R}\circ\bigl(\mathcal{U}_{P}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}\bigr)\circ\int_{0}^{\tau}\biggl[(\tau-s)\bigl(\mathcal{L}^{2}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}\bigr)\circ\bigl(e^{s\mathcal{L}}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}\bigr)\,ds\biggr]\circ\bigl(\mathcal{U}_{P}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}\bigr)\circ\mathcal{C}_{\Pi}

Now, applying
Section

B.2
for
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}
:

e^{\tau\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}}=I+\tau\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}+\int_{0}^{\tau}(\tau-s)\,\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}^{2}e^{s\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}}\,ds
, we get:

\displaystyle\|

\displaystyle A_{\tau}-(e^{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}\tau}\circ\,\mathcal{C}_{\Pi})\|_{\diamond}=

\displaystyle=\norm{\frac{1}{4^n}\sum_{P\in\PnHerm}\RMap\circ\bigl(\mathcal U_P\otimes I_{n'-n}\bigr)\circ\int_{0}^{\tau}\biggl[(\tau-s)\bigl(\Lindblad^2e^{s \Lindblad}\otimes I_{n'-n}\bigr)\circ\,ds\biggr] \circ\bigl(\mathcal U_P\otimes I_{n'-n}\bigr) \circ\mathcal C_\Pi- \int_0^\tau(\tau-s)\,\LindbladEff^2 e^{s\LindbladEff}\,ds\circ\mathcal C_\Pi}_{\diamond}.

Using triangle inequality, submultiplicativity of the diamond norm and
\|\mathcal{R}\|_{\diamond},\|\mathcal{C}_{\Pi}\|_{\diamond},\|\mathcal{U}_{P}\|_{\diamond}=1
, we get:

\|A_{\tau}-(e^{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}\tau}\circ\,\mathcal{C}_{\Pi})\|_{\diamond}\leq\int_{0}^{\tau}(\tau-s)\|\mathcal{L}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}\|_{\diamond}^{2}\,\|e^{s\mathcal{L}}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}\|_{\diamond}\,ds+\int_{0}^{\tau}(\tau-s)\,\|\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}\|_{\diamond}^{2}\,\|e^{s\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}}\|_{\diamond}\,ds

Since
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}
, like
\mathcal{L}
, is a valid Lindbladian generator (
Section

C.2
),
e^{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}s}
is a CPTP map and hence
\|e^{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}s}\|_{\diamond}=1
. Therefore,

\|A_{\tau}-(e^{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}\tau}\circ\,\mathcal{C}_{\Pi})\|_{\diamond}\leq\int_{0}^{\tau}(\tau-s)\|\mathcal{L}\|_{\diamond}^{2}\,ds+\int_{0}^{\tau}(\tau-s)\,\|\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}\|_{\diamond}^{2}ds=\frac{(\|\mathcal{L}\|_{\diamond}^{2}+\|\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}\|_{\diamond}^{2})\tau^{2}}{2}

concluding the proof of the lemma.
∎

We next obtain the
N
–fold product error bounds:

Lemma C.17

(N-step reshaping error)
.

Let
t=N\tau
. With the setting from
Section

C.4
, the following holds:

\Big\|\,A_{\tau}^{N}-e^{t\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}}\,\circ\mathcal{C}_{\Pi}\,\Big\|_{\diamond}\;\leq\;\frac{t^{2}(\|\mathcal{L}\|_{\diamond}^{2}+\|\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}\|_{\diamond}^{2})}{2N}.

(87)

Proof.

First, note that effective generator
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}:\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n^{\prime}})\to\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n^{\prime}})
satisfies
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}|\mathfrak{A}_{c}:\mathfrak{A}_{c}\to\mathfrak{A}_{c}
for each of the three maps (see
Sections

C.2
,
C.2
and
C.2
). Therefore:
e^{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}(t_{1}+t_{2})}\circ\mathcal{C}_{\Pi}=e^{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}t_{1}}\circ\mathcal{C}_{\Pi}\circ e^{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}t_{2}}\circ\mathcal{C}_{\Pi}
. Also, by definition
A_{\tau}\circ\mathcal{C}_{\Pi}=A_{\tau}
.

Hence, we can write the telescoping sum as

A_{\tau}^{N}-e^{t\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}}\circ\mathcal{C}_{\Pi}=\sum_{k=0}^{N-1}A_{\tau}^{N-1-k}\,\Big(A_{\tau}-e^{\tau\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}}\circ\mathcal{C}_{\Pi}\Big)\,e^{k\tau\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}}\circ\mathcal{C}_{\Pi}.

Using triangle inequality,
\|A_{\tau}\|_{\diamond}\leq 1
(could be directly checked from the definitions Eq. (
65
), Eq. (
66
), and Eq. eqrefeq:logical-twirling-QEC-reshaping),
\|e^{\tau\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}}\|_{\diamond}=1
and the submultiplicativity of the diamond norm:

\norm{A_\tau^{N} - e^{t\LindbladEff}\circ\mathcal C_\Pi}_{\diamond}\leq N\,\norm{A_\tau-e^{\tau\LindbladEff}\circ\mathcal C_\Pi}_{\diamond}.

Finally, using
Section

C.4
and
t=\tau N
, we conclude the proof of the lemma:

\norm{A_\tau^{N} - e^{t\LindbladEff}\circ\mathcal C_\Pi}_{\diamond}\leq\frac{N\tau^{2}(\|\mathcal{L}\|_{\diamond}^{2}+\|\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}\|_{\diamond}^{2})}{2}=\frac{t^{2}(\|\mathcal{L}\|_{\diamond}^{2}+\|\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}\|_{\diamond}^{2})}{2N}

∎

Corollary C.18

(N-step reshaping error for sparse Lindbladians)
.

Given
\mathcal{L}
is
M
-sparse and all coefficient are bounded in magnitude by one,
Section

C.4
simplifies to

\norm{A_\tau^{N} - e^{t\LindbladEff}\circ\mathcal C_\Pi}_{\diamond}\leq\frac{4M^{2}t^{2}}{N}

since
\|\mathcal{L}\|_{\diamond}\leq 2M
by
Section

B.3
and
\|\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}\|_{\diamond}\leq 2M
by
Section

C.2
.

Corollary C.19

(Comparison with the coherent part of the reshaped generator)
.

Let
A_{\tau}
be either the QEC reshaping map Eq. (
65
) or the QEC reshaping + partial logical twirl map Eq. (
67
). Let
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}
be the corresponding effective generator of
A_{\tau}
(from
Sections

C.2
and
C.2
) defined on the full operator algebra
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n^{\prime}})
in
Section

C.2
.

Consider the decomposition
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}=\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{coh}}+\Delta_{D},
into the coherent part
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{coh}}
and the dissipator part
\Delta_{D}
. Then

\|A_{\tau}^{N}-e^{t\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{coh}}}\circ\mathcal{C}_{\Pi}\|_{\diamond}\leq\frac{t^{2}(\|\mathcal{L}\|_{\diamond}^{2}+\|\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}\|_{\diamond}^{2})}{2N}+t\|\Delta_{D}\|_{\diamond}\leq\frac{t^{2}(\|\mathcal{L}\|_{\diamond}^{2}+\|\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}\|_{\diamond}^{2})}{2N}+2M_{D}^{2}\,t\,\max_{P_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}\setminus V_{\mathrm{corr}}}a_{kk}.

Proof.

By triangle inequality and
Section

C.4
,

\|A_{\tau}^{N}-e^{t\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{coh}}}\circ\mathcal{C}_{\Pi}\|_{\diamond}\leq\|A_{\tau}^{N}-e^{t\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}}\circ\mathcal{C}_{\Pi}\|_{\diamond}+\|(e^{t\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}}-e^{t\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{coh}}})\circ\mathcal{C}_{\Pi}\|_{\diamond}.

For the second term, Duhamel’s formula gives

e^{t\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}}-e^{t\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{coh}}}=\int_{0}^{t}e^{(t-s)\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}}\,\Delta_{D}\,e^{s\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{coh}}}\,ds.

Using
\|e^{s\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}}\|_{\diamond}=\|e^{s\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{coh}}}\|_{\diamond}=\|\mathcal{C}_{\Pi}\|_{\diamond}=1
and Eq. (
34
) with an explicit bound on
|a_{kj}|\leq\sqrt{a_{kk}a_{jj}}
, we get

\|(e^{t\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}}-e^{t\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{coh}}})\circ\mathcal{C}_{\Pi}\|_{\diamond}\leq t\|\Delta_{D}\|_{\diamond}\leq 2tM_{D}^{2}\max_{P_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}\setminus V_{\mathrm{corr}}}a_{kk}.

∎

C.5
Deferred proofs for effective generators of QEC reshaping

Let
\mathcal{L}
be an
n
-qubit GKSL generator as in Eq. (
60
), and let
\mathcal{L}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}
denote its trivial extension to the ancilla register. Let
C
be a stabilizer code on
n^{\prime}\geq n
qubits with code projector
\Pi
, corner algebra
\mathfrak{A}_{c}\coloneq\Pi\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n^{\prime}})\Pi
and syndrome map
r(\cdot)
(as in Eq. (
40
)). Suppose
V_{\mathrm{det}}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{n}
and
V_{\mathrm{corr}}\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{D}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{n}
are specified sets, and that all Paulis in
V_{\mathrm{det}}
have non-zero syndromes. Assume furthermore that all elements of
V_{\mathrm{corr}}
, have pairwise distinct nonzero syndromes. Let
\mathcal{R}:\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n^{\prime}})\to\mathfrak{A}_{c}
be the associated recovery map from
Section

C.1
based on the specified sets
V_{\mathrm{det}}
and
V_{\mathrm{corr}}
.

Then, for any
X\in\mathfrak{A}_{c}
, the effective generator of the QEC reshaping map from Eq. (
65
) is

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}(X)\coloneq\mathcal{R}\circ(\mathcal{L}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})(X)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P_{k},P_{j}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}\setminus V_{\mathrm{corr}},\\
r(P_{k})=r(P_{j})\end{subarray}}a_{kj}\Bigl((Q_{P_{k}}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})X(Q_{P_{j}}^{\dagger}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})-\frac{1}{2}\{Q_{P_{j}}^{\dagger}Q_{P_{k}}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n},X\}\Bigr)

(88)

\displaystyle-i\underbrace{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{H},\\
r(P_{k})=0\end{subarray}}h_{k}[P_{k}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n},X]}_{[H_{\mathrm{tar}},X]}\,-\,i\underbrace{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P_{k}\in V_{\mathrm{corr}},\\
P_{j}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}\setminus V_{\mathrm{corr}},\\
r(P_{k})=r(P_{j})\end{subarray}}\left({\operatorname{Re}(a_{kj})\left[\frac{i[P_{k},P_{j}]}{2}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}\,,\,X\right]+\operatorname{Im}(a_{kj})\left[\frac{\{P_{k},P_{j}\}}{2}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}\,,\,X\right]}\right)}_{[H_{\mathrm{bias}},X]}

where
Q_{P}\coloneqq\widetilde{R}_{r(P)}P\in\mathbb{P}_{n}
are Pauli operators composed of
P
and the corresponding recovery actions
\widetilde{R}_{r(P)}
as specified in Eq. (
69
) with
R_{r(P)}=\widetilde{R}_{r(P)}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}
. Notably, the resulting Hamiltonian part of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}
contains only zero-syndrome Paulis. In particular, no Pauli term from

V_{\mathrm{det}}\cup V_{\mathrm{corr}}\cup\{\operatorname{Herm}(PP^{\prime}):P,P^{\prime}\in V_{\mathrm{corr}},\ P\neq P^{\prime}\}

is present in the Pauli decompositions of
H_{\mathrm{tar}}
and
H_{\mathrm{bias}}
.

Proof.

Throughout the proof, for any probe-system Pauli
P\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
, we write again
P
for its canonical embedding
P\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}\in\mathcal{P}_{n^{\prime}}
. Likewise, we write
H
and
\mathcal{L}
for their trivial extensions to the ancilla register. Expand the Lindbladian
\mathcal{L}
acting on
X\in\mathfrak{A}_{c}
in the Pauli basis:

\mathcal{L}(X)=-i\sum_{P_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{H}}h_{k}\,[P_{k},X]\;+\;\sum_{P_{k},P_{j}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}}a_{kj}\!\left(P_{k}XP_{j}-\tfrac{1}{2}\{P_{j}P_{k},X\}\right)=-i[H,X]\,+\,\mathcal{D}(X)

We split the dissipator into four sums:

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(X)=\left(\sum_{P_{k},P_{j}\in V_{\mathrm{corr}}}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P_{k}\in V_{\mathrm{corr}},\\
P_{j}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}\setminus V_{\mathrm{corr}}\end{subarray}}+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}\setminus V_{\mathrm{corr}},\\
P_{j}\in V_{\mathrm{corr}}\end{subarray}}+\sum_{P_{k},P_{j}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}\setminus V_{\mathrm{corr}}}\right)a_{kj}\!\left(P_{k}XP_{j}-\tfrac{1}{2}\{P_{j}P_{k},X\}\right)=

\displaystyle=\mathcal{D}_{1}(X)+\mathcal{D}_{2}(X)+\mathcal{D}_{3}(X)+\mathcal{D}_{4}(X).

where
\mathcal{D}_{j}(X)
denotes the
j
-th sum in the parentheses from the left. Therefore, since
X=\Pi X\Pi

\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}(X)=\mathcal{R}\circ\mathcal{L}\,(X)=\mathcal{R}(-i\,[H,\Pi X\Pi])+\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{D}_{1}(\Pi X\Pi))+\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{D}_{2}(\Pi X\Pi))+\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{D}_{3}(\Pi X\Pi))+\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{D}_{4}(\Pi X\Pi))

We first analyze the
\mathcal{D}_{1}
term by explicitly expanding the recovery channel
\mathcal{R}
from
Section

C.1
:

\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{D}_{1}(\Pi X\Pi))=\sum_{s\in\{0,1\}^{m}}\Biggl[\sum_{P_{k},P_{j}\in V_{\mathrm{corr}}}a_{kj}\!\left(R_{s}\Pi_{s}P_{k}\Pi X\Pi P_{j}\Pi_{s}R_{s}-\tfrac{1}{2}R_{s}\Pi_{s}\{P_{j}P_{k},\Pi X\Pi\}\Pi_{s}R_{s}\right)\Biggr]

Exchanging the summation order and using the projector properties
\Pi_{s}\Pi_{s^{\prime}}=\delta_{s,s^{\prime}}\Pi_{s}
and
P_{k}\Pi_{s}=\Pi_{s\oplus r(P_{k})}P_{k}
,
\Pi_{s}P_{k}=P_{k}\Pi_{s\oplus r(P_{k})}
from Eq. (
42
), rewrite:

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{D}_{1}(\Pi X\Pi))

\displaystyle=\sum_{P_{k},P_{j}\in V_{\mathrm{corr}}}a_{kj}\!\left(\sum_{s\in\{0,1\}^{m}}\Bigl[R_{s}P_{k}\Pi_{s\oplus r(P_{k})}\,\Pi X\Pi\,\Pi_{s\oplus r(P_{j})}P_{j}R_{s}\Bigr]-\tfrac{1}{2}\Pi\{P_{j}P_{k},\Pi X\Pi\}\Pi\right)=

(89)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P_{k},P_{j}\in V_{\mathrm{corr}},\\
r(P_{k})=r(P_{j})\end{subarray}}a_{kj}\!\left(R_{r(P_{k})}P_{k}\Pi X\Pi P_{j}R_{r(P_{k})}-\tfrac{1}{2}\{\Pi P_{j}P_{k}\Pi,\Pi X\Pi\}\right)

By assumption of the lemma all Paulis
P\in V_{\mathrm{corr}}
have mutually distinguishable nonzero syndromes. Then, by construction of
\mathcal{R}
(see
Section

C.1
), all Paulis
P_{k}\in V_{\mathrm{corr}}
have a corresponding
R_{r(P_{k})}=P_{k}
. Therefore:

\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{D}_{1}(\Pi X\Pi))=\sum_{P_{k}\in V_{\mathrm{corr}}}a_{kk}\!\left(P_{k}^{2}\Pi X\Pi P_{k}^{2})-\Pi X\Pi\right)=0

(90)

Similarly to Eq. (
89
) with
X=\Pi X\Pi
:

\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{D}_{2}(X))=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P_{k}\in V_{\mathrm{corr}},\\
P_{j}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}\setminus V_{\mathrm{corr}},\\
r(P_{k})=r(P_{j})\end{subarray}}a_{kj}\!\left(R_{r(P_{k})}P_{k}XP_{j}R_{r(P_{k})}-\tfrac{1}{2}\{\Pi P_{j}P_{k}\Pi,X\}\right)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P_{k}\in V_{\mathrm{corr}},\\
P_{j}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}\setminus V_{\mathrm{corr}},\\
r(P_{k})=r(P_{j})\end{subarray}}\frac{a_{kj}}{2}\!\left(XP_{j}P_{k}-P_{j}P_{k}X\right)

where in the last equality we used
R_{r(P_{k})}=P_{k}
for all
P_{k}\in V_{\mathrm{corr}}
combined with
r(P_{k}P_{j})=r(P_{k})\oplus r(P_{j})=0
implying commutativity of
\Pi
with
P_{k}P_{j}
. Same calculation for
\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{D}_{3}(X))
yields:

\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{D}_{3}(X))=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}\setminus V_{\mathrm{corr}},\\
P_{j}\in V_{\mathrm{corr}},\\
r(P_{k})=r(P_{j})\end{subarray}}a_{kj}\!\left(R_{r(P_{j})}P_{k}XP_{j}R_{r(P_{j})}-\tfrac{1}{2}\{\Pi P_{j}P_{k}\Pi,X\}\right)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}\setminus V_{\mathrm{corr}},\\
P_{j}\in V_{\mathrm{corr}},\\
r(P_{k})=r(P_{j})\end{subarray}}\frac{a_{kj}}{2}\!\left(P_{j}P_{k}X-XP_{j}P_{k}\right)

Combining
\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{D}_{2}(X))
and
\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{D}_{3}(X))
and using the positivity of the Kossakowski matrix
a
, we get:

\mathcal{R}((\mathcal{D}_{2}+\mathcal{D}_{3})(X))=\,-\,i\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P_{k}\in V_{\mathrm{corr}},\\
P_{j}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}\setminus V_{\mathrm{corr}},\\
r(P_{k})=r(P_{j})\end{subarray}}\left({\operatorname{Re}(a_{kj})\left[\frac{i[P_{k},P_{j}]}{2}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}\,,\,X\right]+\operatorname{Im}(a_{kj})\left[\frac{\{P_{k},P_{j}\}}{2}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}\,,\,X\right]}\right)

(91)

Repeating this calculation for
\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{D}_{4}(X))
, we get

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(\mathcal{D}_{4}(X))

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P_{k},P_{j}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}\setminus V_{\mathrm{corr}},\\
r(P_{k})=r(P_{j})\end{subarray}}a_{kj}\!\left(R_{r(P_{k})}P_{k}XP_{j}R_{r(P_{j})}-\tfrac{1}{2}\{P_{j}R_{r(P_{j})}R_{r(P_{k})}P_{k},X\}\right)

(92)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P_{k},P_{j}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}\setminus V_{\mathrm{corr}},\\
r(P_{k})=r(P_{j})\end{subarray}}a_{kj}\Bigl((Q_{P_{k}}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})X(Q_{P_{j}}^{\dagger}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})-\frac{1}{2}\{Q_{P_{j}}^{\dagger}Q_{P_{k}}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n},X\}\Bigr)

where
Q_{P_{k}}\coloneq R_{r(P_{k})}P_{k}\in\mathbb{P}_{n}
are Pauli operators composed of
P_{k}
and the corresponding recovery actions
R_{r(P_{k})}
from
Section

C.1
.

Now, expand the Hamiltonian part of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}(X)
using the recovery channel
\mathcal{R}
from
Section

C.1
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(-i\,[H,\Pi X\Pi])=\sum_{s\in\{0,1\}^{m}}\Biggl[-i\sum_{P_{i}\in\mathcal{S}_{H}}h_{i}\,R_{s}\Pi_{s}[P_{i},\Pi X\Pi]\Pi_{s}R_{s}\Biggr]=

\displaystyle=\sum_{s\in\{0,1\}^{m}}\Biggl[-i\sum_{P_{i}\in\mathcal{S}_{H}}h_{i}\,R_{s}\Pi_{s}P_{i}\,\Pi X\Pi\,\Pi_{s}R_{s}+i\sum_{P_{i}\in\mathcal{S}_{H}}h_{i}\,R_{s}\Pi_{s}\Pi X\Pi\,P_{i}\,\Pi_{s}R_{s}\Biggr]

Since
\Pi_{s}\Pi_{s^{\prime}}=\delta_{s,s^{\prime}}\Pi_{s}
,
\Pi_{0}=\Pi
and
R_{0}=I
, the Hamiltonian part of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}(X)
simplifies to:

\mathcal{R}(-i\,[H,\Pi X\Pi])=-i\sum_{P_{i}\in\mathcal{S}_{H}}h_{i}\,\Pi P_{i}\Pi X\Pi\,+i\sum_{P_{i}\in\mathcal{S}_{H}}h_{i}\,\Pi X\Pi P_{i}\Pi.

Since
\Pi P\Pi=0
for every Pauli
P
with
r(P)\neq 0
, and
[\Pi,Q]=0
for every Pauli
Q
with
r(Q)=0
, the above expression further simplifies to

\mathcal{R}(-i\,[H,\Pi X\Pi])=-i\sum_{P_{i}\in\mathcal{S}_{H}\,:\,r(P_{i})=0}h_{i}\left(\ P_{i}\Pi^{2}X\Pi\,-\,\Pi X\Pi^{2}P_{i}\right)=-i\sum_{P_{i}\in\mathcal{S}_{H}\,:\,r(P_{i})=0}h_{i}[P_{i},X].

which combined with the reshaped dissipator terms (Eq. (
90
), Eq. (
91
), and Eq. (
92
)) yields Eq. (
88
) for inputs in
\mathfrak{A}_{c}
.

Finally, by detectability on
V_{\mathrm{det}}
and syndrome distinguishability on
V_{\mathrm{corr}}
, all Paulis in the following set have non-zero syndromes:
