V_{\mathrm{det}}\cup V_{\mathrm{corr}}\cup\{\operatorname{Herm}(PP^{\prime}):P,P^{\prime}\in V_{\mathrm{corr}},\ P\neq P^{\prime}\},

and are thus excluded from the Pauli decompositions of the resulting Hamiltonians
H_{\mathrm{tar}}
and
H_{\mathrm{bias}}
.
∎

Let
\mathcal{L}
be an
n
-qubit GKSL generator as in Eq. (
60
), and let
\mathcal{L}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}
denote its trivial extension to the ancilla register. Let
C
be a stabilizer code on
n^{\prime}\geq n
qubits with code projector
\Pi
and corner algebra
\mathfrak{A}_{c}\coloneq\Pi\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n^{\prime}})\Pi
. Let
\overline{P}_{a}\coloneq P_{a}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}
be the nontrivial logical Pauli to be isolated. Suppose
V_{\mathrm{corr}}\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{D}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{n}
is a specified set and let
\mathcal{R}:\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n^{\prime}})\to\mathfrak{A}_{c}
be the associated recovery map from
Section

C.1
based on
V_{\mathrm{corr}}
. Assume furthermore that all elements of
V_{\mathrm{corr}}
have pairwise distinct nonzero syndromes. Assume also that
P_{a}
is the unique
n
-qubit Pauli whose restriction to the code space contributes to the logical Pauli
\overline{P}_{a}
. Equivalently, for every
P_{k}\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
with
P_{k}\neq P_{a}
, the compressed operator
\Pi(P_{k}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})\Pi
has zero component along
\Pi\overline{P}_{a}\Pi
. Assume moreover that no nontrivial product of zero-syndrome Pauli operators appearing in the QEC-reshaped generator of
\mathcal{L}
from
Section

C.2
is proportional to a stabilizer.

Then for any
X\in\mathfrak{A}_{c}
, the effective generator of QEC reshaping + the partial logical twirl over
\overline{\mathcal{K}}_{\overline{P}_{a}}
is

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}(

\displaystyle X)\coloneq\frac{1}{|\overline{\mathcal{K}}_{\overline{P}_{a}}|}\sum_{\overline{Q}\in\overline{\mathcal{K}}_{\overline{P}_{a}}}\mathcal{U}_{\overline{Q}}\circ\mathcal{R}\circ(\mathcal{L}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})\circ\mathcal{U}_{\overline{Q}}(X)=

(93)

\displaystyle=-ih_{a}[\overline{P}_{a},X]\,-\,i\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P_{k}\in V_{\mathrm{corr}},\\
P_{j}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}\setminus V_{\mathrm{corr}},\\
P_{k}P_{j}\propto P_{a}\end{subarray}}\left({\operatorname{Re}(a_{kj})\left[\frac{i[P_{k},P_{j}]}{2}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}\,,\,X\right]+\operatorname{Im}(a_{kj})\left[\frac{\{P_{k},P_{j}\}}{2}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}\,,\,X\right]}\right)+

\displaystyle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}\setminus V_{\mathrm{corr}},\\
\operatorname{Herm}(P_{k}P_{a})\in\mathcal{S}_{D}\setminus V_{\mathrm{corr}};\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P_{j}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}\setminus V_{\mathrm{corr}},\\
\operatorname{Herm}(P_{k}P_{j})\in\{I_{n},P_{a}\}\end{subarray}}a_{kj}\Bigl((Q_{P_{k}}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})X(Q_{P_{j}}^{\dagger}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})-\frac{1}{2}\{Q_{P_{j}}^{\dagger}Q_{P_{k}}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n},X\}\Bigr)

\displaystyle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}\setminus V_{\mathrm{corr}},\\
\operatorname{Herm}(P_{k}P_{a})\notin\mathcal{S}_{D}\setminus V_{\mathrm{corr}}\end{subarray}}a_{kk}\Bigl((Q_{P_{k}}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})X(Q_{P_{k}}^{\dagger}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})-X\Bigr).

where
Q_{P}\coloneqq\widetilde{R}_{r(P)}P\in\mathbb{P}_{n}
are Pauli operators composed of
P
and the corresponding recovery actions
\widetilde{R}_{r(P)}
as specified in
Eq. (
69
) with
R_{r(P)}=\widetilde{R}_{r(P)}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}

Proof.

First, apply
Section

C.2
with
V_{\mathrm{det}}=\emptyset
for the chosen code and recovery map. Since all elements of
V_{\mathrm{corr}}
have pairwise distinct nonzero syndromes, the QEC-reshaped generator on
X\in\mathfrak{A}_{c}
is

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}\circ(\mathcal{L}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})(X)=\underbrace{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P_{k},P_{j}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}\setminus V_{\mathrm{corr}},\\
r(P_{k})=r(P_{j})\end{subarray}}a_{kj}\Bigl((Q_{P_{k}}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})X(Q_{P_{j}}^{\dagger}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})-\frac{1}{2}\{Q_{P_{j}}^{\dagger}Q_{P_{k}}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n},X\}\Bigr)}_{A}

\displaystyle-i\underbrace{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{H},\\
r(P_{k})=0\end{subarray}}h_{k}[P_{k}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n},X]}_{B}\,-\,i\underbrace{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P_{k}\in V_{\mathrm{corr}},\\
P_{j}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}\setminus V_{\mathrm{corr}},\\
r(P_{k})=r(P_{j})\end{subarray}}\left({\operatorname{Re}(a_{kj})\left[\frac{i[P_{k},P_{j}]}{2}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}\,,\,X\right]+\operatorname{Im}(a_{kj})\left[\frac{\{P_{k},P_{j}\}}{2}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}\,,\,X\right]}\right)}_{C}

where
r(\cdot)
is the syndrome map of code
C
.

We first analyze the contribution of term
B
to
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}
. Applying the logical twirl to
B
, we get that for any
X\in\mathfrak{A}_{c}
:

\frac{-i}{|\overline{\mathcal{K}}_{\overline{P}_{a}}|}\sum_{\overline{Q}\in\overline{\mathcal{K}}_{\overline{P}_{a}}}\mathcal{U}_{\overline{Q}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{H},\\
r(P_{k})=0\end{subarray}}h_{k}[P_{k}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n,\,}\mathcal{U}_{\overline{Q}}(X)]=-i\left[\frac{1}{|\overline{\mathcal{K}}_{\overline{P}_{a}}|}\sum_{\overline{Q}\in\overline{\mathcal{K}}_{\overline{P}_{a}}}\mathcal{U}_{\overline{Q}}\bigl(\Pi(H\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})\Pi\bigr),X\right]

where the right-hand side follows from the fact that
\mathcal{U}_{\overline{Q}}
is a unitary conjugation.

By the assumptions of the lemma, the projected Hamiltonian
\Pi(H\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})\Pi
has exactly one contribution proportional to
\overline{P}_{a}
, namely
h_{a}\overline{P}_{a}
. Applying
Section

B.4.4
, the average over
\overline{\mathcal{K}}_{\overline{P}_{a}}
removes all other non-identity logical Pauli terms, while any multiple of
\Pi
can be absorbed into
X
. Therefore the contribution of term
B
to
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}
is

-ih_{a}[\overline{P}_{a},X].

(94)

The same exact argument applies to term
C
. Since each
P_{k}P_{j}
in term
C
has zero syndrome, the only surviving Pauli terms are those whose restriction to the code space contributes to
\overline{P}_{a}
, namely the terms with
P_{k}P_{j}\propto P_{a}
. Thus the contribution of term
C
to
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}
is Eq. (
95
).

-\,i\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P_{k}\in V_{\mathrm{corr}},\\
P_{j}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}\setminus V_{\mathrm{corr}},\\
P_{k}P_{j}\propto P_{a}\end{subarray}}\left({\operatorname{Re}(a_{kj})\left[\frac{i[P_{k},P_{j}]}{2}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}\,,\,X\right]+\operatorname{Im}(a_{kj})\left[\frac{\{P_{k},P_{j}\}}{2}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}\,,\,X\right]}\right)

(95)

It remains to quantify the contribution of term
A
. For that, consider logically twirling its single summand:

\frac{a_{kj}}{|\overline{\mathcal{K}}_{\overline{P}_{a}}|}\sum_{\overline{Q}\in\overline{\mathcal{K}}_{\overline{P}_{a}}}\mathcal{U}_{\overline{Q}}\Bigl((Q_{P_{k}}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})\mathcal{\,}U_{\overline{Q}}(X)\,(Q_{P_{j}}^{\dagger}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})-\frac{1}{2}\{Q_{P_{j}}^{\dagger}Q_{P_{k}}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n},\mathcal{U}_{\overline{Q}}(X)\}\Bigr)

If
P_{k}=P_{j}
and thus
Q_{P_{k}}=Q_{P_{j}}
for
r(P_{k})=r(P_{j})
, a single summand contributes

\frac{a_{kk}}{|\overline{\mathcal{K}}_{\overline{P}_{a}}|}\sum_{\overline{Q}\in\overline{\mathcal{K}}_{\overline{P}_{a}}}\mathcal{U}_{\overline{Q}}\Bigl((Q_{P_{k}}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})\mathcal{\,}U_{\overline{Q}}(X)\,(Q_{P_{k}}^{\dagger}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})-\mathcal{U}_{\overline{Q}}(X)\}\Bigr)=a_{kk}\Bigl((Q_{P_{k}}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})X(Q_{P_{k}}^{\dagger}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})-X\Bigr)

(96)

where the right-hand side follows from each
Q_{P_{k}}
being a Pauli. Concretely, conjugation of
Q_{P_{k}}
by any
\overline{Q}\in\overline{\mathcal{K}}_{\overline{P}_{a}}
maps
Q_{P_{k}}
to
\pm Q_{P_{k}}
, and since
Q_{P_{k}}
is conjugated twice, the final sign cancels.

Now consider a summand where
\operatorname{Herm}(P_{k}P_{j})\notin\{I_{n},\pm P_{a}\}
and
r(P_{k})=r(P_{j})
. Then
Q_{P_{j}}^{\dagger}Q_{P_{k}}
is a nonidentity zero-syndrome probe Pauli obtained as a product of Pauli operators appearing in the QEC-reshaped generator. By the assumption of the lemma, it is not proportional to a stabilizer, and since
\operatorname{Herm}(P_{k}P_{j})\neq\pm P_{a}
, its logical action is neither equal to the identity nor to
\overline{P}_{a}
. Therefore the anti-commutator part is zero directly by
Section

B.4.4
. The sandwiched term
\overline{Q}(Q_{P_{k}}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})\overline{Q}\,X\,\overline{Q}(Q_{P_{j}}^{\dagger}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})\overline{Q}
also sums to zero, since half of
\overline{\mathcal{K}}_{\overline{P}_{a}}
commute with the
Q_{P_{j}}^{\dagger}Q_{P_{k}}
and half anti-commute:

\frac{a_{kj}}{|\overline{\mathcal{K}}_{\overline{P}_{a}}|}\sum_{\overline{Q}\in\overline{\mathcal{K}}_{\overline{P}_{a}}}\overline{Q}(Q_{P_{k}}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})\overline{Q}X\overline{Q}\,(Q_{P_{j}}^{\dagger}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})\overline{Q}-\frac{1}{2}\{\overline{Q}(Q_{P_{j}}^{\dagger}Q_{P_{k}}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})\overline{Q},X\}\Bigr)=0

(97)

Finally, if
P_{k}P_{j}\propto P_{a}
and thus
Q_{P_{k}}Q_{P_{j}}^{\dagger}\propto P_{a}
for
r(P_{k})=r(P_{j})
, a single summand contributes:

\displaystyle\frac{a_{kj}}{|\overline{\mathcal{K}}_{\overline{P}_{a}}|}\sum_{\overline{Q}\in\overline{\mathcal{K}}_{\overline{P}_{a}}}\overline{Q}(Q_{P_{k}}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})\overline{Q}X\overline{Q}\,(Q_{P_{j}}^{\dagger}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})\overline{Q}-\frac{1}{2}\{\overline{Q}(Q_{P_{j}}^{\dagger}Q_{P_{k}}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})\overline{Q},X\}\Bigr)=

(98)

\displaystyle=a_{kj}(Q_{P_{k}}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})X(Q_{P_{j}}^{\dagger}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})-\frac{1}{2}\{Q_{P_{j}}^{\dagger}Q_{P_{k}}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n},X\}

where again the anti-commutator simplifies directly by
Section

B.4.4
. The
(Q_{P_{k}}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})X(Q_{P_{j}}^{\dagger}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})
term follows from the fact that
Q_{P_{k}}Q_{P_{j}}^{\dagger}\propto P_{a}
and thus all
\overline{Q}\in{\mathcal{K}}_{\overline{P}_{a}}
commute with
Q_{P_{k}}Q_{P_{j}}^{\dagger}
. This implies that the commutation sign (defined in Eq. (
31
)) of these Paulis satisfies
\operatorname{sgn}(\overline{Q},Q_{P_{k}})=\operatorname{sgn}(\overline{Q},Q_{P_{j}})
and hence:

\overline{Q}(Q_{P_{k}}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})\overline{Q}X\overline{Q}\,(Q_{P_{j}}^{\dagger}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})\overline{Q}=\overline{Q}^{2}(Q_{P_{k}}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})X(Q_{P_{j}}^{\dagger}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})\overline{Q}^{2}=(Q_{P_{k}}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})X(Q_{P_{j}}^{\dagger}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})

Adding the summands together and simplifying according to Eq. (
98
), Eq. (
96
), and Eq. (
97
), we obtain that the contribution of term
A
to
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}
is:

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P_{k},P_{j}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}\setminus V_{\mathrm{corr}},\\
r(P_{k})=r(P_{j})\end{subarray}}\frac{a_{kj}}{|\overline{\mathcal{K}}_{\overline{P}_{a}}|}\sum_{\overline{Q}\in\overline{\mathcal{K}}_{\overline{P}_{a}}}\mathcal{U}_{\overline{Q}}\Bigl((Q_{P_{k}}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})\,\mathcal{U}_{\overline{Q}}(X)\,(Q_{P_{j}}^{\dagger}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})-\frac{1}{2}\{Q_{P_{j}}^{\dagger}Q_{P_{k}}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n},\,\mathcal{U}_{\overline{Q}}(X)\}\Bigr)=

(99)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}\setminus V_{\mathrm{corr}},\\
\operatorname{Herm}(P_{k}P_{a})\in\mathcal{S}_{D}\setminus V_{\mathrm{corr}};\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P_{j}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}\setminus V_{\mathrm{corr}},\\
\operatorname{Herm}(P_{k}P_{j})\in\{I_{n},P_{a}\}\end{subarray}}a_{kj}\Bigl((Q_{P_{k}}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})X(Q_{P_{j}}^{\dagger}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})-\frac{1}{2}\{Q_{P_{j}}^{\dagger}Q_{P_{k}}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n},X\}\Bigr)+

\displaystyle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}\setminus V_{\mathrm{corr}},\\
\operatorname{Herm}(P_{k}P_{a})\notin\mathcal{S}_{D}\setminus V_{\mathrm{corr}}\end{subarray}}a_{kk}\Bigl((Q_{P_{k}}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})X(Q_{P_{k}}^{\dagger}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})-X\Bigr).

Combining the contributions of terms
A,B,C
from Eq. (
99
), Eq. (
94
), and Eq. (
95
) proves the
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}
in Eq. (
93
).
∎

Appendix D
Dissipator Structure Learning

Recall the
n
-qubit Lindbladian we wish to learn:

\mathcal{L}(\rho)=-i\sum_{P_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{H}}h_{k}\commutator{P_k}{\rho}+\sum_{P_{k},P_{j}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}}a_{kj}\left(P_{k}\rho P_{j}-\tfrac{1}{2}\anticommutator{P_j P_k}{\rho}\right).

(100)

As in the rest of the work, we assume without loss of generality that the Lindbladian has been rescaled so that all coefficients have magnitude at most one:
|h_{k}|\leq 1,|a_{kj}|\leq 1.
In this section, however, we do not assume sparsity.

In this section, we present our results on learning the
\eta
-heavy subset of the dissipator structure. In particular, we present
Algorithm

4
that identifies:

\mathcal{S}_{D,\eta}\coloneq\{P_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}:a_{kk}>\eta\}

(101)

given an upper bound on
\|\mathcal{L}\|_{\diamond}
. To attain the
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(1/\eta\right)
evolution time, our algorithm extends the hierarchical learning strategy of
70
to open-system dynamics using the QEC-based Lindbladian reshaping introduced in Eq. (
66
). More specifically, the algorithm first samples the strongest dissipative terms and then applies a testing step to eliminate false positives. It subsequently updates the error correcting code adaptively to suppress the identified terms, and then iterates the same procedure on the remaining weaker terms.

The main result of this section is the following theorem:

Theorem D.1

(Hierarchical dissipator structure learning)
.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be an
n
-qubit Lindbladian as in Eq. (
100
) with
\|\mathcal{L}\|_{\diamond}\leq\Lambda_{L}
. Fix
\eta,\delta_{d}\in(0,1)
and assume black-box access to
e^{t\mathcal{L}}
. Let
\mathcal{S}_{D,\eta}\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{D}
denote the
\eta
-heavy part of the dissipator structure defined in Eq. (
101
). Let
\Gamma_{\mathrm{tot}}
denote the total Lindbladian decay rate defined in Eq. (
120
). Then running
Algorithm

4
returns an estimator
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,\eta}
of
\mathcal{S}_{D,\eta}
, satisfying:

1.

(Accuracy) With probability
\geq 1-\delta_{d}
, the output estimator obeys

\mathcal{S}_{D,\eta}\subseteq\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,\eta}\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{D,\eta/2}
.

2.

(Evolution time) It applies
e^{\mathcal{L}t}
for a total evolution time

t_{\mathrm{tot}}=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{1}{\eta}\right).

3.

(Time resolution) It only ever applies
e^{\mathcal{L}t}
for
t\geq t_{\mathrm{res}}
, where

t_{\mathrm{res}}=\Omega\Bigl(\frac{\eta}{\Lambda_{L}^{2}}\Bigr).

4.

(Number of experiments) The total number of experiments is

N_{\exp}\;=\;\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{\Gamma_{\mathrm{tot}}}{\eta}+1\right).

with the total number of interleaved QEC rounds bounded by
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\Lambda_{L}^{2}/\eta^{2}\right)
.

5.

(Ancilla cost)
The number of ancillary qubits used by the procedure (dominated by Bell sampling + QEC) is at most

n+\mathcal{O}\left(\log(\frac{\Gamma_{\mathrm{tot}}}{\eta}+1)\right)

6.

(Classical overhead) The classical cost of the algorithm (dominated by repeated QEC in structure testing) is:

\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\bigl(n+(\Gamma_{\mathrm{tot}}/\eta)^{4}\bigr)\Lambda_{L}^{2}/\eta^{2}\right)

Proof.

See Appendix
D.4
.
∎

Remark D.2

(Ancilla-reduced dissipator structure learner)
.

In Appendix
H
, following
70
, we also briefly describe an ancilla-reduced variant of
Theorem

D.1
that improves the total ancilla count from
n+\mathcal{O}\left(\log(\frac{\Gamma_{\mathrm{tot}}}{\eta}+1)\right)
to
\mathcal{O}\left(\log(\frac{\Gamma_{\mathrm{tot}}}{\eta}+1)\right)
at the cost of increased black-box evolution time
t_{\mathrm{tot}}=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{M_{D,\mathrm{eff}}(\eta)\log(n)}{\eta}\right)
, where
M_{D,\mathrm{eff}}(\eta)
is the
\eta
-effective sparsity:

M_{D,\mathrm{eff}}(\eta)\coloneq\sum_{P_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}}\min\left\{1,\frac{a_{kk}}{\eta}\right\}=|\{P_{k}:a_{kk}\geq\eta\}|+\frac{1}{\eta}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{D},\\
a_{kk}\leq\eta\end{subarray}}a_{kk}

This section is organized as follows:

•

Dissipator structure hierarchy & notation.
In Appendix
D.1
, we describe the hierarchical learning procedure and introduce the necessary notation.

•

Candidate identification via Bell sampling.
In Appendix
D.2
, we present
Algorithm

2
for sampling the strongest unknown dissipator terms via Bell sampling and prove its guarantees in
Theorem

D.5
.

•

Candidate testing via syndrome measurements.
In Appendix
D.3
, we present
Algorithm

3
that enables removal of false positives of the dissipator structure identification by testing the decay rates of identified terms. The testing is done via syndrome measurements of random stabilizer codes.

•

Dissipator structure learning algorithm.
In Appendix
D.4
, we combine the dissipator candidate identification (
Algorithm

2
) and dissipator structure testing (
Algorithm

3
) in a hierarchical fashion into
Algorithm

4
, which learns the
\eta
-heavy part of the dissipator structure
\mathcal{S}_{D,\eta}
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(1/\eta\right)
interaction time.

•

Deferred proofs.

In Appendix
D.5
and Appendix
D.6
, we provide the deferred proofs related to dissipator structure identification and testing.

D.1
Hierarchy and notation

The hierarchy is formed by splitting the learning problem according to the following definition:

Definition D.3

(Round-
j
dissipator set)
.

Given a Lindbladian from Eq. (
100
) and target accuracy
\eta>0
, define the round-
j
dissipator Pauli set as:

\mathcal{Q}_{j}\;\coloneq\;\bigl\{\,P_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}:2^{-(j+1)}<a_{kk}\leq 2^{-j}\,\bigr\},\qquad j=0,1,2,\ldots,\lceil\log_{2}(1/\eta)\rceil-1.

(102)

By construction, learning all
\mathcal{Q}_{j}
is sufficient for learning the
\eta
-heavy dissipator structure
\mathcal{S}_{D,\eta}
:

\mathcal{S}_{D,\eta}\subseteq\bigcup_{j=0}^{\lceil\log_{2}(1/\eta)\rceil-1}\mathcal{Q}_{j}\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{D,\eta/2}.

Hierarchical learning proceeds in rounds indexed by
j
, where round
j
targets the terms in
\mathcal{Q}_{j}
. The algorithm begins by identifying the largest diagonal dissipator terms and then updates the QEC target-removal set
V_{\mathrm{corr}}
(
Section

C.2
) to suppress the terms that have been learned. It subsequently increases the interrogation time and repeats the same procedure on progressively weaker bins. Within each round, the algorithm first samples the strongest remaining unknown dissipator terms at the current scale and then tests the resulting candidates to eliminate false positives.

Across the hierarchical rounds, we keep track of all terms identified so far. Let
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,j-1}
denote the set of Hermitian Paulis known at the beginning of round
j
. For every
j
, we maintain the following invariant:

\mathcal{Q}_{k}\subseteq\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,j-1}\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{D,\eta/2}\qquad\text{for all }k\in\{0,\dots,j-1\}.

(103)

Another relevant quantity is the deterministic residual tail-rate
\Gamma_{j}
:

\Gamma_{j}\coloneq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{D},\\
a_{kk}\leq 2^{-j}\end{subarray}}a_{kk}

(104)

D.2
Candidate identification via Bell sampling

We now describe
Algorithm

2
, which identifies a superset of
\mathcal{Q}_{j}\setminus\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,j-1}
via Bell sampling. In round
j
, we set
V_{\mathrm{corr}}=\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,j-1}
and draw a random stabilizer code via
Algorithm

1
, together with its recovery map. For the resulting round-
j
code, recovery map, and code projector, define the corresponding physical-twirling plus QEC-reshaping map

A_{\tau,D}^{(j)}\coloneq\mathcal{R}_{j}\circ\frac{1}{4^{n}}\sum_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}\bigl(\mathcal{U}_{P}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}\bigr)\circ\bigl(e^{\mathcal{L}\tau}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}\bigr)\circ\bigl(\mathcal{U}_{P}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}\bigr)\circ\mathcal{C}_{\Pi_{j}}.

(105)

This is the round-specific instance of Eq. (
66
). For an intended interrogation time
t
and an integer
N
, we write

A_{D}^{(j)}(t,N)\coloneq\left(A_{t/N,D}^{(j)}\right)^{N}.

(106)

Conditioned on the success of the random QEC construction,
Section

C.2
and
Section

C.2
imply that the effective generator of
A_{\tau,D}^{(j)}
on
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n^{\prime}})
is purely dissipative and takes the form

\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}^{(j)}(\rho)=\sum_{P_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}\setminus\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,j-1}}a_{kk}\Bigl((Q_{P_{k}}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})\rho(Q_{P_{k}}^{\dagger}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})-\rho\Bigr),

(107)

where

Q_{P}\coloneqq\widetilde{R}_{r(P)}P\in\mathbb{P}_{n}
is the reshaped Pauli obtained by composing
P
with the recovery action
specified in Eq. (
69
), with

R_{r(P)}=\widetilde{R}_{r(P)}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}
. Therefore, every unknown term
P_{k}\in\mathcal{Q}_{j}\setminus\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,j-1}
contributes to the reshaped dynamics through the logical Pauli
Q_{P_{k}}
. We then use Bell sampling to identify these recovered logical Paulis modulo stabilizers. To convert the sampled logical Paulis back into physical Pauli candidates, the algorithm composes each Bell-sampling outcome with the recovery actions associated with all possible syndromes and retains the resulting probe-supported Paulis. This produces a candidate set
\widehat{\mathcal{Q}}_{j}
satisfying
\widehat{\mathcal{Q}}_{j}\supseteq\mathcal{Q}_{j}\setminus\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,j-1}
with probability at least
1-\delta_{j}
.

Given
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,j-1}
and a particular drawn code, let

\Gamma_{j}^{\mathrm{eff}}\coloneq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}\setminus\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,j-1}\\
Q_{P_{k}}\not\propto I_{n}\end{subarray}}a_{kk}

(108)

denote the total effective decay rate of the reshaped dissipator. By Eq. (
103
), all bins above the current scale have already been included in
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,j-1}
. Hence every term in
\mathcal{S}_{D}\setminus\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,j-1}
has rate at most
2^{-j}
, and therefore
\Gamma_{j}^{\mathrm{eff}}\leq\Gamma_{j}
.

The interrogation time is chosen as
t_{j}\coloneq 1/\widehat{\Gamma}_{j}
, where the estimator
\widehat{\Gamma}_{j}
is obtained by applying the pre-calibration routine
Section

D.5
to the reshaped dynamics
A_{D}^{(j)}
down to the current bin scale
2^{-(j+1)}
. With probability at least
1-\delta_{j}/4
, it satisfies

\max\{\Gamma_{j}^{\mathrm{eff}},2^{-(j+1)}\}\leq\widehat{\Gamma}_{j}\leq 4\max\{\Gamma_{j}^{\mathrm{eff}},2^{-(j+1)}\}.

(109)

and admits a trivial upper bound
\widehat{\Gamma}_{j}=\mathcal{O}\left(\Gamma_{j}^{\mathrm{eff}}+2^{-(j+1)}\right)\leq\mathcal{O}\left(\Gamma_{j}+2^{-(j+1)}\right).

Notably, because of QEC reshaping, the sampled Paulis might extend beyond the probe qubits via stabilizer products. Therefore, for any Pauli
A\in\mathcal{P}_{n^{\prime}}
, we define its probe-supported stabilizer reduction with respect to the round-
j
code by

\operatorname{Probe}_{j}(A)\coloneq\left\{P\in\mathcal{P}_{n}:\exists\sigma\in S_{j}\text{ such that }\sigma A\propto P\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}\right\},

(110)

where
S_{j}
is the stabilizer subgroup of the
j
-th round code. If no stabilizer representative of
A
is supported only on the probe subsystem, then
\operatorname{Probe}_{j}(A)=\emptyset
. By the
Z
-padding step in
Algorithm

1
, no two distinct probe Paulis are equivalent modulo
S_{j}
. Hence for every
A\in\mathcal{P}_{n^{\prime}}
, we have
\absolutevalue{\operatorname{Probe}_j(A)}\leq 1.

When
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,j-1}=\emptyset
, the QEC step is trivial: we take

\mathcal{G}_{j}=\emptyset
,
S_{j}=\{I_{n}\}
,
\Pi_{j}=I_{n}
,
\mathcal{R}_{j}=I
,
m_{j}=0
, and
n^{\prime}=n
. Equivalently, the reshaping map is just physical Pauli twirling, without error correction. All bounds below are stated with
(|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,j-1}|+1)^{2}
so that this initial case is included uniformly.

Inputs :

[label=(0), leftmargin=*, nosep]

1.

Hierarchical round index
j
.

2.

Set
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,j-1}
of Hermitian Paulis identified in prior hierarchical rounds satisfying the invariant in eq.(
103
).

3.

Upper bound
\Lambda_{L}\geq\|\mathcal{L}\|_{\diamond}
.

4.

Confidence parameter
\delta_{j}\in(0,1)
.

5.

Access to
n
-qubit evolution
e^{t\mathcal{L}}
.

6.

Access to
n+\mathcal{O}\left(\log(|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,j-1}|+1)\right)
noiseless ancilla qubits.

Output :

A set
\widehat{\mathcal{Q}}_{j}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{n}
with

|\widehat{\mathcal{Q}}_{j}|=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left((2^{j}\Gamma_{j}+1)(|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,j-1}|+1)^{2}\right)
such that
\widehat{\mathcal{Q}}_{j}\supseteq\mathcal{Q}_{j}\setminus\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,j-1}
with probability
\geq 1-\delta_{j}
.

if

\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,j-1}=\emptyset

then

\mathcal{G}_{j}\leftarrow\emptyset,\quad S_{j}\leftarrow\{I_{n}\},\quad\Pi_{j}\leftarrow I_{n},\quad\mathcal{R}_{j}\leftarrow I,\quad n^{\prime}\leftarrow n

//
no error correction needed

else

V_{\mathrm{corr}}\leftarrow\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,j-1}

//
QEC target correctable set

(\mathcal{G}_{j},\mathcal{R}_{j})\leftarrow\texttt{RandomQEC}(\emptyset,V_{\mathrm{corr}},|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,j-1}|^{2},\delta_{j}/4,2)

//
Alg
1
stab. generator & recovery ch.

S_{j}\leftarrow\langle\mathcal{G}_{j}\rangle

//
corresponding stabilizer group

\Pi_{j}\leftarrow\prod_{G\in\mathcal{G}_{j}}(1+G)/2

n^{\prime}\leftarrow n+\frac{5}{2}\left\lceil\log_{2}(2|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,j-1}|^{2})\right\rceil

//
left register size accounting for QEC ancillas

m_{j}\leftarrow|\mathcal{G}_{j}|

//
cardinality of the stabilizer generator
m_{j}=\mathcal{O}\left(\log( |\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,j-1}|^2+1)\right)

Define access to
A_{D}^{(j)}(t,N)
using Eq. (
106
)

\widehat{\Gamma}_{j}\leftarrow\texttt{EstimateGamma}(\max\{\Lambda_{L},2^{-(j+1)}\},2^{-(j+1)},\delta_{j}/4,A_{D}^{(j)})

//
estimate from
Section

D.5

N_{\mathrm{reshape}}\leftarrow 10e\cdot 2^{j}\Lambda_{L}^{2}/\widehat{\Gamma}_{j}

//
# of reshape cycles per sample

t_{j}\leftarrow 1/\widehat{\Gamma}_{j},\quad\tau\leftarrow t_{j}/N_{\mathrm{reshape}}

//
interrogation time and reshape step

N_{\mathrm{meas}}\leftarrow 4e\cdot\widehat{\Gamma}_{j}\,2^{j}\log(8\widehat{\Gamma}_j2^j/\delta_j)

//
# of Bell-basis measurements

\widehat{\mathcal{Q}}_{j}\leftarrow\bigcup_{s\in\{0,1\}^{m_{j}}}\operatorname{Probe}_{j}(R_{s})

//
identity-coset candidates,
R_{s}
-- recovery operators of
\mathcal{R}_{j}

for

i\leftarrow 1

to

N_{\mathrm{meas}}

do

Prepare
\ket{\Phi_0}\leftarrow\ket{\Phi_+}^{\otimes n^{\prime}}
on
L\otimes R

//
split into left and right registers

Project to code corner algebra:
\rho_{\mathrm{prep}}\propto(\Pi_{j}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}})\ket{\Phi_0}\bra{\Phi_0}(\Pi_{j}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}})

//
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,j-1}|^{2}\right)
attempts

for

k\leftarrow 1

to

N_{\mathrm{reshape}}

do

Randomly sample
P\sim\mathcal{P}_{n}

//
for Physical Pauli twirling

Apply
P\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}
to the left register

Evolve the left register qubits by
e^{\tau\mathcal{L}}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}

Apply
P\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}
to the left register

Apply
\mathcal{R}_{j}
to the left register

//
error detection + correction, trivial if
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,j-1}=\emptyset

Measure in the Bell basis and let
Q\in\mathcal{P}_{n^{\prime}}
be the Pauli outcome

\widehat{\mathcal{Q}}_{j}\leftarrow\widehat{\mathcal{Q}}_{j}\cup\bigcup_{s\in\{0,1\}^{m_{j}}}\operatorname{Probe}_{j}(R_{s}Q)

//
compose sampled logical w all recovery operators of
\mathcal{R}_{j}

return

\widehat{\mathcal{Q}}_{j}\setminus\{I_{n}\}

Algorithm 2

Dissipator candidate identification subroutine,
j
th round

The key ingredient of this algorithm is the following lemma, which we invoke on the reshaped dynamics:

Lemma D.4

(Bell sampling of effective diagonal dissipator)
.

Let
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}
be an
n^{\prime}
-qubit Lindbladian with purely diagonal dissipator part

\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}(\rho)=\sum_{Q_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{eff}}\,\subseteq\,\mathcal{P}_{n^{\prime}}\setminus\{I\}}\alpha_{kk}\left(Q_{k}\rho Q_{k}-\rho\right)

where
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{eff}}
is a set of distinct Hermitian Paulis and
\alpha_{kk}\geq 0
for all
Q_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{eff}}
. Define the total effective dissipative rate
\Gamma_{\mathrm{eff}}\coloneq\sum_{Q_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{eff}}}\alpha_{kk}.
Fix a target Pauli
Q_{r}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{eff}}
. Let
C
be a stabilizer code on
n^{\prime}
qubits with code projector
\Pi
and stabilizer subgroup
S
. Let
G_{\mathrm{eff}}\coloneq\langle\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{eff}}\rangle\subseteq\mathbb{P}_{n^{\prime}}
denote the subgroup generated by
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{eff}}
. Assume that all Paulis

Q\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{eff}}
have zero syndrome
r(Q)=0
with respect to
C
, and that for every
R\in G_{\mathrm{eff}}
,
R\propto s\in S\implies s=I_{n^{\prime}}
. Equivalently, no nontrivial product of elements of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{eff}}
is proportional to a stabilizer. Let
\rho_{0}
denote a
2n^{\prime}
-qubit maximally entangled state
\rho_{0}\coloneq\ket{\Phi_0}\bra{\Phi_0}
where
\ket{\Phi_0}\coloneq\ket{\Phi_+}^{\otimes n^{\prime}}
and
\ket{\Phi_+}=(\ket{00}+\ket{11})/\sqrt{2}.

Let
\widetilde{\rho}
denote the state immediately before the Bell basis measurement

\widetilde{\rho}\coloneq(e^{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}t}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}})\left(\frac{\Pi\otimes I_{n^{\prime}}\,\rho_{0}\,\Pi\otimes I_{n^{\prime}}}{\tr(\Pi\otimes I_{n'} \,\rho_0)}\right).

For any Pauli
Q\in\mathbb{P}_{n^{\prime}}
, let
O_{Q}\coloneq(Q\otimes I_{n^{\prime}})\ket{\Phi_0}\bra{\Phi_0}(Q^{\dagger}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}})
denote the corresponding Bell-measurement projector. Then, for any
t\geq 0
, a single run of Bell sampling on
\widetilde{\rho}
satisfies

\displaystyle\Pr(\text{sample $Q_r$ modulo stabilizers})

\displaystyle\coloneq\tr\left(\sum_{\sigma\in S}O_{\sigma Q_{r}}\,\widetilde{\rho}\right)\geq e^{-\Gamma_{\mathrm{eff}}t}\alpha_{rr}t.

(111)

Proof.

See Appendix
D.5
.
∎

Theorem D.5

(Round-
j
dissipator structure sampling)
.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be an
n
-qubit Lindbladian as in Eq. (
100
) with
\|\mathcal{L}\|_{\diamond}\leq\Lambda_{L}
. Assume black-box access to
e^{t\mathcal{L}}
. Fix a hierarchical round
j
and a failure probability
\delta_{j}\in(0,1)
. Let
\mathcal{S}_{D,\eta}\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{D}
denote the
\eta
-heavy part of the dissipator structure defined in Eq. (
101
). Let
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,j-1}
denote the set of Hermitian Paulis known at the beginning of the
j
-th hierarchical round, and assume it satisfies the invariant in Eq. (
103
). Let
\Gamma_{j}
denote the round-
j
residual rate from Eq. (
104
) and let
\widehat{\Gamma}_{j}
denote the output of the pre-calibration subroutine from
Section

D.5
.

Then running
Algorithm

2
outputs a candidate set
\widehat{\mathcal{Q}}_{j}
and satisfies:

1

(Coverage and size) With probability
\geq 1-\delta_{j}
,

\widehat{\mathcal{Q}}_{j}\supseteq\mathcal{Q}_{j}\setminus\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,j-1},\qquad|\widehat{\mathcal{Q}}_{j}|=\mathcal{O}\left(2^{j}\widehat{\Gamma}_{j}\bigl(|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,j-1}|+1\bigr)^{2}\log(2^j\widehat{\Gamma}_j/\delta_j)\right)=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left((2^{j}\Gamma_{j}+1)\bigl(|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,j-1}|+1\bigr)^{2}\right).

2

(Total evolution time)
The total evolution time under
e^{t\mathcal{L}}
, including pre-calibration, is

t_{\mathrm{tot}}=\mathcal{O}\left(2^{j}\log\left(\frac{2^{j}\Gamma_{j}+1}{\delta_{j}}\right)+2^{j}\log\left(\frac{\log(2^j\Lambda_L+4)}{\delta_{j}}\right)\right).

3

(Time resolution) It only ever applies
e^{\mathcal{L}t}
for
t\geq t_{\mathrm{res}}
, where

t_{\mathrm{res}}=\Omega\Bigl(\frac{1}{\Lambda_{L}^{2}2^{j}}\Bigr).

4

(Number of experiments)
The number of Bell-sampling experiments, including pre-calibration, is

N_{\exp}=\mathcal{O}\left((2^{j}\Gamma_{j}+1)\log\left(\frac{2^{j}\Gamma_{j}+1}{\delta_{j}}\right)+\log(2^j\Lambda_L+4)\log\left(\frac{\log(2^j\Lambda_L+4)}{\delta_{j}}\right)\right).

The number of raw state-preparation attempts is larger by a factor

\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,j-1}|^{2}\right)
due to the probabilistic projection to the code space before reshaping. Including pre-calibration, the total number of interleaved QEC rounds is
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\Lambda_{L}^{2}4^{j}\right)
.

5

(Ancilla cost)
The procedure uses
n+5\left\lceil\log_{2}\bigl(2(|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,j-1}|+1)^{2}\bigr)\right\rceil
ancillary qubits in addition to the
n
probe qubits.

6

(Classical overhead) The classical cost of the algorithm is dominated by repeated QEC and the syndrome-lift post-processing of Bell outcomes, and is bounded by

\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\bigl(n+(|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,j-1}|+1)^{2}\bigr)\Lambda_{L}^{2}4^{j}\right).

Proof.

Most of the proof is for item
(1)
, the coverage of the algorithm. We then verify the resource bounds in items
(2)
–
(4)
, including the pre-calibration overhead. Items
(5)
–
(6)
are proved at the end.

Let us begin with item
(1)
. To suppress the already known Lindbladian terms we use the physical twirling + partial QEC reshaping map from
Eq. (
66
):

A_{\tau,D}=\mathcal{R}_{j}\circ\frac{1}{4^{n}}\sum_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}\bigl(\mathcal{U}_{P}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}\bigr)\circ\bigl(e^{\mathcal{L}\tau}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}\bigr)\circ\bigl(\mathcal{U}_{P}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}\bigr)\circ\mathcal{C}_{\Pi_{j}}.

If
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,j-1}=\emptyset
, we use the trivial code:

\mathcal{G}_{j}=\emptyset
,
S_{j}=\{I_{n}\}
,
\Pi_{j}=I_{n}
,
\mathcal{R}_{j}=I
, and
n^{\prime}=n
; in this case let

E_{\mathrm{tar}}
denote the sure event. Otherwise,
\mathcal{R}_{j}
is the recovery map of the random
stabilizer code draw from
Algorithm

1
with inputs

V_{\mathrm{corr}}=\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,j-1}
,
c=2
and

M_{\mathrm{det}}=|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,j-1}|^{2}
, and
E_{\mathrm{tar}}
denotes the event that this
code corrects all Paulis in
V_{\mathrm{corr}}
. In both cases,

|S_{j}|\leq 4\bigl(|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,j-1}|+1\bigr)^{2}.

(112)

Conditioned on
E_{\mathrm{tar}}
, by

Section

C.2
and

Section

C.2
, the effective generator
of this reshaping on
\rho\in\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n^{\prime}})
is

\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}^{(j)}(\rho)=\sum_{P_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}\setminus\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,j-1}}a_{kk}\Bigl((Q_{P_{k}}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})\rho(Q_{P_{k}}^{\dagger}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})-\rho\Bigr),

(113)

where

Q_{P}\coloneqq\widetilde{R}_{r(P)}P\in\mathbb{P}_{n}

is the Pauli obtained by composing
P
with the recovery action

\widetilde{R}_{r(P)}
specified in
Eq. (
69
), with

R_{r(P)}=\widetilde{R}_{r(P)}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}
. Thus, every unknown term
P_{k}\in\mathcal{Q}_{j}\setminus\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,j-1}
contributes to the reshaped dynamics through the logical Pauli
Q_{P_{k}}
. When several physical Paulis induce the same nonidentity recovered Pauli, we aggregate their rates in the effective diagonal dissipator. Thus, the coefficient of an effective Pauli
Q
is the sum of all
a_{kk}
with
Q_{P_{k}}\propto Q
. In particular, for any target
P_{r}
with
Q_{P_{r}}\not\propto I_{n}
, the aggregate rate of
Q_{P_{r}}
is at least
a_{rr}
. Terms for which
Q_{P_{k}}\propto I_{n}
do not contribute to the effective rate
\Gamma_{j}^{\mathrm{eff}}
and are not detected through nonidentity Bell outcomes. These terms are nevertheless accounted for by the initialization

\widehat{\mathcal{Q}}_{j}\leftarrow\bigcup_{s\in\{0,1\}^{m_{j}}}\operatorname{Probe}_{j}(R_{s}).

Indeed, if
Q_{P_{k}}\propto I_{n}
, then up to stabilizers,
P_{k}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}
is equivalent to
R_{r(P_{k})}
, and therefore
P_{k}\in\operatorname{Probe}_{j}(R_{r(P_{k})})
. Thus every target Pauli reshaped into the identity coset is included in the candidate set before the Bell-sampling loop begins.

Before proceeding to candidate identification via Bell sampling, we first record the Lindbladian reshaping error. From
Section

C.4
with

\tau\coloneq t/N_{\mathrm{reshape}}
and using
\Gamma_{j}
from Eq. (
104
),

\|A_{\tau,D}^{N_{\mathrm{reshape}}}-e^{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}^{(j)}t}\circ\mathcal{C}_{\Pi_{j}}\|_{\diamond}\leq\frac{t^{2}(\|\mathcal{L}\|_{\diamond}^{2}+\|\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}^{(j)}\|_{\diamond}^{2})}{2N_{\mathrm{reshape}}}\leq\frac{t^{2}(\Lambda_{L}^{2}+4\Gamma_{j}^{2})}{2N_{\mathrm{reshape}}}\leq\frac{5t^{2}\Lambda_{L}^{2}}{2N_{\mathrm{reshape}}}.

(114)

where the last inequality follows from
\Gamma_{j}\leq\|\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{diag}}\|_{\diamond}\leq\|\mathcal{L}\|_{\diamond}
, with
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{diag}}
denoting the Pauli-twirled version of
\mathcal{L}
. Thus, going forward in the analysis, we calculate the measurement statistics as
if we had access to
e^{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}^{(j)}t}
and then account for the reshaping
error separately.

Now, suppose that both the random QEC construction (event
E_{\mathrm{tar}}
) and the
\widehat{\Gamma}_{j}
pre-calibration from
Section

D.5
have succeeded. Let
\Pi_{j}
denote the corner algebra projection of the code. We are interested in the probability to sample the logical
\{Q_{P_{k}}:a_{kk}\geq 2^{-(j+1)}\}
of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}^{(j)}
using Bell sampling. For that, we use
Section

D.2
, but first we argue why the assumptions of this lemma are satisfied in our setting. Let
\rho_{0}
denote a
2n^{\prime}
-qubit maximally entangled state
\rho_{0}\coloneq\ket{\Phi_0}\bra{\Phi_0}
where
\ket{\Phi_0}\coloneq\ket{\Phi_+}^{\otimes n^{\prime}}
and
\ket{\Phi_+}=(\ket{00}+\ket{11})/\sqrt{2}.

To prepare the state:

\widetilde{\rho}\coloneq(e^{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}^{(j)}t}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}})\left(\frac{\Pi_{j}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}}\,\rho_{0}\,\Pi_{j}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}}}{\tr(\Pi_j \otimes I_{n'} \,\rho_0)}\right).

we first do a projection of the
2n^{\prime}
-qubit maximally entangled state into the corner algebra (Eq. (
37
)). Operationally, this could be done via measuring the stabilizers and discarding if the measured syndrome is not zero. The probability of successful projection is

\tr(\Pi_j \otimes I_{n'} \,\rho_0)=\frac{1}{|S_{j}|}\geq 1/(4(|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,j-1}|+1)^{2})

(115)

where we used Eq. (
112
).
After projection, we evolve the state under
e^{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}^{(j)}t}
(using Lindbladian reshaping). The remaining assumption of
Section

D.2
is that the group generated by the
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}^{(j)}
Pauli terms has no non-identity stabilizer elements. In the trivial-code case this is immediate since
S_{j}=\{I_{n}\}
. In the nontrivial-code case, this is guaranteed by the
Z
-padding of
Algorithm

1
: each non-identity stabilizer
\sigma\in S_{j}\setminus\{I\}
acts non-trivially on the ancilla qubits, while the
n
-qubit Lindbladian terms act trivially on ancillas. Therefore, we can use
Section

D.2
. Let
P_{r}\in\mathcal{Q}_{j}\setminus\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,j-1}
with
Q_{P_{r}}\not\propto I_{n}
denote a particular Pauli we wish to identify, where
Q_{P_{r}}
is the corresponding logical Pauli from Eq. (
113
). By
Section

D.2
and using
\widehat{\Gamma}_{j}\geq\Gamma_{j}^{\mathrm{eff}}
, we have

\Pr_{\text{ideal}}(\text{sample $Q_{P_{r}}$ modulo stabilizers})\geq e^{-\Gamma_{j}^{\mathrm{eff}}t}\alpha_{rr}t\geq e^{-\widehat{\Gamma}_{j}t}2^{-(j+1)}t

accounting for the reshaping diamond norm error from Eq. (
114
) and using
t=1/\widehat{\Gamma}_{j}
,

\Pr(\text{sample $Q_{P_r}$ modulo stabilizers})\geq e^{-\widehat{\Gamma}_{j}t}2^{-(j+1)}t-\frac{5t^{2}\Lambda_{L}^{2}}{2N_{\mathrm{reshape}}}\geq\frac{1}{2e\,2^{j}\,\widehat{\Gamma}_{j}}-\frac{5\Lambda_{L}^{2}}{2\widehat{\Gamma}_{j}^{2}N_{\mathrm{reshape}}}

(116)

By setting
N_{\mathrm{reshape}}\geq 10e\cdot 2^{j}\Lambda_{L}^{2}/\widehat{\Gamma}_{j}
, we get

\Pr(\text{sample $Q_{P_r}$ modulo stabilizers})\geq\frac{1}{4e\,2^{j}\,\widehat{\Gamma}_{j}}.

(117)

We now have the necessary ingredients to guarantee the success of the algorithm by enforcing the following four events:

A

–the random code suppresses all Paulis from
V_{\mathrm{corr}}
(event
E_{\mathrm{tar}}
)
\rightarrow
this is sure in the trivial-code case (
V_{\mathrm{corr}}=\emptyset
) and succeeds with probability
\geq 1-\delta_{j}/4
otherwise by
Algorithm

1
;

B

–the precalibration
\widehat{\Gamma}_{j}
estimate satisfies Eq. (
128
)
\rightarrow
succeeds w. prob
\geq 1-\delta_{j}/4
by
Section

D.5
;

C

–all
N_{\mathrm{meas}}
experiments succeed at preparing the code-projected Bell states
\rightarrow
Eq. (
119
);

D

–each Pauli
P\in\mathcal{Q}_{j}\setminus\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,j-1}
is covered at least once (via initialization or coupon collector)
\rightarrow
Eq. (
118
).

First, let us lower bound the probability that each Pauli
P\in\mathcal{Q}_{j}\setminus\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,j-1}
is covered at least once (event
D
). Fix a single
P_{r}\in\mathcal{Q}_{j}\setminus\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,j-1}
. By Eq. (
117
), repeating Bell sampling
N_{\mathrm{meas}}\leftarrow 4e\,2^{j}\,\widehat{\Gamma}_{j}\,\log(8\widehat{\Gamma}_j2^j/\delta_j)
times guarantees:

\Pr(\text{$Q_{P_r}$ is not sampled once})=(1-\Pr(\text{sample $Q_{P_r}$}))^{N_{\mathrm{meas}}}\leq\frac{\delta_{j}}{8\widehat{\Gamma}_{j}2^{j}}

To recover the probe-only
P_{r}\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
from the sampled
Q_{P_{r}}
, for every sampled logical
Q\in\mathbb{P}_{n}
, we compose
Q
with all possible recovery operators
R_{s}
of
\mathcal{R}_{j}
and add their probe representations
\bigcup_{s\in\{0,1\}^{m_{j}}}\operatorname{Probe}_{j}(R_{s}Q)
to the candidate set. For every sampled logical, this introduces a candidate-size overhead of
|S_{j}|\leq 4(|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,j-1}|+1)^{2}
. Using a union bound over elements of
\mathcal{Q}_{j}\setminus\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,j-1}
that induce nonidentity recovered Paulis, of which there are at most
\widehat{\Gamma}_{j}/2^{-(j+1)}
, we get

\Pr[\text{$\geq 1$ Pauli from $\mathcal{Q}_{j}\setminus\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,j-1}$ not covered}]\leq\frac{\widehat{\Gamma}_{j}}{2^{-(j+1)}}\Pr(\text{given Pauli not sampled once})\leq\frac{\delta_{j}}{4}

Hence,

\Pr(D|A\wedge B \wedge C)=1-\Pr[\text{$\geq 1$ Pauli from $\mathcal{Q}_{j}\setminus\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,j-1}$ not covered}]\geq 1-\delta_{j}/4

(118)

Combining the identity-coset initialization with the Bell sampling outcomes, the total candidate size
|\widehat{\mathcal{Q}}_{j}|
is upper bounded by
(N_{\mathrm{meas}}+1)|S_{j}|
and hence:

|\widehat{\mathcal{Q}}_{j}|\leq(N_{\mathrm{meas}}+1)|S_{j}|=\mathcal{O}\left(2^{j}\widehat{\Gamma}_{j}(|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,j-1}|+1)^{2}\log(2^j\widehat{\Gamma}_j/\delta_j)\right)

The event
A
is guaranteed to succeed with probability at least
1-\delta_{j}/4
by running the random code construction
Algorithm

1
with probability of failure
\delta_{j}/4
. Similarly, the event
B
is guaranteed to succeed with probability at least
1-\delta_{j}/4
by running the pre-calibration from
Section

D.5
and
Section

D.5
with probability of failure
\delta_{j}/4
.

To guarantee that the code-space-projected state preparation succeeds for each of the
N_{\mathrm{meas}}
experiments (event
C
), we distinguish the trivial and nontrivial code cases. If
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,j-1}=\emptyset
, then
\Pi_{j}=I_{n}
and event
C
is sure. Otherwise, we attempt the preparation + projection
N_{\mathrm{prep}}=4|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,j-1}|^{2}\ln(4N_{\mathrm{meas}}/\delta_j)
times per experiment. Then, using a union bound over experiments and Eq. (
115
), we get

\Pr[\neg C]\leq N_{\mathrm{meas}}\left(1-\frac{1}{4|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,j-1}|^{2}}\right)^{N_{\mathrm{prep}}}\leq\frac{\delta_{j}}{4}.

(119)

Combining the
\delta_{j}/4
failure probabilities of events
A
and
B
with Eq. (
119
) and Eq. (
118
), we get:

\Pr[\text{success}]\geq\Pr(A \wedge B \wedge C\wedge D)\geq 1-\Pr(\neg A)-\Pr(\neg B)-\Pr(\neg C)-\Pr(\neg D|A \wedge B\wedge C)\geq 1-4\,\frac{\delta_{j}}{4}=1-\delta_{j}

where we used the conditional probability and union bound. This concludes the proof of item
(1)
.

We next prove item
(2)
. In the Bell-sampling stage, each experiment uses interrogation time
t_{j}=1/\widehat{\Gamma}_{j}
, and the number of Bell-sampling experiments is
N_{\mathrm{meas}}=\mathcal{O}\left(2^{j}\widehat{\Gamma}_{j}\log\left(2^{j}\widehat{\Gamma}_{j}/\delta_{j}\right)\right)
. On the pre-calibration success event,
\widehat{\Gamma}_{j}=\mathcal{O}\left(\Gamma_{j}+2^{-(j+1)}\right)
, and hence
2^{j}\widehat{\Gamma}_{j}=\mathcal{O}\left(2^{j}\Gamma_{j}+1\right)
. Therefore the Bell-sampling stage uses total evolution time

N_{\mathrm{meas}}t_{j}=\mathcal{O}\left(2^{j}\log\left(\frac{2^{j}\Gamma_{j}+1}{\delta_{j}}\right)\right).

The pre-calibration is invoked with
\gamma_{\min}=2^{-(j+1)}
and
\Lambda_{D}\leq\max\{\Lambda_{L},2^{-(j+1)}\}
, so by
Section

D.5
it contributes

\mathcal{O}\left(2^{j}\log\left(\frac{\log(2^j\Lambda_L+4)}{\delta_{j}}\right)\right)

additional black-box evolution time. This proves item
(2)
.

For item
(3)
, the Bell-sampling stage applies
e^{\tau\mathcal{L}}
with
\tau=t_{j}/N_{\mathrm{reshape}}=\Omega\left(1/(\Lambda_{L}^{2}2^{j})\right)
.
By
Section

D.5
, the pre-calibration stage has the same resolution scale. Thus item
(3)
follows.

For item
(4)
, the Bell-sampling stage uses

N_{\mathrm{meas}}=\mathcal{O}\left((2^{j}\Gamma_{j}+1)\log\left(\frac{2^{j}\Gamma_{j}+1}{\delta_{j}}\right)\right)

experiments on the pre-calibration success event. By
Section

D.5
, pre-calibration contributes

\mathcal{O}\left(\log(2^j\Lambda_L+4)\log\left(\frac{\log(2^j\Lambda_L+4)}{\delta_{j}}\right)\right)

additional Bell-sampling experiments. The raw state-preparation attempts are larger by the stated projection overhead. Finally, the Bell-sampling stage uses
N_{\mathrm{meas}}N_{\mathrm{reshape}}=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\Lambda_{L}^{2}4^{j}\right)

interleaved QEC rounds, with the pre-calibration stage contributing the same order. This proves item
(4)
.

We now prove item
(5)
. In the trivial-code case, no QEC ancillas are needed. In the nontrivial-code case, by
Theorem

C.14
with
c=2
and
M_{\mathrm{det}}=|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,j-1}|^{2}
, the QEC construction uses at most
5/2\lceil\log_{2}(2|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,j-1}|^{2})\rceil
ancillas for both stabilizers and syndrome extraction. Forming Bell pairs uses at most extra
n+5/2\lceil\log_{2}(2(|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,j-1}|+1)^{2})\rceil
ancillas. Thus, uniformly, the total number is at most

n+5\left\lceil\log_{2}\bigl(2(|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,j-1}|+1)^{2}\bigr)\right\rceil.

The classical overhead (item
(6)
) is dominated by repeated syndrome extraction + correction and is equal to:

\text{Classical cost}=\mathcal{O}\left((|S_{j}|+n)N_{\mathrm{meas}}N_{\mathrm{reshape}}\right)=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\bigl(n+(|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,j-1}|+1)^{2}\bigr)\Lambda_{L}^{2}4^{j}\right).

This concludes the proof of the theorem.
∎

D.3
Candidate testing via syndrome measurements

After identifying a superset
\widehat{\mathcal{Q}}_{j}\supseteq\mathcal{Q}_{j}\setminus\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,j-1}
, the next step is to eliminate false positives in order to construct
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,j}\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{D,\eta/2}
. In this section, we present
Algorithm

3
, which performs batched tolerant thresholding over all candidates in
\widehat{\mathcal{Q}}_{j}
, given an upper bound on the total diagonal decay rate

\Gamma_{\mathrm{tot}}\coloneq\sum_{P_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}}a_{kk}.

(120)

Problem 1

(Batched tolerant testing of diagonal dissipator rates)
.

Fix a candidate set
\mathcal{Z}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{n}\setminus\{I\}
and parameters
\eta>0
and
\delta\in(0,1)
. Given black-box access to short-time channels
e^{t\mathcal{L}}
, design a procedure that outputs a subset
\mathcal{Z}_{\mathrm{acc}}\subseteq\mathcal{Z}
such that, with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\{P_{r}\in\mathcal{Z}:a_{rr}\geq\eta\}\subseteq\mathcal{Z}_{\mathrm{acc}}\subseteq\{P_{r}\in\mathcal{Z}:a_{rr}>\eta/2\}.

For candidates with
a_{rr}\in(\eta/2,\eta)
, the procedure may either accept or reject.

At a high level, our approach uses stabilizer QEC to convert Pauli jumps into syndrome events. For a candidate Pauli
P_{r}
, the syndrome frequency of
r(P_{r})
estimates the total diagonal dissipative rate of all Paulis that share this syndrome. Instead of requiring
P_{r}
to have a unique syndrome among all nonzero dissipator terms, we draw random stabilizer codes with enough checks so that, with high probability, every candidate in
\widehat{\mathcal{Q}}_{j}
has colliding syndrome mass small compared to
\eta
. For each code, we run repeated QEC cycles and test all candidates in parallel from the resulting syndrome histogram. This yields a batched tolerant test that accepts all candidates with
a_{rr}\geq\eta
and rejects all candidates with
a_{rr}\leq\eta/2
with high probability.

Since our goal is to learn from the syndrome measurements, we first simplify the dynamics by resorting to the physical twirling map
46
:

\widetilde{A}_{\tau}\coloneq\frac{1}{4^{n}}\sum_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}\bigl(\mathcal{U}_{P}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}\bigr)\circ\bigl(e^{\mathcal{L}\tau}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}\bigr)\circ\bigl(\mathcal{U}_{P}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}\bigr).

(121)

Crucially, the effective generator of
\widetilde{A}_{\tau}
only retains the diagonal dissipator terms:

\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{diag}}(X)\coloneq\frac{1}{4^{n}}\sum_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}\bigl(\mathcal{U}_{P}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}\bigr)\circ\bigl(\mathcal{L}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}\bigr)\circ\bigl(\mathcal{U}_{P}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}\bigr)(X)=\sum_{P_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}}a_{kk}\Bigl(P_{k}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}XP_{k}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}-X\Bigr)

(122)

Lemma D.6

(One-step twirling error)
.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be an
n
-qubit GKSL generator as in Eq. (
100
) with
\|\mathcal{L}\|_{\diamond}\leq\Lambda_{L}
and
\Gamma_{\mathrm{tot}}\coloneq\sum_{P_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}}a_{kk}.
Let
\widetilde{A}_{\tau}:\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n^{\prime}})\to\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n^{\prime}})
be the physical twirling map from Eq. (
121
), and let
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{diag}}
be the corresponding effective generator from Eq. (
122
). Then

\|\widetilde{A}_{\tau}-e^{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{diag}}\tau}\|_{\diamond}\leq\frac{(\|\mathcal{L}\|_{\diamond}^{2}+\|\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{diag}}\|_{\diamond}^{2})\tau^{2}}{2}\leq\frac{(\Lambda_{L}^{2}+4\Gamma_{\mathrm{tot}}^{2})\tau^{2}}{2}\leq\frac{5\Lambda_{L}^{2}\tau^{2}}{2}.

(123)

Proof.

The proof of the first inequality is identical to
Section

C.4
. For the second inequality, note that
\|\mathcal{L}\|_{\diamond}\leq\Lambda_{L}
by assumption. Moreover,
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{diag}}
is a diagonal Pauli dissipator with total decay rate
\Gamma_{\mathrm{tot}}
, and hence

\|\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{diag}}\|_{\diamond}\leq\sum_{P_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}}a_{kk}\,\|\mathcal{U}_{P_{k}}-I\|_{\diamond}\leq 2\Gamma_{\mathrm{tot}}.

Finally,
\Gamma_{\mathrm{tot}}\leq\|\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{diag}}\|_{\diamond}\leq\|\mathcal{L}\|_{\diamond}\leq\Lambda_{L}
, where the first inequality is the standard Pauli-diagonal lower bound and the second follows from contractivity of physical Pauli twirling in diamond norm. This gives the last inequality in Eq. (
123
).
∎

Next, we relate the
diagonal rate

a_{rr}
of the simplified dynamics to an observable syndrome statistic.
Section

D.3
relates the target syndrome probability to the sum of all diagonal rates sharing that syndrome.

Lemma D.7

(Syndrome hit probability for a diagonal Pauli jump)
.

Assume the Lindbladian
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{diag}}
has the diagonal Pauli-dissipator form
as in (
122
) with dissipator structure
\mathcal{S}_{D}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{n}
. Let
\{G_{\ell}\}_{\ell=1}^{m}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{n^{\prime}}
be stabilizer generators with syndrome projectors
\{\Pi_{s}\}_{s\in\{0,1\}^{m}}
as in (
41
). Fix a non-identity
target Pauli
P_{r}
and assume that its syndrome is nonzero, i.e.
r(P_{r})\neq\mathbf{0}
. Let
\rho=\Pi_{0}\rho\Pi_{0}\in\mathfrak{A}_{c}\subseteq\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n^{\prime}})
be any code state. Then for any
\tau\geq 0
,

p_{r}(\tau)\coloneq\Pr\bigl[\text{measuring syndrome }r(P_{r})\bigr]\;=\;\tr\bigl(\Pi_{r(P_{r})}\,e^{\tau\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{diag}}}(\rho)\bigr)\;=\;\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r(P_{k})=r(P_{r})\\
P_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}\end{subarray}}a_{kk}\,\tau\;+\;\Delta_{r}(\tau),

where the remainder satisfies
\absolutevalue{\Delta_r(\tau)}\ \leq\ \frac{1}{2}\,\|\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{diag}}\|_{\diamond}^{2}\,\tau^{2}.

Proof.

Use
Section

B.2
to expand
e^{\tau\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{diag}}}=I+\tau\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{diag}}+\int_{0}^{\tau}(\tau-s)\,\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{diag}}^{2}e^{s\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{diag}}}\,ds
and study the resulting terms. See Appendix
D.6
for the full proof.
∎

Corollary D.8

(Syndrome hit probability for twirled evolution)
.

Combining
Section

D.3
and
Section

D.3
and using Hölder’s inequality, we get that for any
\rho\in\mathfrak{A}_{c}
and
\tau\geq 0
:

\Pr\bigl[\text{measuring syndrome }r(P_{r})\bigr]=\tr(\Pi_{r(P_r)} \widetilde{A}_\tau(\rho))=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r(P_{k})=r(P_{r})\\
P_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}\end{subarray}}a_{kk}\,\tau+\widetilde{\Delta}(\tau),

where
|\widetilde{\Delta}(\tau)|\leq\frac{1}{2}\|\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{diag}}\|_{\diamond}^{2}\tau^{2}+\frac{1}{2}(\Lambda_{L}^{2}+4\Gamma_{\mathrm{tot}}^{2})\tau^{2}\leq 5\Lambda_{L}^{2}\tau^{2}
. Here we used
\|\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{diag}}\|_{\diamond}\leq 2\Gamma_{\mathrm{tot}}
and
\Gamma_{\mathrm{tot}}\leq\Lambda_{L}
.

To control the contribution of non-target dissipator terms to the syndrome statistic in
Section

D.3
, we bound the total diagonal rate assigned to terms with the same syndrome as a candidate Pauli. The following lemma shows that a random stabilizer code makes this colliding rate small simultaneously for all candidates in a finite set.

Lemma D.9

(Small syndrome-collision mass for random checks)
.

Let
\mathcal{Z}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{n}\setminus\{I\}
be a finite candidate set, and let

\Gamma_{\mathrm{tot}}\coloneq\sum_{P_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}}a_{kk}
. Let
\mathcal{T}=\{P^{(t)}\}_{t=1}^{m}
be sampled i.i.d. uniformly from
\mathcal{P}_{n}
, and let
r(\cdot)\in\{0,1\}^{m}
be the syndrome map with
[r(Q)]_{t}=1
iff
\{Q,P^{(t)}\}=0
. For each candidate
P_{r}\in\mathcal{Z}
, define its colliding syndrome mass

W_{r}\coloneq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}\setminus\{P_{r}\}\\
r(P_{k})=r(P_{r})\end{subarray}}a_{kk}.

Then, for any
\xi>0
,

\Pr_{\mathcal{T}}\!\left[\exists P_{r}\in\mathcal{Z}:r(P_{r})=0\text{ or }W_{r}>\xi\right]\leq|\mathcal{Z}|2^{-m}+\frac{|\mathcal{Z}|\Gamma_{\mathrm{tot}}}{\xi\,2^{m}}.

In particular, if
m=\left\lceil\log_{2}\left(\frac{32|\mathcal{Z}|(\Gamma_{\mathrm{tot}}/\eta+1)}{\delta}\right)\right\rceil
, then with probability at least
1-\delta
, every
P_{r}\in\mathcal{Z}
has nonzero syndrome and colliding syndrome mass
W_{r}\leq\eta/16
.

Proof.

Fix
P_{r}\in\mathcal{Z}
. Since
P_{r}\neq I
, a uniformly random check commutes with
P_{r}
with probability
1/2
. Hence

\Pr_{\mathcal{T}}[r(P_{r})=0]=2^{-m}.

Next fix
P_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}\setminus\{P_{r}\}
. Since
P_{k}P_{r}
is nonidentity, a uniformly random check commutes with
P_{k}P_{r}
with probability
1/2
. Equivalently, for a single random check,
P_{k}
and
P_{r}
have the same syndrome bit with probability
1/2
. Since the
m
checks are independent,

\Pr_{\mathcal{T}}\bigl[r(P_{k})=r(P_{r})\bigr]=2^{-m}.

Therefore,

\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{T}}W_{r}=\sum_{P_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}\setminus\{P_{r}\}}a_{kk}\Pr_{\mathcal{T}}\bigl[r(P_{k})=r(P_{r})\bigr]\leq\Gamma_{\mathrm{tot}}2^{-m}.

By Markov’s inequality,

\Pr_{\mathcal{T}}[W_{r}>\xi]\leq\frac{\Gamma_{\mathrm{tot}}}{\xi\,2^{m}}.

Taking a union bound over
P_{r}\in\mathcal{Z}
proves the first claim. The second claim follows directly from setting
\xi=\eta/16
and the chosen value of
m
.
∎

For our final ingredient, we use a standard result from classical hypothesis-testing:

Lemma D.10

(Threshold test for adapted Bernoulli trials)
.

Let
X_{1},\dots,X_{N}\in\{0,1\}
be Bernoulli random variables, not necessarily independent, and let

\widehat{p}\coloneq\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\ell=1}^{N}X_{\ell}
.
Let
\epsilon\in(0,1]
and
\delta\in(0,1)
. Then there exists a universal constant

C_{\mathrm{mg}}>0
such that, if

N\geq C_{\mathrm{mg}}\,\epsilon^{-1}\log(1/\delta)
, then:

(i)

if
\mathbb{E}[X_{\ell}\mid X_{1},\dots,X_{\ell-1}]\leq\frac{5}{8}\epsilon
for every
\ell\in[N]
, then

\Pr\left(\widehat{p}>\frac{3}{4}\epsilon\right)\leq\delta
;

(ii)

if
\mathbb{E}[X_{\ell}\mid X_{1},\dots,X_{\ell-1}]\geq\frac{7}{8}\epsilon
for every
\ell\in[N]
, then

\Pr\left(\widehat{p}\leq\frac{3}{4}\epsilon\right)\leq\delta
.

Proof.

The proof uses the multiplicative Azuma’s inequality
55
;
86
(martingale version of the multiplicative Chernoff bound). An admissible value for the universal constant is
C_{\mathrm{mg}}=112
. See Appendix
D.6
for details.
∎

Inputs :

[label=(0), leftmargin=*, nosep]

1.

Nonempty set of candidate terms
\widehat{\mathcal{Q}}_{j}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{n}\setminus\{I\}
.

2.

Resolution parameter
\eta\in(0,1)
.

3.

Confidence parameter
\delta_{j}\in(0,1)
.

4.

Upper bound
\Lambda_{L}\geq\|\mathcal{L}\|_{\diamond}
.

5.

Upper bound
\widehat{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{tot}}\geq\Gamma_{\mathrm{tot}}
; one may take
\widehat{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{tot}}=\Lambda_{L}
.

6.

Access to
n
-qubit evolution
e^{t\mathcal{L}}
.

7.

Access to
\mathcal{O}\left(\log\left(|\widehat{\mathcal{Q}}_{j}|(\widehat{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{tot}}/\eta+1)\right)\right)
noiseless ancilla qubits.

Output :
Set
\widehat{\mathcal{Q}}_{j,\mathrm{acc}}
that, with probability at least
1-\delta_{j}
, solves
1
, i.e.

\{P_{r}\in\widehat{\mathcal{Q}}_{j}:a_{rr}\geq\eta\}\subseteq\widehat{\mathcal{Q}}_{j,\mathrm{acc}}\subseteq\{P_{r}\in\widehat{\mathcal{Q}}_{j}:a_{rr}>\eta/2\}

Schedule

m\leftarrow\left\lceil{\log_{2}\bigl(64|\widehat{\mathcal{Q}}_{j}|(\widehat{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{tot}}/\eta+1)\bigr)}\right\rceil

//
# of random checks

N_{\mathrm{codes}}\leftarrow\lceil\log_{2}(2/\delta_{j})\rceil

//
# of random QEC draws

\tau\leftarrow\eta/(80\Lambda_{L}^{2})

//
single step evolution time

N_{\mathrm{QEC}}\leftarrow\left\lceil\frac{112}{\eta\tau}\ln\left(\frac{2N_{\mathrm{codes}}|\widehat{\mathcal{Q}}_{j}|}{\delta_{j}}\right)\right\rceil

//
# of syndrome measurements per code

N_{\mathrm{ancillas}}\leftarrow 3m/2

//
m/2
- abelianity,
m
- syndrome meas.

foreach

P_{k}\in\widehat{\mathcal{Q}}_{j}

do

\widehat{p}_{k}\leftarrow 1

//
to track minimum empirical syndrome rate across codes

for

i\leftarrow 1

to

N_{\mathrm{codes}}

do

\mathcal{G}_{i}\leftarrow\{\,m\text{ random $n$-qubit Pauli strings}\,\}

//
baseline generators

\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{i}\leftarrow
Do ZX padding to ensure abelianity

//
n^{\prime}\leftarrow n+\lceil m/2\rceil

Construct an arbitrary recovery channel
\mathcal{R}_{i}

Apply
\mathcal{R}_{i}
to prepare some logical state

foreach

P_{k}\in\widehat{\mathcal{Q}}_{j}

do

Compute the syndrome
r_{i}(P_{k})

\mathrm{cnt}_{i}(P_{k})\leftarrow 0

//
initialize empirical counter to zero for each Pauli

for

\ell\leftarrow 1

to

N_{\mathrm{QEC}}

do

Randomly sample
P\sim\mathcal{P}_{n}

//
for physical Pauli twirling

Apply
P\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}

Evolve the system by
e^{\tau\mathcal{L}}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}

Apply
P\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}

Measure the syndrome
s_{\ell}
and apply the recovery channel
\mathcal{R}_{i}

foreach

P_{k}\in\widehat{\mathcal{Q}}_{j}
with
r_{i}(P_{k})=s_{\ell}\neq 0

do

\mathrm{cnt}_{i}(P_{k})\leftarrow\mathrm{cnt}_{i}(P_{k})+1

//
record syndrome outcomes

foreach

P_{k}\in\widehat{\mathcal{Q}}_{j}

do

if

r_{i}(P_{k})\neq 0

then

\widehat{p}_{k}\leftarrow\min\left(\widehat{p}_{k},\mathrm{cnt}_{i}(P_{k})/N_{\mathrm{QEC}}\right)

\widehat{\mathcal{Q}}_{j,\mathrm{acc}}\leftarrow\emptyset

foreach

P_{k}\in\widehat{\mathcal{Q}}_{j}

do

if

\widehat{p}_{k}>3\eta\tau/4

then

\widehat{\mathcal{Q}}_{j,\mathrm{acc}}\leftarrow\widehat{\mathcal{Q}}_{j,\mathrm{acc}}\cup\{P_{k}\}

return

\widehat{\mathcal{Q}}_{j,\mathrm{acc}}

Algorithm 3

Batched dissipator candidates testing subroutine,
j
th round

Crucially, the sequential syndrome outcomes can be statistically dependent, because the recovery channel may alter the code state between rounds. For this reason, we use the martingale-based concentration result in
Section

D.3
instead of an independence-based tail bound.

Theorem D.11

(Batched dissipator rate testing)
.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be an
n
-qubit Lindbladian as in Eq. (
100
) with
\|\mathcal{L}\|_{\diamond}\leq\Lambda_{L}
. Let
\widehat{\mathcal{Q}}_{j}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{n}\setminus\{I\}
be a nonempty finite candidate set, and let
\eta,\delta_{j}\in(0,1)
. Let
\widehat{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{tot}}\geq\Gamma_{\mathrm{tot}}
, where
\Gamma_{\mathrm{tot}}
is the total decay rate defined in Eq. (
120
). Assume black-box access to
e^{t\mathcal{L}}
.

Then running
Algorithm

3
with schedule

m=\mathcal{O}\left(\log\left(|\widehat{\mathcal{Q}}_{j}|(\widehat{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{tot}}/\eta+1)\right)\right),\;\;\tau=\Theta(\eta/\Lambda_{L}^{2}),\;\;N_{\mathrm{codes}}=\mathcal{O}\left(\log(1/\delta_j)\right),\;\;N_{\mathrm{QEC}}=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\Lambda_{L}^{2}}{\eta^{2}}\log\left(\frac{N_{\mathrm{codes}}|\widehat{\mathcal{Q}}_{j}|}{\delta_{j}}\right)\right)

outputs
\widehat{\mathcal{Q}}_{j,\mathrm{acc}}
and satisfies:

1

(Correctness) With probability
\geq 1-\delta_{j}
, the algorithm solves
1
. Concretely, the output
\widehat{\mathcal{Q}}_{j,\mathrm{acc}}
satisfies:

\{P_{r}\in\widehat{\mathcal{Q}}_{j}:a_{rr}\geq\eta\}\subseteq\widehat{\mathcal{Q}}_{j,\mathrm{acc}}\subseteq\{P_{r}\in\widehat{\mathcal{Q}}_{j}:a_{rr}>\eta/2\}.

2

(Total evolution time)
The total evolution time under
e^{t\mathcal{L}}
is

t_{\mathrm{tot}}=N_{\mathrm{codes}}N_{\mathrm{QEC}}\tau=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\eta}\log\left(\frac{1}{\delta_{j}}\right)\log\left(\frac{|\widehat{\mathcal{Q}}_{j}|\log(1/\delta_j)}{\delta_{j}}\right)\right).

3

(Time resolution)
It only ever applies
e^{\mathcal{L}t}
for
t\geq t_{\mathrm{res}}
, where

t_{\mathrm{res}}\coloneq\tau=\Theta(\eta/\Lambda_{L}^{2}).

4

(Number of experiments)
The number of random-code experiments is

N_{\exp}\coloneq N_{\mathrm{codes}}=\mathcal{O}\left(\log(1/\delta_j)\right).

The number of interleaved QEC rounds, or syndrome extractions, is larger by a factor of
N_{\mathrm{QEC}}=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\Lambda_{L}^{2}/\eta^{2}\right)

5

(Ancilla cost)
The procedure uses at most
3/2\left\lceil\log_{2}\bigl(64|\widehat{\mathcal{Q}}_{j}|(\widehat{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{tot}}/\eta+1)\bigr)\right\rceil

ancillary qubits for QEC.

6

(Classical overhead)
The classical cost of the algorithm is dominated by repeated QEC rounds and is bounded by

\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\bigl(n+|\widehat{\mathcal{Q}}_{j}|(\widehat{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{tot}}/\eta+1)\bigr)\frac{\Lambda_{L}^{2}}{\eta^{2}}\right).

Proof.

We first prove the correctness claim in item
(1)
under the stated scheduling. Items
(2)
–
(4)
then follow directly from the schedule, and items
(5)
–
(6)
are briefly proved at the end.

Let
\mathcal{Z}\coloneq\widehat{\mathcal{Q}}_{j}
. We use the convention that
a_{kk}=0
for
P_{k}\notin\mathcal{S}_{D}
.

Algorithm

3
draws random checks to form random stabilizer codes. The abelianity is ensured by the
ZX
-padding from
Section

C.3
, which lifts the random checks to a valid stabilizer code without changing the commutation pattern with Paulis on the
n
probe qubits. Hence the syndrome-collision guarantees from
Section

D.3
apply directly to the generated code.

Call a code good if every
P_{k}\in\mathcal{Z}
has nonzero syndrome and colliding syndrome mass at most
\eta/16
. By
Section

D.3
with
\xi=\eta/16
and the chosen value of
m
, a single random code is good with probability at least
1/2
. Therefore, with

N_{\mathrm{codes}}=\lceil\log_{2}(2/\delta_{j})\rceil
, the probability that none of the sampled codes is good is at most

\Pr(\text{no sampled code is good})\leq(1/2)^{N_{\mathrm{codes}}}\leq\delta_{j}/2.

(124)

We next analyze syndrome-hit probabilities for a fixed code. By
Section

D.3
, for any candidate
P_{k}
with nonzero syndrome,

\Pr(\text{measure }r(P_k))=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r(P_{\ell})=r(P_{k})\\
P_{\ell}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}\end{subarray}}a_{\ell\ell}\tau+\widetilde{\Delta}(\tau),\qquad|\widetilde{\Delta}(\tau)|\leq 5\Lambda_{L}^{2}\tau^{2}.

Since
\tau=\eta/(80\Lambda_{L}^{2})
, the error term obeys
|\widetilde{\Delta}(\tau)|\leq\eta\tau/16.

If
a_{kk}\leq\eta/2
and the code is good, then the colliding syndrome mass for
P_{k}
is at most
\eta/16
, so

\Pr(\text{measure }r(P_k))\leq\left(\frac{\eta}{2}+\frac{\eta}{16}\right)\tau+\frac{\eta\tau}{16}=\frac{5}{8}\eta\tau.

On the other hand, if
a_{kk}\geq\eta
, then for every code with
r(P_{k})\neq 0
,

\Pr(\text{measure }r(P_k))\geq\eta\tau-\frac{\eta\tau}{16}>\frac{7}{8}\eta\tau.

We now apply the martingale threshold test from
Section

D.3
with
\epsilon\coloneq\eta\tau
. For each code
i
and candidate
P_{k}
with
r_{i}(P_{k})\neq 0
, let
X_{1},\dots,X_{N_{\mathrm{QEC}}}
be the indicators of observing syndrome
r_{i}(P_{k})
in the sequential syndrome measurements. The conditional expectations of these adapted Bernoulli variables satisfy the upper or lower bounds above, because
Section

D.3
holds for any code state. With

N_{\mathrm{QEC}}=\left\lceil\frac{112}{\eta\tau}\ln\left(\frac{2N_{\mathrm{codes}}|\mathcal{Z}|}{\delta_{j}}\right)\right\rceil,

each such empirical syndrome frequency fails its threshold test with probability at most
\delta_{j}/(2N_{\mathrm{codes}}|\mathcal{Z}|)
. Taking a union bound over all codes and all candidates, all empirical tests used by the algorithm are simultaneously correct with probability at least
1-\delta_{j}/2
.

Condition on the event that at least one sampled code is good and all empirical threshold tests are correct. If
a_{kk}\leq\eta/2
, then for a good code the empirical syndrome frequency of
P_{k}
is at most
3\eta\tau/4
, and therefore the minimum over codes satisfies
\widehat{p}_{k}\leq 3\eta\tau/4
. Hence
P_{k}
is rejected. If
a_{kk}\geq\eta
, then every code with
r_{i}(P_{k})\neq 0
has empirical syndrome frequency greater than
3\eta\tau/4
; codes with zero syndrome are ignored by the algorithm. Thus
\widehat{p}_{k}>3\eta\tau/4
, and
P_{k}
is accepted. Combining the two failure probabilities gives total failure probability at most
\delta_{j}
, proving item
(1)
.

We next prove the ancilla cost in item
(5)
. Since the algorithm draws
m=\left\lceil\log_{2}\bigl(64|\widehat{\mathcal{Q}}_{j}|(\widehat{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{tot}}/\eta+1)\bigr)\right\rceil

random checks per code,
Section

C.3
uses at most
\lceil m/2\rceil
ancillas for abelianity, and we reserve
m
ancillas for syndrome extraction. Thus the total number of ancillas is at most
3m/2
, as claimed.

Finally, the classical overhead in item
(6)
is dominated by repeated QEC rounds. Since the stabilizer group has size
2^{m}=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(|\widehat{\mathcal{Q}}_{j}|(\widehat{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{tot}}/\eta+1)\right)
, the total classical cost is bounded by

\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\bigl(n+|\widehat{\mathcal{Q}}_{j}|(\widehat{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{tot}}/\eta+1)\bigr)N_{\mathrm{codes}}N_{\mathrm{QEC}}\right)=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\bigl(n+|\widehat{\mathcal{Q}}_{j}|(\widehat{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{tot}}/\eta+1)\bigr)\frac{\Lambda_{L}^{2}}{\eta^{2}}\right).

This concludes the proof of the theorem.
∎

D.4
Full hierarchical learning algorithm

We now combine candidate sampling
Algorithm

2
and candidate testing
Algorithm

3
into the full dissipator structure learning algorithm:

Inputs :

[label=(0), leftmargin=*, nosep]

1.

Resolution parameter
\eta\in(0,1)
.

2.

Confidence parameter
\delta_{d}\in(0,1)
.

3.

Upper bound
\Lambda_{L}\geq\|\mathcal{L}\|_{\diamond}
.

4.

Access to
n
-qubit evolution
e^{t\mathcal{L}}
, where the total decay rate of
\mathcal{L}
is
\Gamma_{\mathrm{tot}}
from Eq. (
120
).

5.

Access to
n+\mathcal{O}\left(\log(\Gamma_\mathrm{tot}/\eta+1)\right)
noiseless ancilla qubits.

Output :
Estimator
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,\eta}
of the
\eta
-heavy part of the dissipator structure
\mathcal{S}_{D,\eta}
(Eq. (
101
)) such that
\mathcal{S}_{D,\eta}\subseteq\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,\eta}\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{D,\eta/2}
with probability at least
1-\delta_{d}
.

Schedule

N_{\mathrm{rounds}}\leftarrow\lceil\log_{2}(1/\eta)\rceil

//
# of hierarchical rounds

\delta_{\mathrm{round}}\leftarrow\delta_{d}/(N_{\mathrm{rounds}}+1)

//
failure probability per hierarchical round

\widehat{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{tot}}\leftarrow\texttt{EstimateGamma}(\max\{\Lambda_{L},\eta/2\},\eta/2,\delta_{\mathrm{round}},\text{ Pauli twirled }e^{\mathcal{L}t})

//
estimate from
Section

D.5

\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,\eta}\leftarrow\emptyset

//
Initialize the running dissipator structure estimator.

for

j\leftarrow 0

to

N_{\mathrm{rounds}}-1

do

\widehat{\mathcal{Q}}_{j}\leftarrow\texttt{Diss\_Struct\_Sampling}(j,\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,\eta},\Lambda_{L},\delta_{\mathrm{round}}/2,e^{\mathcal{L}t})

//
superset estimator of
\mathcal{Q}_{j}\setminus\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,\eta}

if

\widehat{\mathcal{Q}}_{j}\neq\emptyset

then

\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,\eta}\leftarrow\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,\eta}\cup\texttt{Diss\_Struct\_Testing}(\widehat{\mathcal{Q}}_{j},\eta,\delta_{\mathrm{round}}/2,\Lambda_{L},\widehat{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{tot}},e^{\mathcal{L}t})

//
update structure estimator

return

\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,\eta}

Algorithm 4

Dissipator structure learning algorithm

Let
\mathcal{L}
be an
n
-qubit Lindbladian as in Eq. (
100
) with
\|\mathcal{L}\|_{\diamond}\leq\Lambda_{L}
. Fix
\eta,\delta_{d}\in(0,1)
and assume black-box access to
e^{t\mathcal{L}}
. Let
\mathcal{S}_{D,\eta}\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{D}
denote the
\eta
-heavy part of the dissipator structure defined in Eq. (
101
). Let
\Gamma_{\mathrm{tot}}
denote the total Lindbladian decay rate defined in Eq. (
120
). Then running
Algorithm

4
returns an estimator
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,\eta}
of
\mathcal{S}_{D,\eta}
, satisfying:

1.

(Accuracy) With probability
\geq 1-\delta_{d}
, the output estimator obeys

\mathcal{S}_{D,\eta}\subseteq\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,\eta}\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{D,\eta/2}
.

2.

(Evolution time) It applies
e^{\mathcal{L}t}
for a total evolution time

t_{\mathrm{tot}}=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{1}{\eta}\right).

3.

(Time resolution) It only ever applies
e^{\mathcal{L}t}
for
t\geq t_{\mathrm{res}}
, where

t_{\mathrm{res}}=\Omega\Bigl(\frac{\eta}{\Lambda_{L}^{2}}\Bigr).

4.

(Number of experiments) The total number of experiments is

N_{\exp}\;=\;\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{\Gamma_{\mathrm{tot}}}{\eta}+1\right).

with the total number of interleaved QEC rounds bounded by
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\Lambda_{L}^{2}/\eta^{2}\right)
.

5.

(Ancilla cost)
The number of ancillary qubits used by the procedure (dominated by Bell sampling + QEC) is at most

n+\mathcal{O}\left(\log(\frac{\Gamma_{\mathrm{tot}}}{\eta}+1)\right)

6.

(Classical overhead) The classical cost of the algorithm (dominated by repeated QEC in structure testing) is:

\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\bigl(n+(\Gamma_{\mathrm{tot}}/\eta)^{4}\bigr)\Lambda_{L}^{2}/\eta^{2}\right)

Proof.

Let
N_{\mathrm{rounds}}\coloneq\lceil\log_{2}(1/\eta)\rceil
and

\delta_{\mathrm{round}}\coloneq\delta_{d}/(N_{\mathrm{rounds}}+1)
. Let

\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,j}
denote the value of
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,\eta}
after round
j
, with
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,-1}\coloneq\emptyset
. First condition on the success of the initial call to
EstimateGamma
, which occurs with
probability at least
1-\delta_{\mathrm{round}}
and gives

\widehat{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{tot}}\geq\Gamma_{\mathrm{tot}}
and

\widehat{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{tot}}/\eta+1=\mathcal{O}\left(\Gamma_{\mathrm{tot}}/\eta+1\right)
by

Section

D.5
.

We prove correctness by induction. At the beginning of round
j
, assume

\mathcal{Q}_{k}\subseteq\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,j-1}\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{D,\eta/2}\qquad\text{for all }k<j.

(125)

For
j=0
this is vacuous. In round
j
,
Theorem

D.5
gives

\widehat{\mathcal{Q}}_{j}\supseteq\mathcal{Q}_{j}\setminus\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,j-1}
with probability
at least
1-\delta_{\mathrm{round}}/2
. If
\widehat{\mathcal{Q}}_{j}=\emptyset
, the testing step is skipped and
returns the empty set. Otherwise,
Theorem

D.11
applies with failure probability
at most
\delta_{\mathrm{round}}/2
and accepts every candidate with
a_{rr}\geq\eta
while accepting only
candidates with
a_{rr}>\eta/2
. Therefore the update preserves

\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,j}\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{D,\eta/2}
. Moreover, for every non-final round

j<N_{\mathrm{rounds}}-1
, every element of
\mathcal{Q}_{j}
has rate strictly larger than
\eta
, and hence is
accepted. In the final round, all elements of
\mathcal{Q}_{j}
with rate at least
\eta
are accepted. Thus,
conditioned on the success of all rounds,

\mathcal{S}_{D,\eta}\subseteq\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,\eta}\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{D,\eta/2}.

The initial calibration and the
N_{\mathrm{rounds}}
rounds fail with total probability
\leq(N_{\mathrm{rounds}}+1)\delta_{\mathrm{round}}=\delta_{d}
, proving item
(1)
.

We now collect resources. Since structure identification internally also invokes
EstimateGamma
, we absorb the resources used by
EstimateGamma
into the structure sampling subroutine. On the success event,
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,\eta}\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{D,\eta/2}
, so
|\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,\eta}|\leq 2\Gamma_{\mathrm{tot}}/\eta
throughout. Hence, by
Theorem

D.5
,
|\widehat{\mathcal{Q}}_{j}|=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left((2^{j}\Gamma_{j}+1)(\Gamma_{\mathrm{tot}}/\eta+1)^{2}\right).
Combining
Theorem

D.5
with
Theorem

D.11
, the round-
j
evolution time is bounded by

T_{j}=\mathcal{O}\left(2^{j}\log\!\left(\frac{2^{j}\Gamma_{j}+1}{\delta_{\mathrm{round}}}\right)+2^{j}\log\!\left(\frac{\log(2^j\Lambda_L+4)}{\delta_{\mathrm{round}}}\right)\right)+\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{1}{\eta}\log\!\left(\frac{1}{\delta_{\mathrm{round}}}\right)\log\!\left(\frac{(2^{j}\Gamma_{j}+1)(\Gamma_{\mathrm{tot}}/\eta+1)^{2}\log(1/\delta_{\mathrm{round}})}{\delta_{\mathrm{round}}}\right)\right).

Uusing
\sum_{j=0}^{N_{\mathrm{rounds}}-1}2^{j}=\mathcal{O}\left(1/\eta\right)
, this proves item
(2)
:

t_{\mathrm{tot}}=\sum_{j=0}^{N_{\mathrm{rounds}}-1}T_{j}+\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(1/\eta\right)=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(1/\eta\right).

The minimum round-
j
evolution time is
\Omega(1/(\Lambda_{L}^{2}2^{j}))
during sampling and
\Theta(\eta/\Lambda_{L}^{2})
during testing. Since
2^{j}\leq 1/\eta
,

t_{\mathrm{res}}=\Omega\Bigl(\frac{\eta}{\Lambda_{L}^{2}}\Bigr),

proving item
(3)
.

For the number of experiments (item
(4)
),
Theorem

D.5
gives

N_{\exp}^{\mathrm{samp}}=\sum_{j=0}^{N_{\mathrm{rounds}}-1}\mathcal{O}\left((2^{j}\Gamma_{j}+1)\log\!\left(\frac{2^{j}\Gamma_{j}+1}{\delta_{\mathrm{round}}}\right)+\log(2^j\Lambda_L+4)\log\!\left(\frac{\log(2^j\Lambda_L+4)}{\delta_{\mathrm{round}}}\right)\right)=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{\Gamma_{\mathrm{tot}}}{\eta}+1\right).

The testing step contributes only
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(1\right)
random-code experiments per round, so
N_{\exp}=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\Gamma_{\mathrm{tot}}/\eta+1\right)
. The number of interleaved QEC rounds is

\sum_{j=0}^{N_{\mathrm{rounds}}-1}\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\Lambda_{L}^{2}4^{j}\right)+\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(N_{\mathrm{rounds}}\Lambda_{L}^{2}/\eta^{2}\right)=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\Lambda_{L}^{2}/\eta^{2}\right).

For ancillas (item
(5)
), the sampling stage uses
n+\mathcal{O}\left(\log(|\EstEtaDiagDissStruct|+1)\right)=n+\mathcal{O}\left(\log(\Gamma_{\mathrm{tot}}/\eta+1)\right)
ancillas. The testing stage uses

\mathcal{O}\left(\log\!\left(|\widehat{\mathcal{Q}}_{j}|(\widehat{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{tot}}/\eta+1)\right)\right)=\mathcal{O}\left(\log\!\left((2^{j}\Gamma_{j}+1)(\Gamma_{\mathrm{tot}}/\eta+1)^{3}\right)\right)

ancillas, which is absorbed into
n+\mathcal{O}\left(\log(\Gamma_{\mathrm{tot}}/\eta+1)\right)
up to the logarithmic factors.

Finally, the total classical cost (item
(6)
) is dominated by the testing stage. Using the candidate-size bound from
Theorem

D.5
, the calibration guarantee

\widehat{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{tot}}/\eta+1=\mathcal{O}\left(\Gamma_{\mathrm{tot}}/\eta+1\right)
, and

Theorem

D.11
, the total classical overhead is

C_{\mathrm{tot}}=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\left(n+\left(\frac{\Gamma_{\mathrm{tot}}}{\eta}+1\right)^{4}\right)\frac{\Lambda_{L}^{2}}{\eta^{2}}\right).

∎

D.5
Deferred proofs for Bell-sampling identification

Here, we present the deferred proofs related to dissipator structure identification via Bell sampling (
Algorithm

2
). We restate the lemmas for convenience.

Let
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}
be
n^{\prime}
-qubit Lindbladian with purely diagonal dissipator part

\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}(\rho)=\sum_{Q_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{eff}}\,\subseteq\,\mathcal{P}_{n^{\prime}}\setminus\{I\}}\alpha_{kk}\left(Q_{k}\rho Q_{k}-\rho\right).

where
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{eff}}
is a set of distinct Hermitian Paulis and
\alpha_{kk}\geq 0
for all
Q_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{eff}}
. Define the total effective dissipative rate
\Gamma_{\mathrm{eff}}\coloneq\sum_{Q_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{eff}}}\alpha_{kk}.
Fix a target Pauli
Q_{r}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{eff}}
. Let
C
be a stabilizer code on
n^{\prime}
qubits with code projector
\Pi
and stabilizer subgroup
S
. Let
G_{\mathrm{eff}}\coloneq\langle\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{eff}}\rangle\subseteq\mathbb{P}_{n^{\prime}}
denote the subgroup generated by
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{eff}}
. Assume that all Paulis

Q\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{eff}}
have zero syndrome
r(Q)=0
with respect to
C
, and that for every
R\in G_{\mathrm{eff}}
,
R\propto s\in S\implies s=I_{n^{\prime}}
. Equivalently, no nontrivial product of elements of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{eff}}
is proportional to a stabilizer. Let
\rho_{0}
denote a
2n^{\prime}
-qubit maximally entangled state
\rho_{0}\coloneq\ket{\Phi_0}\bra{\Phi_0}
where
\ket{\Phi_0}\coloneq\ket{\Phi_+}^{\otimes n^{\prime}}
and
\ket{\Phi_+}=(\ket{00}+\ket{11})/\sqrt{2}.

Let
\widetilde{\rho}
denote the state immediately before the Bell basis measurement

\widetilde{\rho}\coloneq(e^{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}t}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}})\left(\frac{\Pi\otimes I_{n^{\prime}}\,\rho_{0}\,\Pi\otimes I_{n^{\prime}}}{\tr(\Pi\otimes I_{n'} \,\rho_0)}\right).

For any Pauli
Q\in\mathbb{P}_{n^{\prime}}
, let
O_{Q}\coloneq(Q\otimes I_{n^{\prime}})\ket{\Phi_0}\bra{\Phi_0}(Q^{\dagger}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}})
denote the corresponding Bell-measurement projector. Then, for any
t\geq 0
, a single run of Bell sampling on
\widetilde{\rho}
satisfies

\displaystyle\Pr(\text{sample $Q_r$ modulo stabilizers})

\displaystyle\coloneq\tr\left(\sum_{\sigma\in S}O_{\sigma Q_{r}}\,\widetilde{\rho}\right)\geq e^{-\Gamma_{\mathrm{eff}}t}\alpha_{rr}t.

(126)

Proof.

Note that since the code projector can be expressed as
\Pi=\frac{1}{|S|}\sum_{s\in S}s
(Eq. (
36
)), we have
\tr(\Pi\otimes I_{n'} \,\rho_0)=1/|S|
. To further simplify the
\widetilde{\rho}
, we
commute the corner projection
\mathcal{C}_{\Pi}\coloneq\Pi(\cdot)\Pi
with the action of
e^{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}t}
since all
Q_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{eff}}
have zero syndromes and thus commute with
\Pi
:

\widetilde{\rho}=|S|(\mathcal{C}_{\Pi}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}})\circ(e^{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}t}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}})(\rho_{0})

Since
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}
is just a diagonal dissipator,
e^{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}t}
admits the following chi-matrix representation for any
\rho
:

e^{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}t}(\rho)=\sum_{Q_{m}\in G_{\mathrm{eff}}}\chi_{mm}(t)Q_{m}\rho Q_{m}.

In this chi representation, the sum over
G_{\mathrm{eff}}
