is understood modulo global phases, with one Hermitian representative chosen for each phase class; we choose the representative of the target phase class to be
Q_{r}
.

Fix
\sigma\in S
and write

Z_{r}\coloneq\sigma Q_{r}
for the corresponding representative of the coset
SQ_{r}
.
Using the chi-matrix representation of
e^{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}t}
together with
\widetilde{\rho}
and the fact that
\sigma\Pi=\Pi\sigma=\Pi
, we get

\displaystyle\Pr(\text{sample $Z_r$})\coloneq\tr(O_{Z_r}\widetilde{\rho})=|S|\sum_{Q_{m}\in G_{\mathrm{eff}}}\chi_{mm}(t)\bigg|\bra{\Phi_0}(Z_{r}^{\dagger}\Pi Q_{m}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}})\ket{\Phi_0}\bigg|^{2}=

\displaystyle=|S|\sum_{Q_{m}\in G_{\mathrm{eff}}}\chi_{mm}(t)\bigg|\bra{\Phi_0}(Q_{r}\Pi Q_{m}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}})\ket{\Phi_0}\bigg|^{2}=\frac{1}{|S|}\sum_{Q_{m}\in G_{\mathrm{eff}}}\chi_{mm}(t)\bigg|\bra{\Phi_0}(Q_{r}Q_{m}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}})\ket{\Phi_0}\bigg|^{2}.

where the last equality follows from
Q_{r}Q_{m}\in G_{\mathrm{eff}}
,
\Pi=\frac{1}{|S|}\sum_{s\in S}s
and the assumption that no nontrivial product of elements of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{eff}}
is proportional to a stabilizer.
By the property of Bell pairs, this further simplifies to

\Pr(\text{sample $Z_r$})=\frac{1}{|S|}\chi_{rr}(t)\,\bigg|\bra{\Phi_0}(Q_{r}Q_{r}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}})\ket{\Phi_0}\bigg|^{2}=\frac{\chi_{rr}(t)}{|S|}.

Therefore, combining the probabilities across all representatives of the coset
SQ_{r}
,

\Pr(\text{sample $Q_r$ modulo stabilizers})=\tr\left(\sum_{\sigma\in S}O_{\sigma Q_{r}}\,\widetilde{\rho}\right)=\sum_{\sigma\in S}\Pr(\text{sample $\sigma Q_r$})=\chi_{rr}(t).

(127)

We now lower bound
\chi_{rr}(t)
. For each
Q_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{eff}}
, let

\mathcal{U}_{k}(\rho)\coloneq Q_{k}\rho Q_{k}
. The superoperators
\mathcal{U}_{k}
commute with each other, since Pauli products commute up to phase and the global phase disappears under conjugation. Hence

e^{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}t}=\prod_{Q_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{eff}}}\exp\bigl(\alpha_{kk}t(\mathcal{U}_{k}-I)\bigr)=e^{-\Gamma_{\mathrm{eff}}t}\prod_{Q_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{eff}}}\exp\bigl(\alpha_{kk}t\,\mathcal{U}_{k}\bigr).

For each
k
,

\exp\bigl(\alpha_{kk}t\,\mathcal{U}_{k}\bigr)=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\frac{(\alpha_{kk}t)^{\ell}}{\ell!}\mathcal{U}_{k}^{\ell}.

Multiplying these expansions gives a Pauli-channel representation in which every term has nonnegative weight. In particular, the event that exactly one jump of type
Q_{r}
occurs and no other jump occurs contributes the channel

\rho\mapsto Q_{r}\rho Q_{r}
with weight

e^{-\Gamma_{\mathrm{eff}}t}\alpha_{rr}t.

Other jump patterns may also multiply to
Q_{r}
up to phase and hence can only increase
\chi_{rr}(t)
. Therefore

\chi_{rr}(t)\geq e^{-\Gamma_{\mathrm{eff}}t}\alpha_{rr}t.

Combining this lower bound with

\Pr(\text{sample $Q_r$ modulo stabilizers})=\chi_{rr}(t)
gives

\Pr(\text{sample $Q_r$ modulo stabilizers})=\chi_{rr}(t)\geq e^{-\Gamma_{\mathrm{eff}}t}\alpha_{rr}t.

concluding the proof.
∎

The following two lemmas are necessary steps for pre-calibrating the total remaining decay rate in
Algorithm

2
.

Lemma D.12

(Non-identity Bell-sampling probability for a diagonal dissipator)
.

Let
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}
be an
n^{\prime}
-qubit Lindbladian with purely diagonal dissipator part

\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}(\rho)=\sum_{Q_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{eff}}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{n^{\prime}}\setminus\{I\}}\alpha_{kk}(Q_{k}\rho Q_{k}-\rho),

where
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{eff}}
is a set of distinct Hermitian Paulis and
\alpha_{kk}\geq 0
. Define
\Gamma_{\mathrm{eff}}\coloneq\sum_{Q_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{eff}}}\alpha_{kk}.
Let
p(t)
denote the probability that Bell sampling
(e^{t\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}}\otimes I)(\rho_{0})
returns a non-identity Pauli outcome, where
\rho_{0}=\ket{\Phi_0}\bra{\Phi_0}
and
\ket{\Phi_0}=\ket{\Phi_+}^{\otimes n^{\prime}}
. Then, for every
t\geq 0
,

\frac{1}{2}\left(1-e^{-\Gamma_{\mathrm{eff}}t}\right)\leq p(t)\leq 1-e^{-\Gamma_{\mathrm{eff}}t}.

Moreover,
p(t)
is nondecreasing in
t
.

Proof.

The channel
e^{t\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}}
is a Pauli channel. Thus, writing

e^{t\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}}(\rho)=\sum_{R\in G_{\mathrm{eff}}}\chi_{RR}(t)R\rho R,

where
G_{\mathrm{eff}}\coloneq\langle\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{eff}}\rangle
is the phase-stripped Pauli subgroup generated by
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{eff}}
, Bell sampling returns
R
with probability
\chi_{RR}(t)
. Hence

p(t)=\sum_{R\in G_{\mathrm{eff}}\setminus\{I\}}\chi_{RR}(t).

We now rewrite this Pauli channel as a continuous-time random walk in the binary symplectic representation from Appendix
B.4.4
. Let
v_{k}\coloneq v_{Q_{k}}\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2n^{\prime}}
and let

V_{\mathrm{eff}}\coloneq\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{2}}\{v_{k}:Q_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{eff}}\}.

Let
N_{k}(t)
be independent Poisson random variables with rates
\alpha_{kk}t
. Since multiplication of phase-stripped Paulis corresponds to addition of their binary symplectic vectors modulo
2
, the Bell-sampling outcome is distributed as

Y_{t}\coloneq\sum_{Q_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{eff}}}\bigl(N_{k}(t)\bmod 2\bigr)v_{k}\in V_{\mathrm{eff}}.

In this notation,
p(t)=\Pr[Y_{t}\neq 0]
.

The upper bound follows immediately. If
Y_{t}\neq 0
, then at least one Poisson jump must have occurred. Since the total number of jumps is Poisson with rate
\Gamma_{\mathrm{eff}}t
,

p(t)\leq\Pr[\text{at least one jump by time }t]=1-e^{-\Gamma_{\mathrm{eff}}t}.

For the lower bound, we use the Fourier analysis of Boolean functions
107
. Consider parity functions on
V_{\mathrm{eff}}
. We write them as

\pi_{a}(y)\coloneq(-1)^{\langle a,y\rangle_{s}},

where
a\in\mathbb{F}_{2}^{2n^{\prime}}
and the function is restricted to
V_{\mathrm{eff}}
. For such a parity function, define

\lambda_{a}\coloneq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}Q_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{eff}},\\
\pi_{a}(v_{k})=-1\end{subarray}}\alpha_{kk}.

Averaging over all parity functions on
V_{\mathrm{eff}}
, every nonzero
v_{k}
has parity
-1
for exactly half of them. Hence

\mathbb{E}_{a}[\lambda_{a}]=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k}\alpha_{kk}=\frac{\Gamma_{\mathrm{eff}}}{2}.

Therefore there exists a parity function
\pi_{a}
such that
\lambda_{a}\geq\Gamma_{\mathrm{eff}}/2
.

For this parity function, define the two-state process
X_{t}^{(a)}\coloneq\pi_{a}(Y_{t})
. It starts at
+1
and flips precisely when a jump
v_{k}
with
\pi_{a}(v_{k})=-1
occurs. The cumulative flip process is therefore Poisson with rate
\lambda_{a}
, so

\Pr[X_{t}^{(a)}=-1]=\frac{1-e^{-2\lambda_{a}t}}{2}.

Since
\pi_{a}(0)=1
, the event
\{X_{t}^{(a)}=-1\}
implies
Y_{t}\neq 0
. Thus

p(t)=\Pr[Y_{t}\neq 0]\geq\Pr[X_{t}^{(a)}=-1]=\frac{1-e^{-2\lambda_{a}t}}{2}\geq\frac{1}{2}\left(1-e^{-\Gamma_{\mathrm{eff}}t}\right),

where the last inequality uses
\lambda_{a}\geq\Gamma_{\mathrm{eff}}/2
.

It remains to prove monotonicity. By Fourier inversion of the indicator function
\mathbf{1}_{Y_{t}=0}
on
V_{\mathrm{eff}}
,

\Pr[Y_{t}=0]=\frac{1}{|V_{\mathrm{eff}}|}\sum_{\pi}\mathbb{E}[\pi(Y_{t})]=\frac{1}{|V_{\mathrm{eff}}|}\sum_{\pi}e^{-2\lambda_{\pi}t},

where the sum is over all parity functions
\pi:V_{\mathrm{eff}}\to\{\pm 1\}
and
\lambda_{\pi}\coloneq\sum_{k:\pi(v_{k})=-1}\alpha_{kk}\geq 0
. Hence
\Pr[Y_{t}=0]
is nonincreasing in
t
, and therefore
p(t)=1-\Pr[Y_{t}=0]
is nondecreasing.
∎

Lemma D.13

(Pre-calibration of total effective dissipative rate)
.

Let
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}
be an
n^{\prime}
-qubit Lindbladian with purely diagonal dissipator part

\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}(\rho)=\sum_{Q_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{eff}}\,\subseteq\,\mathcal{P}_{n^{\prime}}\setminus\{I\}}\alpha_{kk}\left(Q_{k}\rho Q_{k}-\rho\right),

where
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{eff}}
is a set of distinct Hermitian Paulis and
\alpha_{kk}\geq 0
for all
Q_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{eff}}
. Define the total effective dissipative rate
\Gamma_{\mathrm{eff}}\coloneq\sum_{Q_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{eff}}}\alpha_{kk}
. Fix an upper bound
\Lambda_{D}\geq\Gamma_{\mathrm{eff}}
, a relevance scale
\gamma_{\min}>0
satisfying
\gamma_{\min}\leq\Lambda_{D}
, and a failure probability
\delta\in(0,1)
. Let
\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{eff}}\coloneq\max\{\Gamma_{\mathrm{eff}},\gamma_{\min}\}
.

Assume Bell-sampling access to
e^{t\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}}
: namely, for any chosen
t\geq 0
, one may prepare
\rho_{0}\coloneq\ket{\Phi_0}\bra{\Phi_0}
with
\ket{\Phi_0}\coloneq\ket{\Phi_+}^{\otimes n^{\prime}}
, apply
e^{t\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}}
to the left register, and measure in the Bell basis. Then there is a procedure
EstimateGamma
which returns an estimate
\widehat{\Gamma}
satisfying, with probability at least
1-\delta
,

\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{eff}}\leq\widehat{\Gamma}\leq 4\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{eff}}.

(128)

The procedure uses Bell samples at a geometric grid of interrogation times from
1/\gamma_{\min}
down to
1/(4\Lambda_{D})
. Its total evolution time is

\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\gamma_{\min}}\log\left(\frac{\log(4\Lambda_D/\gamma_{\min})}{\delta}\right)\right),

and the number of Bell-sampling experiments is

\mathcal{O}\left(\log\left(\frac{4\Lambda_{D}}{\gamma_{\min}}\right)\log\left(\frac{\log(4\Lambda_D/\gamma_{\min})}{\delta}\right)\right).

Proof.

Let
p(t)
denote the probability to Bell sample a non-identity Pauli outcome after time
t
. By
Section

D.5
, for every
t\geq 0
,

\frac{1}{2}\left(1-e^{-\Gamma_{\mathrm{eff}}t}\right)\leq p(t)\leq 1-e^{-\Gamma_{\mathrm{eff}}t},

(129)

and
p(t)
is nondecreasing in
t
. Set
\epsilon\coloneq 1/100
and define

p_{\mathrm{th}}\coloneq\frac{1}{2}\left(1-e^{-1/4}+\frac{1}{2}(1-e^{-3/4})\right).

One readily verifies that

1-e^{-1/4}+\epsilon<p_{\mathrm{th}}<\frac{1}{2}(1-e^{-3/4})-\epsilon.

(130)

Consider the geometric schedule

t_{\ell}\coloneq\gamma_{\min}^{-1}(3/4)^{\ell}
for
\ell=0,1,\dots,L,

where
L
is the smallest integer such that
t_{L}\leq 1/(4\Lambda_{D})
. At each time
t_{\ell}
, run Bell sampling
N_{0}\coloneq C_{\mathrm{pc}}\log\left(\frac{L+1}{\delta}\right)

times, and let
\widehat{p}_{\ell}
denote the empirical frequency of non-identity outcomes. The procedure scans the times in decreasing order. If
\widehat{p}_{0}<p_{\mathrm{th}}
, it returns
\widehat{\Gamma}\coloneq\gamma_{\min}
. Otherwise, let
\ell_{\star}
be the largest index such that
\widehat{p}_{\ell_{\star}}\geq p_{\mathrm{th}}
, and return

\widehat{\Gamma}\coloneq\frac{1}{t_{\ell_{\star}}}.

By Hoeffding’s inequality and the union bound, choosing
C_{\mathrm{pc}}
sufficiently large ensures that, with probability at least
1-\delta
,
|\widehat{p}_{\ell}-p(t_{\ell})|\leq\epsilon
simultaneously for all
\ell=0,\dots,L
. We condition on this event for the remainder of the proof.

If
\Gamma_{\mathrm{eff}}t_{\ell}\leq 1/4
, then Eq. (
129
) and Eq. (
130
) give

p(t_{\ell})\leq 1-e^{-1/4}<p_{\mathrm{th}}-\epsilon,

and therefore
\widehat{p}_{\ell}<p_{\mathrm{th}}
. Hence the empirical test rejects at
\ell
. Conversely, if
\Gamma_{\mathrm{eff}}t_{\ell}\geq 3/4
, then Eq. (
129
) and Eq. (
130
) give

p(t_{\ell})\geq\frac{1}{2}(1-e^{-3/4})>p_{\mathrm{th}}+\epsilon,

and therefore
\widehat{p}_{\ell}\geq p_{\mathrm{th}}
. Hence the empirical test accepts at
\ell
.

If the procedure returns
\widehat{\Gamma}=\gamma_{\min}
, then the test rejected at
\ell=0
. Hence we cannot have
\Gamma_{\mathrm{eff}}t_{0}\geq 3/4
, so
\Gamma_{\mathrm{eff}}<\gamma_{\min}
. Thus
\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{eff}}=\gamma_{\min}
, and the returned estimate satisfies Eq. (
128
).

It remains to consider the case where the procedure stops at
\ell_{\star}
. Since the test accepts at
\ell_{\star}
, the rejection implication gives
\Gamma_{\mathrm{eff}}t_{\ell_{\star}}>1/4
, and therefore

\widehat{\Gamma}=\frac{1}{t_{\ell_{\star}}}<4\Gamma_{\mathrm{eff}}.

Moreover,
\ell_{\star}<L
, since
t_{L}\leq 1/(4\Lambda_{D})
and
\Gamma_{\mathrm{eff}}\leq\Lambda_{D}
imply
\Gamma_{\mathrm{eff}}t_{L}\leq 1/4
, so the final grid point rejects. By maximality of
\ell_{\star}
, the test rejects at
\ell_{\star}+1
. Hence we cannot have
\Gamma_{\mathrm{eff}}t_{\ell_{\star}+1}\geq 3/4
. Since
t_{\ell_{\star}+1}=(3/4)t_{\ell_{\star}}
, this implies
\Gamma_{\mathrm{eff}}t_{\ell_{\star}}<1
, and therefore

\widehat{\Gamma}=\frac{1}{t_{\ell_{\star}}}>\Gamma_{\mathrm{eff}}.

Also, since
t_{\ell_{\star}}\leq t_{0}=1/\gamma_{\min}
, we have
\widehat{\Gamma}\geq\gamma_{\min}
. Combining these bounds gives

\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{eff}}\leq\widehat{\Gamma}\leq 4\widetilde{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{eff}},

as claimed.

Finally,
L+1=\mathcal{O}\left(\log(4\Lambda_D/\gamma_{\min})\right)
, and each grid point uses
N_{0}=\mathcal{O}\left(\log((L+1)/\delta)\right)
Bell samples. This gives

\mathcal{O}\left(\log\left(\frac{4\Lambda_{D}}{\gamma_{\min}}\right)\log\left(\frac{\log(4\Lambda_D/\gamma_{\min})}{\delta}\right)\right)

Bell-sampling experiments. Since the times
t_{\ell}
decrease geometrically,

\sum_{\ell=0}^{L}N_{0}t_{\ell}\leq 4N_{0}\gamma_{\min}^{-1}=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\gamma_{\min}}\log\left(\frac{\log(4\Lambda_D/\gamma_{\min})}{\delta}\right)\right),

which proves the claimed total evolution time.
∎

Remark D.14

(Implementation of pre-calibration using reshaped dynamics)
.

In
Algorithm

2
, the pre-calibration routine from
Section

D.5
is not given direct access to the ideal semigroup
e^{t\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}^{(j)}}
. Instead, it is implemented using the round-
j
reshaped dynamics
A_{D}^{(j)}(t,N)
from Eq. (
106
), which consists of physical Pauli twirling interleaved with repeated QEC recovery.

There are two minor implementation points. First, the use of
A_{D}^{(j)}(t,N)
introduces a reshaping error. By
Section

C.4
,

\left\|A_{D}^{(j)}(t,N)-e^{t\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}^{(j)}}\circ\mathcal{C}_{\Pi_{j}}\right\|_{\diamond}\leq\frac{t^{2}(\|\mathcal{L}\|_{\diamond}^{2}+\|\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}^{(j)}\|_{\diamond}^{2})}{2N}\leq\frac{5\Lambda_{L}^{2}t^{2}}{2N}.

where the second inequality follow from
\Lambda_{L}\geq\|\mathcal{L}\|_{\diamond}
,
\Gamma_{\mathrm{eff},j}\leq\Lambda_{L}
, and

\|\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}^{(j)}\|_{\diamond}\leq 2\Gamma_{j}^{\mathrm{eff}}\leq 2\Lambda_{L}
due to the diagonal form of
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}^{(j)}
. Thus we choose the number of QEC-reshaping cycles as a function of the interrogation time:

N_{\mathrm{pc}}(t)\coloneq\left\lceil 500\,\Lambda_{L}^{2}t^{2}\right\rceil\leq\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\Lambda_{L}^{2}}{\gamma_{\min}^{2}}\right).

where the second inequality is due to the longest time
t\leq 1/\gamma_{\min}
. This achieves the reshaping error is at most
1/200
for every interrogation time in the geometric pre-calibration grid. Since the grid is geometric, the total number of pre-calibration QEC-reshaping cycles is dominated by the largest interrogation time and is also
\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\Lambda_{L}^{2}}{\gamma_{\min}^{2}}\right)
.

Since the diamond-norm distance upper bounds the additive error in every Bell-measurement probability, the proof of
Section

D.5
goes through by estimating the empirical non-identity probability to accuracy
1/200
and allocating the remaining
1/200
to reshaping error.

The corresponding time resolution is:

\frac{t}{N_{\mathrm{pc}}(t)}=\Omega\left(\frac{1}{t\Lambda_{L}^{2}}\right)\geq\Omega\left(\frac{\gamma_{\min}}{\Lambda_{L}^{2}}\right)

Second, the implemented experiment uses the Bell state projected into the code corner algebra. This does not change the effective Bell-sampling probabilities once outcomes are grouped modulo stabilizers. Indeed, for a Pauli-channel coefficient
\chi_{rr}(t)
corresponding to a logical Pauli
Q_{r}
, Eq. (
127
) gives

\Pr(\text{sample $Q_r$ modulo stabilizers})=\chi_{rr}(t).

Therefore the abstract pre-calibration lemma applies to the code-projected implementation with “non-identity” interpreted as a nontrivial stabilizer coset. Terms whose reshaped Pauli lies in the identity coset are excluded from
\Gamma_{j}^{\mathrm{eff}}
and are handled separately by the identity-coset initialization in
Algorithm

2
.

D.6
Deferred proofs for syndrome-based testing

Here, we present the deferred proofs related to dissipator structure testing via syndrome measurements (
Theorem

D.11
). We restate the lemmas for convenience.

Assume the Lindbladian
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{diag}}
has the diagonal Pauli-dissipator form
as in (
122
) with dissipator structure
\mathcal{S}_{D}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{n}
. Let
\{G_{\ell}\}_{\ell=1}^{m}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{n^{\prime}}
be stabilizer generators with syndrome projectors
\{\Pi_{s}\}_{s\in\{0,1\}^{m}}
as in (
41
). Fix a non-identity
target Pauli
P_{r}
and assume that its syndrome is nonzero, i.e.
r(P_{r})\neq\mathbf{0}
. Let
\rho=\Pi_{0}\rho\Pi_{0}\in\mathfrak{A}_{c}\subseteq\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n^{\prime}})
be any code state. Then for any
\tau\geq 0
,

p_{r}(\tau)\coloneq\Pr\bigl[\text{measuring syndrome }r(P_{r})\bigr]\;=\;\tr\bigl(\Pi_{r(P_{r})}\,e^{\tau\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{diag}}}(\rho)\bigr)\;=\;\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r(P_{k})=r(P_{r})\\
P_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}\end{subarray}}a_{kk}\,\tau\;+\;\Delta_{r}(\tau),

(131)

where the remainder satisfies
\absolutevalue{\Delta_r(\tau)}\ \leq\ \frac{1}{2}\,\|\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{diag}}\|_{\diamond}^{2}\,\tau^{2}.

Proof.

By
Section

B.2
,
e^{\tau\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{diag}}}=I+\tau\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{diag}}+\int_{0}^{\tau}(\tau-s)\,\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{diag}}^{2}e^{s\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{diag}}}\,ds,
and therefore

p_{r}(\tau)=\tr\bigl(\Pi_{r(P_{r})}\rho\bigr)+\tau\,\tr\bigl(\Pi_{r(P_{r})}\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{diag}}(\rho)\bigr)+\tr\left(\Pi_{r(P_{r})}\int_{0}^{\tau}(\tau-s)\,\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{diag}}^{2}e^{s\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{diag}}}(\rho)\,ds\right).

(132)

Since
\rho=\Pi_{0}\rho\Pi_{0}
is a code state and
r(P_{r})\neq\mathbf{0}
, we have
\Pi_{r(P_{r})}\rho=0
, so the first term vanishes.

We now evaluate the linear term. Using the diagonal Pauli-dissipator form,

\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{diag}}(\rho)=\sum_{P_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}}a_{kk}\bigl((P_{k}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})\rho(P_{k}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})-\rho\bigr),

and again, since
\tr(\Pi_{r(P_r)}\rho)=0
, it follows that

\tr\bigl(\Pi_{r(P_{r})}\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{diag}}(\rho)\bigr)=\sum_{P_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}}a_{kk}\,\tr\bigl(\Pi_{r(P_{r})}(P_{k}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})\rho(P_{k}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})\bigr).

By syndrome projector shift property Eq. (
42
) and trace cyclic property:

\tr\bigl(\Pi_{r(P_{r})}\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{diag}}(\rho)\bigr)=\sum_{P_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}}a_{kk}\tr\bigl((P_{k}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})\Pi_{r(P_{r})\oplus r(P_{k})}\rho(P_{k}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})\bigr)=\sum_{P_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}}a_{kk}\tr\bigl(\Pi_{r(P_{r})\oplus r(P_{k})}\rho\bigr)

Since
\rho=\Pi_{0}\rho\Pi_{0}
, the
k
th summand can be nonzero only if
r(P_{k})=r(P_{r})
. Together with
\tr(\rho)=1
this yields

\tr\bigl(\Pi_{r(P_{r})}\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{diag}}(\rho)\bigr)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r(P_{k})=r(P_{r})\\
P_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}\end{subarray}}a_{kk}

Define

\Delta_{r}(\tau)\coloneq\tr\left(\Pi_{r(P_{r})}\int_{0}^{\tau}(\tau-s)\,\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{diag}}^{2}e^{s\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{diag}}}(\rho)\,ds\right).

Then Eq. (
132
) becomes

p_{r}(\tau)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r(P_{k})=r(P_{r})\\
P_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}\end{subarray}}a_{kk}\tau+\Delta_{r}(\tau),

which proves Eq. (
131
).

It remains to bound the remainder. Using Hölder’s inequality and
\|\Pi_{r(P_{r})}\|_{\infty}\leq 1
,

|\Delta_{r}(\tau)|\leq\left\|\int_{0}^{\tau}(\tau-s)\,\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{diag}}^{2}e^{s\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{diag}}}(\rho)\,ds\right\|_{1}\|\Pi_{r(P_{r})}\|_{\infty}\leq\left\|\int_{0}^{\tau}(\tau-s)\,\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{diag}}^{2}e^{s\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{diag}}}(\rho)\,ds\right\|_{1}

Since
\|\rho\|_{1}=1
and
\|e^{s\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{diag}}}\|_{\diamond}=1
, we further obtain

|\Delta_{r}(\tau)|\leq\left\|\int_{0}^{\tau}(\tau-s)\,\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{diag}}^{2}e^{s\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{diag}}}\,ds\right\|_{\diamond}\leq\int_{0}^{\tau}(\tau-s)\,\|\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{diag}}\|_{\diamond}^{2}\|e^{s\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{diag}}}\|_{\diamond}\,ds\leq\frac{1}{2}\|\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{diag}}\|_{\diamond}^{2}\tau^{2},

concluding the proof.
∎

Let
X_{1},\dots,X_{N}\in\{0,1\}
be Bernoulli random variables, not necessarily independent, and let

\widehat{p}\coloneq\frac{1}{N}\sum_{\ell=1}^{N}X_{\ell}
.
Let
\epsilon\in(0,1]
and
\delta\in(0,1)
. Then there exists a universal constant

C_{\mathrm{mg}}>0
such that, if

N\geq C_{\mathrm{mg}}\,\epsilon^{-1}\log(1/\delta)
, then:

(i)

if
\mathbb{E}[X_{\ell}\mid X_{1},\dots,X_{\ell-1}]\leq\frac{5}{8}\epsilon
for every
\ell\in[N]
, then

\Pr\left(\widehat{p}>\frac{3}{4}\epsilon\right)\leq\delta
;

(ii)

if
\mathbb{E}[X_{\ell}\mid X_{1},\dots,X_{\ell-1}]\geq\frac{7}{8}\epsilon
for every
\ell\in[N]
, then

\Pr\left(\widehat{p}\leq\frac{3}{4}\epsilon\right)\leq\delta
.

Proof.

Let
S_{N}\coloneq\sum_{\ell=1}^{N}X_{\ell}.
Since
X_{\ell}\in\{0,1\}
for every
\ell
, both Corollary 6 and Corollary 14 of
86
apply with
c=1
.

For part (i), set
a_{\ell}\coloneq\frac{5}{8}\epsilon
and
\mu\coloneq\sum_{\ell=1}^{N}a_{\ell}=\frac{5}{8}\epsilon N
. Then

\frac{3}{4}\epsilon N=\frac{6}{5}\mu=\left(1+\frac{1}{5}\right)\mu.

Since
\mathbb{E}[X_{\ell}\mid X_{1},\dots,X_{\ell-1}]\leq a_{\ell}
for all
\ell
, Corollary 6 of
86
with deviation parameter
\frac{1}{5}
gives

\Pr\left(\widehat{p}>\frac{3}{4}\epsilon\right)\leq\Pr\left(S_{N}\geq\left(1+\frac{1}{5}\right)\mu\right)\leq\exp\left(-\frac{(1/5)^{2}}{2+1/5}\mu\right)=\exp\left(-\frac{\epsilon N}{88}\right).

Thus part (i) holds whenever
N\geq 88\,\epsilon^{-1}\log(1/\delta)
.

For part (ii), set
a_{\ell}\coloneq\frac{7}{8}\epsilon
and
\mu\coloneq\sum_{\ell=1}^{N}a_{\ell}=\frac{7}{8}\epsilon N
. Then

\frac{3}{4}\epsilon N=\frac{6}{7}\mu=\left(1-\frac{1}{7}\right)\mu.

Since
\mathbb{E}[X_{\ell}\mid X_{1},\dots,X_{\ell-1}]\geq a_{\ell}
for all
\ell
, Corollary 14 of
86
with deviation parameter
\frac{1}{7}
gives

\Pr\left(\widehat{p}\leq\frac{3}{4}\epsilon\right)\leq\Pr\left(S_{N}\leq\left(1-\frac{1}{7}\right)\mu\right)\leq\exp\left(-\frac{(1/7)^{2}}{2}\mu\right)=\exp\left(-\frac{\epsilon N}{112}\right).

Thus part (ii) holds whenever
N\geq 112\,\epsilon^{-1}\log(1/\delta)
.

Therefore both conclusions hold with the universal constant
C_{\mathrm{mg}}=112
.
∎

Appendix E
Hamiltonian-Disjoint-from-Dissipator (HDD) Learning

Let us write down the unknown
n
-qubit Lindbladian yet again:

\mathcal{L}(\rho)=-i\sum_{P_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{H}}h_{k}\commutator{P_k}{\rho}+\sum_{P_{k},P_{j}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}}a_{kj}\left(P_{k}\rho P_{j}-\tfrac{1}{2}\anticommutator{P_j P_k}{\rho}\right).

(133)

In general, Markovian dissipation precludes Heisenberg-limited learning of arbitrary Hamiltonian coefficients
148
;
40
. This obstruction is further complicated in the ansatz-free setting. If the dissipator is known exactly, one can in principle attempt to learn the Hamiltonian-not-in-the-Lindblad-span component (HNLS
148
;
40
;
59
) at Heisenberg-limited scaling. However, learning the dissipator itself is standard-quantum-limited
21
. We therefore choose a weaker Heisenberg-learnable target: the part of the Hamiltonian outside of the dissipator footprint defined in
Appendix

E
.

In this section, we first prove a conditional Heisenberg-limited learning result for this target. Assuming the
\eta
-heavy dissipator structure
\mathcal{S}_{D,\eta}
is known for
\eta
sufficiently small,
Algorithm

7
learns the support and coefficients of all Hamiltonian-Disjoint-from-Dissipator terms to additive accuracy
\varepsilon
, without prior knowledge of the Hamiltonian structure. We then remove the known-dissipator assumption in Appendix
E.5
by composing this routine with the dissipator structure learner from Appendix
D
. In the fully general guarantee, it suffices to learn the dissipator down to scale
\eta\propto\varepsilon^{2}
(
Section

E.5
); under an additional regularity assumption, this can be relaxed to
\eta\propto\varepsilon
, yielding Heisenberg-limited HDD Hamiltonian learning (
Section

E.5
) with no prior knowledge of either the Hamiltonian or dissipator structures.

Definition E.1

(Dissipator footprint,
\eta
-heavy)
.

Given the
\eta
-heavy dissipator structure
\mathcal{S}_{D,\eta}
from Eq. (
101
), we use

\mathfrak{D}_{\eta}\coloneq\mathcal{S}_{D,\eta}\cup\{P=\operatorname{Herm}(P_{\ell}P_{m}):P_{\ell},P_{m}\in\mathcal{S}_{D,\eta},P_{\ell}\neq P_{m}\}

to denote the
\eta
-heavy
dissipator footprint
.

Definition E.2

(Hamiltonian-Disjoint-from-Dissipator (HDD) at scale
\eta
)
.

Given the dissipator footprint
\mathfrak{D}_{\eta}
from
Appendix

E
, we define the
Hamiltonian-Disjoint-from-Dissipator at scale
\eta
as

\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{HDD},\eta}\coloneq\mathcal{S}_{H}\setminus\mathfrak{D}_{\eta}.

Equivalently,
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{HDD},\eta}
consists of the Hermitian Pauli terms in
\mathcal{S}_{H}
that do not lie in the
\eta
-heavy dissipator footprint.

At a high level,
Algorithm

7
parallels the dissipator structure learner from Appendix
D.4
. It samples the strongest remaining HDD terms, estimates their coefficients to remove false positives, updates the error-correcting code to suppress the identified terms, and iterates on the weaker residual Hamiltonian. The reshaping scheme uses only Pauli check measurements and applications of Pauli operators, eliminating any need for quantum controls that simulate inverse-time evolution.

The main result of this section is summarized in the following theorem. The exact role of bias and residual parameters
B_{\infty},K_{\infty},R_{\diamond}
used in
Theorem

E.3
is described in
Theorems

E.9
and
E.7
, but intuitively they control the errors caused by unknown dissipator terms. In Appendix
D
, we quantify what choice of
\eta
is sufficient to guarantee the required bounds on
B_{\infty},K_{\infty},R_{\diamond}
.

Theorem E.3

(Hierarchical HDD Hamiltonian learning)
.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be an
n
-qubit
M
-sparse Lindbladian as in Eq. (
133
). Fix
\varepsilon,\delta\in(0,1)
and assume black-box access to
e^{t\mathcal{L}}
. Assume the
\eta
-heavy dissipator structure
\mathcal{S}_{D,\eta}
is given, and that it satisfies the following uniform small-bias/small-residual condition. For every successful QEC code draw used by
Algorithm

5
(and reused by
Algorithm

6
), the corresponding effective generator
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}
written in the form of Eq. (
136
) has bias and residual parameters from Eq. (
139
) satisfying

B_{\infty}\leq\varepsilon/50,\qquad K_{\infty}\leq\varepsilon/50,\qquad R_{\diamond}\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{8\sqrt{2}\pi}.

Then running
Algorithm

7
returns coefficient estimates
\widehat{h}_{\mathrm{HDD},\eta}
for the Hamiltonian-Disjoint-from-Dissipator Hamiltonian at scale
\eta
satisfying the following guarantees.

1.

(Accuracy)
With probability at least
1-\delta
,

\|\widehat{h}_{\mathrm{HDD},\eta}-h_{\mathrm{HDD},\eta}\|_{\infty}\leq\varepsilon.

2.

(Evolution time)
The total evolution time under
e^{t\mathcal{L}}
is

t_{\mathrm{tot}}=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{M^{2}}{\varepsilon}\right).

3.

(Time resolution)
The procedure only applies
e^{t\mathcal{L}}
for times at least

t_{\mathrm{res}}=\Omega\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{M^{3}}\right).

4.

(Number of experiments)
The total number of collected measurements is

\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(M^{3}\right),

dominated by Bell sampling during the candidate-identification stage.

5.

(Ancilla cost)
The procedure uses at most

n+\mathcal{O}\left(\log M\right)

noiseless ancilla qubits in addition to the
n
probe qubits.

6.

(Classical overhead)
The classical processing cost is

\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{M^{6}+nM^{5}+n^{2}M^{4}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right).

The first and second terms come from real-time QEC processing in the candidate-identification stage, while the last term comes from logical-Pauli sampling in coefficient learning.

Proof.

See Appendix
E.4
.
∎

Remark E.4

(Ancilla-reduced HDD Hamiltonian learner)
.

In Appendix
H
, we also briefly describe an ancilla-reduced variant of
Theorem

E.3
that improves the total ancilla count from
n+\mathcal{O}\left(\log M\right)
to
\mathcal{O}\left(\log M\right)
at the cost of increased black-box evolution time
t_{\mathrm{tot}}=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{M^{4}\log(n)}{\varepsilon}\right)
following
70
.

This section is organized as follows:

•

Hamiltonian-Disjoint-from-Dissipator hierarchy & notation.

In Appendix
E.1
, we describe the hierarchical learning procedure and introduce the notation used throughout the conditional construction.

•

Candidate identification via Bell sampling.

In Appendix
E.2
, we present
Algorithm

5
, which samples the strongest unknown HDD terms via Bell sampling, and prove its guarantees in
Theorem

E.7
.

•

HDD coefficient learning via robust frequency estimation.

In Appendix
E.3
, we present
Algorithm

6
, which estimates the coefficients of the candidate Hamiltonian terms and removes the false positives produced by the HDD candidate-identification step.

•

Hamiltonian-Disjoint-from-Dissipator hierarchical learning algorithm.

In Appendix
E.4
, we combine Hamiltonian-Disjoint-from-Dissipator candidate identification (
Algorithm

5
) and coefficient learning (
Algorithm

6
) into the full hierarchical algorithm
Algorithm

7
, which learns the HDD Hamiltonian with Heisenberg-limited interaction time.

•

Removing the known dissipator structure assumption.

In Appendix
E.5
, we lift the known dissipator-structure assumption by invoking the learner from Appendix
D
.

•

Deferred proofs.

In Appendix
E.6
, we provide the deferred proofs for the HDD candidate-identification guarantees.

E.1
Hierarchy and notation

Throughout the conditional HDD learning analysis, we take the
\eta
-heavy dissipator structure
\mathcal{S}_{D,\eta}
as input. Similar to dissipator structure learning (Appendix
D
), the HDD learner uses QEC-based Lindbladian reshaping (Eq. (
65
) and Eq. (
67
)) together with a hierarchical learning protocol adapted from
70
. The role of
\eta
is to control the light dissipative terms not corrected by QEC: after reshaping, these terms appear as a residual Lindbladian and as a small Hamiltonian bias.

The hierarchy is formed by splitting the learning problem according to the following definition:

Definition E.5

(Round-
j
Hamiltonian-Disjoint-from-Dissipator set)
.

Given the Hamiltonian-Disjoint-from-Dissipator set
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{HDD},\eta}
from
Appendix

E
and target accuracy
\varepsilon>0
, define the round-
j
HDD Pauli set as

\mathcal{Y}_{j}\coloneq\bigl\{P_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{HDD},\eta}:2^{-(j+1)}<|h_{k}|\leq 2^{-j}\bigr\},\qquad j=0,1,\ldots,\lceil\log_{2}(1/\varepsilon)\rceil-1.

(134)

By construction, learning all sets
\mathcal{Y}_{j}
and the corresponding Hamiltonian coefficients to accuracy
\varepsilon
is sufficient for learning the entire Hamiltonian-Disjoint-from-Dissipator at scale
\eta
.

Hierarchical learning proceeds in rounds indexed by
j
, where round
j
uses interrogation time
T_{j}=2^{j}T
and targets the bin
\mathcal{Y}_{j}
. The algorithm starts from the largest Hamiltonian-Disjoint-from-Dissipator terms and then moves to progressively weaker bins. At the beginning of each round, we choose a QEC code that corrects the known
\eta
-heavy dissipator structure
\mathcal{S}_{D,\eta}
and suppresses the Hamiltonian-Disjoint-from-Dissipator Hamiltonian terms learned in previous rounds. Within the round, we first use Bell sampling to identify a candidate set containing the strongest remaining terms, and then use coefficient learning to estimate the candidate coefficients and remove false positives. The newly confirmed terms are then added to the suppressed set, the interrogation time is doubled, and the procedure is repeated. Since the QEC code is drawn randomly, the Bell-sampling step is repeated with multiple independent code draws, ensuring that with high probability every
P_{k}\in\mathcal{Y}_{j}
is probed by at least one QEC instance.

More concretely, for each round
j
, let

\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathrm{HDD{}},\eta}^{(j-1)}\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{HDD},\eta}

denote the set of Hamiltonian-Disjoint-from-Dissipator Paulis identified in the previous rounds. Throughout the hierarchical learning algorithm, we maintain the invariant

\{P_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{HDD},\eta}:\ |h_{k}|\geq 2^{-j}\}\subseteq\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathrm{HDD{}},\eta}^{(j-1)}.

(135)

The HDD candidate-identification and coefficient-learning steps use different reshaping primitives, but both are analyzed through the same effective-generator interface. In each case, the reshaped dynamics on
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n^{\prime}})
is described, using the full-space realization from
Section

C.2
, by an effective Lindbladian of the form

\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}(\rho)=-i[H_{\mathrm{tar}}+H_{\mathrm{bias}},\rho]+\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{resid}}(\rho).

(136)

For candidate identification,
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}
is obtained from QEC reshaping alone (
Section

C.2
); here
H_{\mathrm{tar}}
is the Hamiltonian signal that survives the QEC reshaping. For coefficient learning of a candidate Pauli
P_{a}
,
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}
is obtained from QEC reshaping followed by a partial logical twirl (
Section

C.2
); in this case, with
\overline{P}_{a}\coloneq P_{a}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}
, the target Hamiltonian is
H_{\mathrm{tar}}=h_{a}\overline{P}_{a}
. In both cases,
H_{\mathrm{bias}}
denotes the Hamiltonian bias induced by corrected–uncorrected dissipator cross terms, and
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{resid}}
denotes the residual dissipator supported on uncorrected light dissipator Paulis. We write the residual dissipator in the aggregate Pauli-Kossakowski form:

\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{resid}}(\rho)=\sum_{Q_{k},Q_{j}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{D,res}}}\alpha_{kj}\left(Q_{k}\rho Q_{j}-\frac{1}{2}\{Q_{j}Q_{k},\rho\}\right),

(137)

where
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{D,res}}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{n^{\prime}}\setminus\{I\}
is the residual Pauli support and

\alpha\succeq 0
is the corresponding Kossakowski matrix. Explicit expressions for the
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}
generators are given in Eq. (
70
) and Eq. (
74
). Since the coefficients
\alpha_{kj}
are aggregate, they need not coincide with the original Kossakowski entries
a_{kj}
appearing in Eq. (
70
) and Eq. (
74
).

For the Bell sampling analysis, we also use the quantum jump unraveling. In particular, define

K_{\mathrm{resid}}\coloneq\sum_{Q_{k},Q_{j}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{D,res}}}\alpha_{kj}Q_{j}Q_{k}.

(138)

The no-jump branch of the effective Lindblad evolution is generated by the non-Hermitian operator

H_{\mathrm{tar}}+H_{\mathrm{bias}}-\frac{i}{2}K_{\mathrm{resid}}
.

To bound the bias and residual errors in Eq. (
136
), we define the following parameters:

B_{\infty}\coloneq\|H_{\mathrm{bias}}\|_{\infty,\mathrm{coeff}},\qquad R_{\diamond}\coloneq\|\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{resid}}\|_{\diamond},\qquad K_{\infty}\coloneq\|K_{\mathrm{resid}}\|_{\infty,\mathrm{coeff}}.

(139)

where
\|\cdot\|_{\infty,\mathrm{coeff}}
denotes the maximum absolute Pauli coefficient (including the identity in the case of
K_{\mathrm{resid}}
). The candidate-identification and coefficient-learning subsections state their admissible upper bounds on
B_{\infty}
,
R_{\diamond}
and
K_{\infty}
. In Appendix
E.5
, we choose
\eta
so that the corresponding bounds hold uniformly over all rounds and code draws.

E.2
Candidate identification via Bell sampling

We now describe
Algorithm

5
, the candidate-identification subroutine for round
j
. Under the round invariant Eq. (
135
), all Hamiltonian-Disjoint-from-Dissipator terms above scale
2^{-j}
have already been identified, and the goal is to output a candidate set containing the current bin
\mathcal{Y}_{j}
. For this step, we use the QEC reshaping routine from
Section

C.2
with

V_{\mathrm{corr}}=\mathcal{S}_{D,\eta},\qquad V_{\mathrm{det}}=\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathrm{HDD{}},\eta}^{(j-1)}.

(140)

Thus, the
\eta
-heavy dissipator terms are corrected, while previously learned Hamiltonian-Disjoint-from-Dissipator Hamiltonian terms are detected and suppressed.

The procedure has a
two-level coupon-collector
structure. First, we draw random stabilizer codes via
Algorithm

1
. For each
P_{k}\in\mathcal{Y}_{j}
, a fresh code makes
P_{k}
survive, i.e.
r(P_{k})=0
, as a nontrivial
logical
operator with non-negligible probability. Repeating the code draw ensures, with high probability, that every target term survives the QEC construction at least once. This differs from dissipator structure identification in Appendix
D.2
, where the unknown terms of interest need not have zero syndrome: detectability is sufficient to suppress Hamiltonian terms to leading order, whereas a dissipative Pauli jump with nonzero syndrome can still contribute after recovery as a logical Pauli. Second, conditioned on survival, Bell sampling on the projected EPR state must hit one of the stabilizer-coset representatives of the survived logical Pauli. Repeating the Bell measurements for each code ensures that we see at least one representative for every survived term. Together, these two coverage steps yield a candidate set
\widehat{\mathcal{Y}}_{j}
that contains
\mathcal{Y}_{j}
with probability at least
1-\delta_{j}
.

Inputs :

[label=(0), leftmargin=*, nosep]

1.

Hierarchical round index
j
.

2.

Access to
n
-qubit evolution
e^{t\mathcal{L}}
(Eq. (
133
)), where
|\mathcal{S}_{D}|=M_{D}
,
|\mathcal{S}_{H}|=M_{H}
, and thus
M\leq M_{D}^{2}+M_{H}
.

3.

The
\eta
-heavy dissipator structure
\mathcal{S}_{D,\eta}
, chosen so that, for the QEC reshaping with
V_{\mathrm{corr}}=\mathcal{S}_{D,\eta}
, every successful code draw induces an effective generator
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}
(Eq. (
136
)) whose bias and no-jump residual parameters satisfy
B_{\infty}\leq 2^{-(j+1)}/25
and
K_{\infty}\leq 2^{-(j+1)}/25
.

4.

Set
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathrm{HDD{}},\eta}^{(j-1)}\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{HDD},\eta}
of Hermitian Paulis identified in previous hierarchical rounds.

\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathrm{HDD{}},\eta}^{(j-1)}
is guaranteed to contain every Pauli
P_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{HDD},\eta}
with
|h_{k}|\geq 2^{-j}
.

5.

Confidence parameter
\delta_{j}\in(0,1)
.

6.

Access to
n+5\lceil\log_{2}(2M)\rceil
noiseless ancilla qubits in addition to the
n
probe qubits.

Output :

A set
\widehat{\mathcal{Y}}_{j}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{n}
with

|\widehat{\mathcal{Y}}_{j}|=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(M^{2}\right)

such that
\widehat{\mathcal{Y}}_{j}\supseteq\mathcal{Y}_{j}\setminus\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathrm{HDD{}},j-1}
with probability at least
1-\delta_{j}
.

Schedule

V_{\mathrm{corr}}\leftarrow\mathcal{S}_{D,\eta}

//
QEC target correctable set

V_{\mathrm{det}}\leftarrow\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathrm{HDD{}},\eta}^{(j-1)}

//
QEC target detectable set

N_{\mathrm{codes}}\leftarrow 32M\ln(\frac{4M_H}{\delta_j})

//
# of random QEC draws

N_{\mathrm{meas}}\leftarrow 210M^{2}

//
# of Bell-basis measurements per QEC

N_{\mathrm{reshape}}\leftarrow 600M^{2}4^{j}

//
# of error-correction cycles per sample

n^{\prime}\leftarrow n+\frac{5}{2}\left\lceil\log_{2}(2M)\right\rceil

//
left-register size including QEC ancillas

t\leftarrow 2^{j}/(2M)
,
\tau\leftarrow t/N_{\mathrm{reshape}}

//
interrogation time and reshape step

\widehat{\mathcal{Y}}_{j}\leftarrow\emptyset

//
candidate Pauli set

for

i\leftarrow 1

to

N_{\mathrm{codes}}

do

(\mathcal{G}_{i},\mathcal{R}_{i})\leftarrow\texttt{RandomQEC}(V_{\mathrm{det}},V_{\mathrm{corr}},M,\,\frac{\delta_{j}}{4N_{\mathrm{codes}}},2)

//
Alg.
1
stab. generator & recovery ch.

\Pi_{i}\leftarrow\prod_{G\in\mathcal{G}_{i}}(1+G)/2

//
code-space projector

for

r\leftarrow 1

to

N_{\mathrm{meas}}

do

Prepare
\ket{\Phi_0}\leftarrow\ket{\Phi_+}^{\otimes n^{\prime}}
on
L\otimes R

//
left and right registers

Project to the code corner algebra:

\rho_{\mathrm{prep}}\propto(\Pi_{i}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}})\ket{\Phi_0}\bra{\Phi_0}(\Pi_{i}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}})

//
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(M\right)
attempts

for

k\leftarrow 1

to

N_{\mathrm{reshape}}

do

Evolve the left register by
e^{\tau\mathcal{L}}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}

Apply
\mathcal{R}_{i}
to the left register

//
error detection and correction

Measure in the Bell basis; update candidates:

\widehat{\mathcal{Y}}_{j}\leftarrow\widehat{\mathcal{Y}}_{j}\cup\{\text{Pauli outcome}\}

\widehat{\mathcal{Y}}_{j}\leftarrow
nontrivial logical representatives from
\widehat{\mathcal{Y}}_{j}
acting only on the
n
probe qubits

//
I_{n^{\prime}-n}
on ancillas

return

\widehat{\mathcal{Y}}_{j}

Algorithm 5

HDD Hamiltonian candidate identification subroutine,
j
th round

The key ingredient of this algorithm is the following lemma, which we invoke on the reshaped dynamics.

Lemma E.6

(Hamiltonian Bell sampling under bias and residual dissipation)
.

Let
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}
be an
n^{\prime}
-qubit effective generator of the form in
Eq. (
136
), with bias and residual parameters
B_{\infty},K_{\infty}
as in
Eq. (
139
). Write it in the jump unraveling picture using Eq. (
139
) and Eq. (
138
):

\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}(\rho)=-iH_{\rm eff}\rho+i\rho H_{\rm eff}^{\dagger}+\sum_{w,m}\alpha_{wm}P_{w}\rho P_{m},\qquad H_{\rm eff}\coloneq H_{\rm tar}+H_{\rm bias}-\frac{i}{2}K_{\rm resid}.

Expand the target Hamiltonian in the Pauli basis as
H_{\mathrm{tar}}=\sum_{P_{m}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{H,tar}}}h_{m}P_{m}
, the bias as
H_{\mathrm{bias}}=\sum_{P_{m}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{H,bias}}}b_{m}P_{m}
and the residual non-Hermitian contribution as
K_{\mathrm{resid}}=\sum_{P_{m}\in\mathcal{S}_{K}}k_{m}P_{m}
, where

\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{H,tar}},\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{H,bias}}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{n^{\prime}}\setminus\{I\}
,
\mathcal{S}_{K}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{n^{\prime}}
and
\{h_{m}\},\{b_{m}\},\{k_{m}\}
are real coefficients.
Let
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{H,eff}}\coloneq\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{H,tar}}\cup\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{H,bias}}\cup\mathcal{S}_{K}
and assume
|\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{H,eff}}|\leq M
.

Fix an integer
j\geq 0
. Assume that
|h_{m}|\leq 2^{-j}
for every

P_{m}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{H,tar}}
and that a target Pauli

P_{r}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{H,tar}}
satisfies
|h_{r}|\geq 2^{-(j+1)}
. Assume that the bias and residual parameters satisfy
B_{\infty}\leq 2^{-(j+1)}/25
and
K_{\infty}\leq 2^{-(j+1)}/25.

Let
C
be a stabilizer code on
n^{\prime}
qubits with code projector
\Pi
and stabilizer subgroup

S\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{n^{\prime}}
. Let
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{eff}}\coloneq\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{H,eff}}\cup\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{D,res}}
and let
G_{\mathrm{eff}}\coloneq\langle\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{eff}}\rangle\subseteq\mathbb{P}_{n^{\prime}}
denote the subgroup generated by
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{eff}}
. Assume that all Paulis

P\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{eff}}
have zero syndrome
r(P)=0
with respect to
C
, and that no
nontrivial product of elements of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{eff}}
is proportional to a stabilizer; equivalently,
for every
Q\in G_{\mathrm{eff}}
,
Q\propto s\in S
implies
s=I_{n^{\prime}}
.

Let
\rho_{0}\coloneq\ket{\Phi_0}\bra{\Phi_0}
, where

\ket{\Phi_0}\coloneq\ket{\Phi_+}^{\otimes n^{\prime}}
and

\ket{\Phi_+}=(\ket{00}+\ket{11})/\sqrt{2}
. Let
\widetilde{\rho}
denote the state immediately before Bell-basis measurement,

\widetilde{\rho}\coloneq(e^{t\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}})\left(\frac{(\Pi\otimes I_{n^{\prime}})\rho_{0}(\Pi\otimes I_{n^{\prime}})}{\tr((\Pi\otimes I_{n'})\rho_0)}\right).

For any Pauli
P\in\mathbb{P}_{n^{\prime}}
, let

O_{P}\coloneq(P\otimes I_{n^{\prime}})\ket{\Phi_0}\bra{\Phi_0}(P^{\dagger}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}})

denote the corresponding Bell-measurement projector. Then, for

t=2^{j}/(2M)
, a single Bell-sampling experiment on
\widetilde{\rho}
satisfies

\displaystyle\Pr(\text{sample $P_r$ modulo stabilizers})

\displaystyle\coloneq\tr\left(\sum_{s\in S}O_{sP_{r}}\,\widetilde{\rho}\right)\geq\frac{1}{200M^{2}},

\displaystyle\Pr(\text{sample a stabilizer})

\displaystyle\coloneq\tr\left(\sum_{s\in S}O_{s}\,\widetilde{\rho}\right)\geq 1-\frac{1}{5M}.

Proof.

See Appendix
E.6
.
∎

Theorem E.7

(Round-
j
HDD candidate identification via Bell sampling)
.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be an
n
-qubit
M
-sparse Lindbladian as in Eq. (
133
). Assume black-box access to
e^{t\mathcal{L}}
. Fix a hierarchical round
j
and a failure probability
\delta_{j}\in(0,1)
. Let
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathrm{HDD{}},\eta}^{(j-1)}
denote the set of HDD terms identified in previous rounds and assume that
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathrm{HDD{}},\eta}^{(j-1)}
satisfies the round invariant Eq. (
135
).

In
Algorithm

5
, the QEC reshaping uses
V_{\mathrm{corr}}=\mathcal{S}_{D,\eta}
and
V_{\mathrm{det}}=\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathrm{HDD{}},\eta}^{(j-1)}
. For every successful QEC code draw (
Algorithm

1
), let
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}
denote the resulting
n^{\prime}
-qubit effective generator, written in the form of Eq. (
136
). Suppose the corresponding bias and no-jump residual parameters
B_{\infty},K_{\infty}
from Eq. (
139
) satisfy
B_{\infty}\leq 2^{-(j+1)}/25
and
K_{\infty}\leq 2^{-(j+1)}/25
. Then running
Algorithm

5
with schedule

t=\frac{2^{j}}{2M},\qquad N_{\mathrm{codes}}=\mathcal{O}\left(M\log(M/\delta_j)\right),\qquad N_{\mathrm{meas}}=\mathcal{O}\left(M^{2}\right),\qquad N_{\mathrm{reshape}}=\mathcal{O}\left(M^{2}4^{j}\right)

outputs a candidate set
\widehat{\mathcal{Y}}_{j}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{n}
satisfying the following guarantees:

1.

(Coverage and size) With probability at least
1-\delta_{j}
,

\widehat{\mathcal{Y}}_{j}\supseteq\mathcal{Y}_{j}\setminus\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathrm{HDD{}},\eta}^{(j-1)},\qquad|\widehat{\mathcal{Y}}_{j}|=\mathcal{O}\left(M^{2}\log(M/\delta_j)\right).

2.

(Total evolution time)
The total evolution time under
e^{t\mathcal{L}}
is

t_{\mathrm{tot}}=N_{\mathrm{codes}}N_{\mathrm{meas}}t=\mathcal{O}\left(2^{j}M^{2}\log(M/\delta_j)\right).

3.

(Time resolution)
The procedure only applies
e^{\mathcal{L}t}
for times at least

t_{\mathrm{res}}\coloneq\tau=\frac{t}{N_{\mathrm{reshape}}}\geq\Omega\left(\frac{1}{M^{3}2^{j}}\right).

4.

(Number of experiments)
The number of Bell-sampling experiments is

N_{\exp}=N_{\mathrm{codes}}N_{\mathrm{meas}}=\mathcal{O}\left(M^{3}\log(M/\delta_j)\right).

The number of interleaved QEC rounds is larger by a factor
\mathcal{O}\left(M^{2}4^{j}\right)
, while the number of raw state-preparation attempts is larger by a factor
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(M\right)
due to the probabilistic projection to the code space before reshaping.

5.

(Ancilla cost)
The procedure uses at most

n+5\lceil\log_{2}(2M)\rceil

ancillary qubits in addition to the
n
probe qubits.

6.

(Classical overhead)
The classical cost is dominated by repeated QEC and is

\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left((n+M)M^{5}4^{j}\right).

Proof.

Most of the proof concerns item
(1)
, namely coverage of the candidate set.
Items
(2)
–
(4)
follow directly from the schedule, and items
(5)
–
(6)
are verified at the end.

We first prove coverage. The candidate-identification subroutine has a
two-level coupon-collector
structure. The first level draws random error-correcting codes until every term
P\in\mathcal{Y}_{j}\setminus\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathrm{HDD{}},\eta}^{(j-1)}

survives the reshaping, i.e. has zero syndrome
r(P)=0
for at least one code. The second level, conditioned on a code in which a target term survives, repeats Bell sampling until one stabilizer-coset representative of that survived logical Pauli is observed.

We now analyze the first level. In
Algorithm

5
, the QEC reshaping map is

A_{\tau,H}^{I}\coloneq\mathcal{R}\circ\bigl(e^{\mathcal{L}\tau}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}\bigr)\circ\mathcal{C}_{\Pi},

(141)

where the original Lindbladian acts on the
n
system qubits and is trivially extended to the
n^{\prime}-n
QEC ancillas. The recovery map
\mathcal{R}
is obtained from a random stabilizer code drawn by
Algorithm

1
with
c=2
and inputs

V_{\mathrm{corr}}=\mathcal{S}_{D,\eta},\qquad V_{\mathrm{det}}=\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathrm{HDD{}},\eta}^{(j-1)},\qquad M_{\mathrm{det}}=M.

Let
E_{\mathrm{tar}}
denote the success event from
Theorem

C.14
: the sampled code corrects all Paulis in
V_{\mathrm{corr}}
and detects all Paulis in
V_{\mathrm{det}}
. By the same theorem, the number of stabilizer generators is
\lceil\log_{2}(2M)\rceil
, and therefore the stabilizer group satisfies

|S|\leq 4M.

(142)

Conditioned on
E_{\mathrm{tar}}
,
Section

C.2
and the effective-generator notation in Eq. (
136
) imply that the effective generator of the reshaping map in Eq. (
141
), realized on
\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H}_{n^{\prime}})
as in
Section

C.2
, can be written as

\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}(\rho)=-i[H_{\mathrm{tar}}+H_{\mathrm{bias}},\rho]+\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{resid}}(\rho)=-i\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{H}\\
r(P_{k})=0\end{subarray}}h_{k}[P_{k}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n},\rho]-i[H_{\mathrm{bias}},\rho]+\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{resid}}(\rho).

(143)

Here
H_{\mathrm{tar}}
is the Hamiltonian signal surviving QEC reshaping;
H_{\mathrm{bias}}
and
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{resid}}
are controlled by the bias and residual parameters
B_{\infty}
and
K_{\infty}
from Eq. (
139
). The explicit expression for
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}
is given in Eq. (
70
).

For a nonidentity Hermitian Pauli
P
, let
A_{P}
denote the event that
P
has zero syndrome under the sampled checks. Recall the target-suppression set from Eq. (
76
). With the present choices of
V_{\mathrm{corr}}
and
V_{\mathrm{det}}
, it becomes

\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{tar}}\coloneq V_{\mathrm{det}}\cup V_{\mathrm{corr}}\cup\{\operatorname{Herm}(P^{\prime}P^{\prime\prime}):P^{\prime},P^{\prime\prime}\in V_{\mathrm{corr}},\ P^{\prime}\neq P^{\prime\prime}\}=\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathrm{HDD{}},\eta}^{(j-1)}\cup\mathfrak{D}_{\eta}.

By
Theorem

C.14
with
c=2
and
M_{\mathrm{det}}=M
, any given
P_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{H}\setminus\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{tar}}=\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{HDD},\eta}\setminus\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathrm{HDD{}},\eta}^{(j-1)}\supseteq\mathcal{Y}_{j}\setminus\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathrm{HDD{}},\eta}^{(j-1)}
survives with conditional probability

p_{\mathrm{surv}}\coloneq\Pr[A_{P_{k}}\mid E_{\mathrm{tar}}]\geq\frac{1}{8M}.

(144)

This completes the first coupon-collector layer.

To quantify the reshaping error, we use
Section

C.4
with
\tau\coloneq t/N_{\mathrm{reshape}}
:

\|(A_{\tau,H}^{I})^{N_{\mathrm{reshape}}}-e^{t\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}}\circ\mathcal{C}_{\Pi}\|_{\diamond}\leq\frac{t^{2}(\|\mathcal{L}\|_{\diamond}^{2}+\|\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}\|_{\diamond}^{2})}{2N_{\mathrm{reshape}}}\leq\frac{4t^{2}M^{2}}{N_{\mathrm{reshape}}}.

(145)

thus, going forward in our analysis, we calculate the measurement statistics as if we had access to
e^{t\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}}
and then account for the reshaping error separately.

For the second level of the coupon collector, suppose that the
i
-th QEC code draw satisfies
E_{\mathrm{tar}}
, and fix a particular
P_{r}\in\mathcal{Y}_{j}\setminus\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathrm{HDD{}},\eta}^{(j-1)}
that survives this draw, i.e.
A_{P_{r}}
holds. We are interested in the probability of measuring
P_{r}
using Bell sampling. For that, we use
Section

E.2
, but first we argue why the assumptions of this lemma are satisfied in our setting.

Conditioned on
E_{\mathrm{tar}}
, the QEC-reshaped generator is
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}
from Eq. (
143
), which has the form required by
Section

E.2
. The effective Hamiltonian support has size at most
M
: every Hamiltonian term in
H_{\mathrm{tar}}
is inherited from a nonzero Hamiltonian coefficient, and the terms in
H_{\mathrm{bias}}
and
K_{\mathrm{resid}}
are inherited from dissipator cross term in the original
M
-sparse Lindbladian (see Eq. (
70
)).

The coefficient assumptions also hold. Since
V_{\mathrm{det}}=\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathrm{HDD{}},\eta}^{(j-1)}
,
Section

C.2
removes all previously learned Hamiltonian-Disjoint-from-Dissipator terms from
H_{\mathrm{tar}}
. Combining this with the round invariant Eq. (
135
), every Pauli term appearing in
H_{\mathrm{tar}}
has coefficient at most
2^{-j}
. On the other hand, because
P_{r}\in\mathcal{Y}_{j}
, its coefficient satisfies
|h_{r}|>2^{-(j+1)}
. Finally, the theorem assumes the Bell-admissible bias and residual bounds
B_{\infty}\leq 2^{-(j+1)}/25
and
K_{\infty}\leq 2^{-(j+1)}/25
.

We also need to verify that no nontrivial product of effective Hamiltonian Pauli terms is proportional to a stabilizer. This is guaranteed by the
Z
-padding in
Algorithm

1
: every nonidentity stabilizer
\sigma\in S\setminus\{I\}
acts nontrivially on the padding ancillas, while all Pauli terms inherited from the original Lindbladian act trivially on these ancillas. Hence, for every
Q\in G_{\mathrm{eff}}
, the implication
Q\propto s\in S\Rightarrow s=I_{n^{\prime}}
holds, as required by
Section

E.2
.

We now instantiate the Bell-sampling experiment. Let
\rho_{0}\coloneq\ket{\Phi_0}\bra{\Phi_0}
, where
\ket{\Phi_0}\coloneq\ket{\Phi_+}^{\otimes n^{\prime}}
and
\ket{\Phi_+}=(\ket{00}+\ket{11})/\sqrt{2}
. The left half of
\rho_{0}
is projected into the code corner algebra:

\rho_{\mathrm{code}}\coloneq\frac{(\Pi_{i}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}})\rho_{0}(\Pi_{i}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}})}{\tr((\Pi_i\otimes I_{n'})\rho_0)}.

Operationally, this is implemented by measuring the stabilizers on the left register and postselecting on the trivial syndrome. Using Eq. (
142
), the success probability of this post-selection is

\tr((\Pi_i\otimes I_{n'})\rho_0)=\frac{1}{|S|}\geq\frac{1}{4M}.

(146)

For the ideal effective evolution under

(e^{t\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}})
with
t=2^{j}/(2M)
,
Section

E.2
gives

\Pr_{\mathrm{ideal}}(\text{sample $P_{r}$ modulo stabilizers})\geq\frac{1}{200M^{2}}.

(147)

Accounting for the reshaping diamond norm error from Eq. (
145
) and setting
N_{\mathrm{reshape}}\geq 600M^{2}\,4^{j}
, we obtain

\Pr(\text{sample $P_r$ modulo stabilizers}\mid A_{P_r}\wedge E_{\mathrm{tar}})\geq\frac{1}{200M^{2}}-\frac{1}{600M^{2}}=\frac{1}{300M^{2}}.

(148)

This completes the second coupon-collector layer.

We can guarantee the success of the algorithm by enforcing the following four events:

\mathsf{A}

–all
N_{\mathrm{codes}}
random codes suppress all previously learned Paulis, i.e. event
E_{\mathrm{tar}}

\rightarrow
Eq. (
150
);

\mathsf{B}

- all
N_{\mathrm{codes}}N_{\mathrm{meas}}
experiments succeed at preparing the code-projected Bell states
\rightarrow
Eq. (
151
) ;

\mathsf{C}

- each Pauli
P\in\mathcal{Y}_{j}\setminus\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathrm{HDD{}},j-1}
is covered at least once (this is the two-level coupon collector)
\rightarrow
Eq. (
149
);

\mathsf{D}

- the number of non-trivial Bell-sampling outcomes (hence
|\widehat{\mathcal{Y}}_{j}|
) is at most
\mathcal{O}\left(M^{2}\log(M/\delta_j)\right)

\rightarrow
Eq. (
152
);

First, let us lower bound the probability that each Pauli
P\in\mathcal{Y}_{j}\setminus\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathrm{HDD{}},j-1}
is covered at least once (event
\mathsf{C}
). Fix a single
P_{r}\in\mathcal{Y}_{j}\setminus\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathrm{HDD{}},j-1}
. Then, combining Eq. (
144
) and Eq. (
148
) and setting
N_{\mathrm{meas}}\geq 210M^{2}=\mathcal{O}\left(M^{2}\right)
:

\Pr(\text{$P_r$ is covered by a single code experiment})=p_{\mathrm{surv}}\biggl(1-(1-\Pr(\text{sample $P_r$}))^{N_{\mathrm{meas}}}\biggr)\geq\frac{p_{\mathrm{surv}}}{2}\geq\frac{1}{16M}

Using a union bound over elements of
\mathcal{Y}_{j}\setminus\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathrm{HDD{}},j-1}
and
N_{\mathrm{codes}}\geq 16M\ln(\frac{4M_H}{\delta_j})
, we get:

\Pr[\text{$\geq 1$ Pauli from $\mathcal{Y}_{j}\setminus\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathrm{HDD{}},j-1}$ not covered}]\leq M_{H}\Pr(\text{given Pauli not covered})\leq M_{H}\left(1-\frac{1}{16M}\right)^{N_{\mathrm{codes}}}\leq\frac{\delta_{j}}{4}

Hence,

\Pr(\mathsf{C}|\mathsf{A}\wedge\mathsf{B})=1-\Pr[\text{$\geq 1$ Pauli from $\mathcal{Y}_{j}\setminus\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathrm{HDD{}},j-1}$ not covered}]\geq 1-\delta_{j}/4

(149)

The event
\mathsf{A}
is guaranteed to succeed with probability at least
1-\delta_{j}/4
by running each random code construction
Algorithm

1
with probability of failure
\delta_{j}/(4N_{\mathrm{codes}})
. By
Theorem

C.14
and a union bound, we get:

\Pr[\mathsf{A}]\geq 1-\frac{\delta_{j}}{4}

(150)

Let
N_{\mathrm{exp}}\coloneq N_{\mathrm{codes}}N_{\mathrm{meas}}=\mathcal{O}\left(M^{3}\log(M_H/\delta_j)\right)
. To guarantee that the code-space-projected state preparation succeeds for each of the
N_{\mathrm{exp}}
experiments (event
\mathsf{B}
), we attempt the preparation + projection
N_{\mathrm{prep}}=4M\ln(4N_{\mathrm{exp}}/\delta_j)
times per experiment. Then, using a union bound over experiments and and Eq. (
146
), we get:

\Pr[\neg\mathsf{B}]\leq N_{\mathrm{codes}}N_{\mathrm{meas}}\left(1-\frac{1}{4M}\right)^{N_{\mathrm{prep}}}\leq\frac{\delta_{j}}{4}

(151)

For event
\mathsf{D}
, we follow
121
. Let
\mathbf{1}_{i}
be an indicator r.v. for whether the
i
-th round Bell sample is non-trivial (i.e. a non-stabilizer). By
Section

E.2
and Eq. (
145
),
\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{1}_{i}]\leq 1/(5M)+1/(600M^{2})\leq 1/(4.9M)
. The size of the the candidate set after removing trivial samples is at most
X\coloneq\sum_{i=1}^{N_{\mathrm{exp}}}\mathbf{1}_{i}
. Since
\alpha\coloneq N_{\mathrm{exp}}/(4.9M)\gg 3\ln(4/\delta_j)
, by multiplicative Chernoff bound:

\Pr(\neg\mathsf{D} \mid\mathsf{A} \wedge\mathsf{B})\coloneq\Pr(X \ge 2 \alpha)\leq\Pr(X \ge 2 \frac{\alpha}{\mathbb{E}[X]} \mathbb{E}[X])\leq\exp(-\frac{ \alpha}{3})\leq\frac{\delta_{j}}{4}

(152)

Thus with high probability, the size of
|\widehat{\mathcal{Y}}_{j}|
is at most
\mathcal{O}\left(N_{\mathrm{exp}}/M\right)=\mathcal{O}\left(M^{2}\log(M/\delta_j)\right)
.

Combining Eq. (
150
), Eq. (
151
), Eq. (
149
), and Eq. (
152
), we get:

\Pr[\text{success}]\geq\Pr(\mathsf A \wedge\mathsf B \wedge\mathsf C \wedge\mathsf D)\geq 1-\Pr(\neg\mathsf A)-\Pr(\neg\mathsf B)-\Pr(\neg\mathsf C| \mathsf A \wedge\mathsf B)-\Pr(\neg\mathsf D|\mathsf A \wedge\mathsf B)\geq 1-4\,\frac{\delta_{j}}{4}=1-\delta_{j}

where we used the conditional probability and union bound. This concludes the proof of correctness.

We now prove item
(5)
. By
Theorem

C.14
with
c=2
and
M_{\mathrm{det}}=M
, our QEC construction uses at most
5/2\lceil\log_{2}(2M)\rceil
ancillas for both stabilizers and syndrome extraction. Forming Bell pairs uses at most extra
n+5/2\lceil\log_{2}(2M)\rceil
ancillas. Thus the total number is
n+5\lceil\log_{2}(2M)\rceil
.

The classical overhead (item
(6)
) is dominated by repeated syndrome extraction + correction and is equal to:

\text{Classical cost}=\mathcal{O}\left((|S|+n)N_{\mathrm{exp}}N_{\mathrm{reshape}}\right)=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left((n+M)M^{5}4^{j}\right)

This concludes the proof of the theorem.
∎

E.3
Coefficient learning via robust frequency estimation

In this section, we describe Hamiltonian-Disjoint-from-Dissipator coefficient learning at the Heisenberg limit. Given the candidate set
\widehat{\mathcal{Y}}_{j}
produced by Bell sampling, the goal is twofold: estimate the coefficients of the true round-
j
terms and remove the false positives. This produces the updated set
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathrm{HDD{}},\eta}^{(j)}\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{HDD},\eta}
needed to maintain the round invariant in Eq. (
135
).

For each candidate Pauli
P_{a}
, the coefficient-learning routine uses QEC reshaping followed by a partial logical twirl, as in Eq. (
67
), to isolate the corresponding logical Hamiltonian signal. In the notation of Eq. (
136
), the resulting effective generator has target Hamiltonian
H_{\mathrm{tar}}=h_{a}\overline{P}_{a}
, where
\overline{P}_{a}=P_{a}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}
, together with a bias Hamiltonian and residual dissipator of sizes
B_{\infty}
and
R_{\diamond}
from Eq. (
139
). When
B_{\infty}
and
R_{\diamond}
are below the error budget specified below, the residual dynamics gives only a controlled perturbation of the ideal single-frequency signal. We can then apply Robust Frequency Estimation (RFE) within the code to estimate
h_{a}
with Heisenberg-limited interaction time. This extends the Hamiltonian coefficient-learning methods of
72
;
70
;
97
;
93
to the present dissipative setting.

At a high level, RFE is a binary search algorithm that starts with an initial coefficient guess
\hat{h}_{a}\in[-A,A]
and iteratively narrows down this range using a test statistic
F(t)
built from expectation values; after
\mathcal{O}\left(\log(1/\varepsilon)\right)
steps it returns
\hat{h}_{a}
with
|\hat{h}_{a}-h_{a}|\leq\varepsilon
. The main requirement on
F(t)
is the separation property in Lemma
E.3
(copied from
93
;
70
), which our algorithm satisfies for encoded (logical) dynamics.

Lemma E.8

(Lemma 10 of
93
)
.

Let
\mu\in[a,b]
. Let
F(t)
be a random variable such that

|F(t)-e^{i\mu t}|\leq\tfrac{1}{2}.

Then we can correctly conclude whether
\mu\in[a,(a+2b)/3]
or
\mu\in[(2a+b)/3,b]
using a single sample of
F(t)
at
t=\pi/(b-a)
.

Proof.

Set
t=\pi/(b-a)
and define the real-valued function

f(\mu):=\sin\left(\frac{\pi}{b-a}\left(\mu-\frac{a+b}{2}\right)\right).

Apply phase shift
e^{-i\frac{(a+b)\pi}{2(b-a)}}
to
(F(t)-e^{i\mu t})
and take the imaginary part:

\left|\operatorname{Im}\left(e^{-i\frac{(a+b)\pi}{2(b-a)}}F\left(\frac{\pi}{b-a}\right)\right)-f(\mu)\right|\leq\frac{1}{2}.

This centers the signal
F(t)
such that the sign of its imaginary part distinguishes between the two cases:

\displaystyle\text{If }\operatorname{Im}\left(e^{-i\frac{(a+b)\pi}{2(b-a)}}F\left(\frac{\pi}{b-a}\right)\right)\leq 0,\ \text{then }f(\mu)\leq\frac{1}{2},\ \text{which implies }\mu\in\left[a,\frac{a+2b}{3}\right].

\displaystyle\text{If }\operatorname{Im}\left(e^{-i\frac{(a+b)\pi}{2(b-a)}}F\left(\frac{\pi}{b-a}\right)\right)>0,\ \text{then }f(\mu)\geq-\frac{1}{2},\ \text{which implies }\mu\in\left[\frac{2a+b}{3},b\right].

∎

With QEC-based Lindbladian reshaping (
Section

C.2
and Eq. (
136
)) and the RFE (
Section

E.3
), we now have all the necessary ingredients for learning HDD coefficients.

Inputs :

[label=(0), leftmargin=*, nosep]

1.

Candidate Pauli
P_{a}\in\mathcal{P}_{n}\setminus\mathfrak{D}_{\eta}
whose Hamiltonian coefficient
h_{a}
we wish to learn.

2.

Accuracy parameter
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
.

3.

Confidence parameter
\delta_{\mathrm{coeff}}\in(0,1)
.

4.

QEC code with stabilizer
S
and recovery channel
\mathcal{R}
under which
\overline{P}_{a}\coloneq P_{a}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}
is a nontrivial logical Pauli, and the resulting
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}
from Eq. (
136
) has bias and residual parameters
B_{\infty}\leq\varepsilon/2
and
R_{\diamond}\leq\varepsilon/(4\sqrt{2}\pi)
.

5.

Access to
n
-qubit evolution
e^{t\mathcal{L}}
(Eq. (
133
)), where the total Lindbladian sparsity is
M
.

6.

Access to
\mathcal{O}\left(\log M\right)
noiseless ancilla qubits used for QEC padding and check measurements.

Output :

An estimator
\widehat{h}_{a}
such that
\absolutevalue{\widehat h_a-h_a}\leq\varepsilon
with probability at least
1-\delta_{\mathrm{coeff}}
.

Schedule

(x,y)\leftarrow(-2,2)

//
initial range ansatz

L\leftarrow\left\lceil\log_{3/2}(4/\varepsilon)\right\rceil

//
# of RFE iterations

N_{\mathrm{exp}}\leftarrow\left\lceil 128\ln(4L/\delta_{\mathrm{coeff}})\right\rceil

//
# of samples per iteration

\overline{T}\leftarrow\text{any logical Pauli s.t. }\anticommutator{\overline T}{\overline P_a}=0

//
used for state preparation

for

i\leftarrow 0

to

L-1

do

t_{i}\leftarrow(\pi/4)(3/2)^{i}

//
physical evolution time

N_{\mathrm{reshape}}\leftarrow\left\lceil 32\sqrt{2}\,M^{2}t_{i}^{2}\right\rceil
,
\tau_{i}\leftarrow t_{i}/N_{\mathrm{reshape}}

//
# reshape steps & step length

\hat{o}_{s}^{+},\hat{o}_{s}^{-}\leftarrow 0,0

for

r\leftarrow 1

to

N_{\mathrm{exp}}

do

Prepare
\rho_{c}\leftarrow\mathcal{R}(\ket{0}\bra{0}^{\otimes n^{\prime}})

//
state-prep to the code

v\leftarrow
record logical measurement of
\overline{T};
the new state is
\rho_{v}

//
+1
or
-1

\rho(t_{i})\leftarrow\prod_{k=1}^{N_{\mathrm{reshape}}}(\mathcal{U}_{\overline{Q}_{k}}\circ\mathcal{R}\circ e^{\mathcal{L}\tau_{i}}\circ\mathcal{U}_{\overline{Q}_{k}})(\rho_{v})

//
\overline{Q}_{k}\in\overline{\mathcal{K}}_{\overline{P}_{a}}
uniformly

\hat{o}_{s}^{+}\leftarrow\hat{o}_{s}^{+}+v\,\cdot
logical measurement of
\overline{T}
on
\rho(t_{i})

//
for half of
N_{\mathrm{exp}}
sample "cosine"

\hat{o}_{s}^{-}\leftarrow\hat{o}_{s}^{-}+v\,\cdot
logical measurement of
(i\overline{T}\,\overline{P}_{a})
on
\rho(t_{i})

//
for half of
N_{\mathrm{exp}}
sample "sine"

\widehat{F}\leftarrow(\hat{o}_{s}^{+}+i\,\hat{o}_{s}^{-})/(N_{\mathrm{exp}}/2)

//
estimator of
e^{2i\omega_{a}t_{i}}

if

\operatorname{Im}\left(e^{-i(x+y)t_{i}}\,\widehat{F}\right)\leq 0

then

y\leftarrow(x+2y)/3

else

x\leftarrow(2x+y)/3

return

\widehat{h}_{a}\leftarrow(x+y)/2

Algorithm 6

HDD Hamiltonian coefficient learning subroutine

Theorem E.9

(HDD coefficient learning via QEC reshaping and RFE)
.

Fix a target accuracy
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and failure probability
\delta_{\mathrm{coeff}}\in(0,1)
. Fix a candidate Pauli
P_{a}\in\mathcal{P}_{n}\setminus\mathfrak{D}_{\eta}
. Assume that the QEC reshaping plus partial logical twirl used in
Algorithm

6
produces an
n^{\prime}
-qubit effective generator
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}
of the form in Eq. (
136
), with target Hamiltonian

H_{\mathrm{tar}}=h_{a}\overline{P}_{a}
and bias
H_{\mathrm{bias}}=b_{a}\overline{P}_{a}
, where
\overline{P}_{a}\coloneq P_{a}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}\in\mathcal{P}_{n^{\prime}}
is a nontrivial logical Pauli. Assume additionally that the corresponding bias and residual parameters
B_{\infty},R_{\diamond}
from Eq. (
139
) satisfy
B_{\infty}\leq\varepsilon/2
and
R_{\diamond}\leq\varepsilon/(4\sqrt{2}\pi)
.

Then running
Algorithm

6
outputs an estimator
\widehat{h}_{a}
satisfying the following guarantees:

1.

(Accuracy) With probability
\geq 1-\delta_{\mathrm{coeff}}
,

|\widehat{h}_{a}-h_{a}|\leq\varepsilon

2.

(Total evolution time) The total evolution time under

e^{t\mathcal{L}}
is

t_{\mathrm{tot}}\;=\;\mathcal{O}\biggl({\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left[\log\biggl(\frac{1}{\delta_{\mathrm{coeff}}}\biggr)+\log\log\biggl(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\biggr)\right]}\biggr),

3.

(Time resolution) It only ever applies
e^{\mathcal{L}t}
for
t\geq t_{\mathrm{res}}
, where

t_{\mathrm{res}}\;=\;\min_{i}\frac{t_{i}}{N_{\mathrm{reshape}}(t_{i})}\;=\;\Omega\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{M^{2}}\right),

4.

(Number of experiments) The total number of state preparations / evolutions is

N_{\exp}^{\mathrm{(tot)}}\;=\;L\,N_{\exp}\;=\;\mathcal{O}\left(\log\biggl(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\biggr)\left[\log\biggl(\frac{1}{\delta_{\mathrm{coeff}}}\biggr)+\log\log\biggl(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\biggr)\right]\right).

5.

(Ancilla cost) The procedure uses
\mathcal{O}(\log M)
ancillary qubits for code padding and check measurements (the same code reused from the structure-learning round in which
P_{a}
was sampled).

6.

(Classical overhead) The classical cost is:

\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(n^{2}M^{2}/\varepsilon^{2}\right)

Proof.

Matching the QEC reshaping plus logical twirl generator Eq. (
74
) with the effective-generator form from Eq. (
136
), we can write

\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}(\rho)=-i[(h_{a}+b_{a})\overline{P}_{a},\rho]+\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{resid}}(\rho),\qquad|b_{a}|\leq B_{\infty},\quad\|\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{resid}}\|_{\diamond}\leq R_{\diamond}.

Let
\omega_{a}\coloneq h_{a}+b_{a}
. We first analyze RFE under the ideal unitary evolution
e^{-i\omega_{a}\overline{P}_{a}t}
, and then account for finite-step Lindbladian reshaping and the residual Lindbladian. Since
B_{\infty}\leq\varepsilon/2
, estimating
\omega_{a}
to accuracy
\varepsilon/2
suffices to estimate
h_{a}
to accuracy
\varepsilon
.

To perform iterative improvements via
Section

E.3
, we want to prepare a measurement statistic
\widehat{F}(s_{i})
that approximates
e^{i\omega_{a}s_{i}}
. To get a better sense of how
F(s_{i})
is generated, consider a toy example of learning a single-qubit reshaped Hamiltonian
H=\omega Z
. By preparing an equal superposition of the
+1
and
-1
eigenstates of
H
, namely
|\Phi_{0}\rangle=|+\rangle
, evolving by
e^{-i\omega Zs/2}
, and measuring the
X
and
Y
observables, one obtains
\langle X\rangle=\cos(\omega s)
and
\langle Y\rangle=\sin(\omega s)
. Thus
F(s)=\langle X\rangle+i\langle Y\rangle=e^{i\omega s}
.

Within
Algorithm

6
, for
L\coloneq\left\lceil\log_{3/2}\left(4/\varepsilon\right)\right\rceil
, we set

s_{i}\coloneq\frac{\pi}{2}\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^{i},\qquad t_{i}\coloneq\frac{s_{i}}{2}=\frac{\pi}{4}\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^{i},\qquad i=0,\ldots,L-1.

With this choice, RFE narrows the initial interval to width at most
\varepsilon
, and hence estimates
\omega_{a}
to accuracy
\varepsilon/2
.

For a general
n
-qubit target Pauli
P_{a}
, choose a Hermitian logical Pauli
\overline{T}
such that
\{\overline{T},\overline{P}_{a}\}=0
, which exists by
Section

B.4.4
. It can be obtained in the symplectic picture (Appendix
B.4.4
) by solving an under-specified system of linear equations encoding the commutation relation of
\overline{T}
with the stabilizer matrix and with the symplectic representation of
\overline{P}_{a}
.

Let
\mathcal{R}
denote the recovery map of the QEC used by
Algorithm

6
. We initialize an arbitrary state in the code space,

\rho_{0}=\mathcal{R}(\ket{0}\bra{0}^{\otimes n^{\prime}}).

We then measure the logical Pauli
\overline{T}
using the projectors

\Pi_{v}^{T}\coloneq\frac{I+v\overline{T}}{2},\qquad v\in\{\pm 1\},

record the measurement outcome
v
, and obtain

\rho_{v}=\frac{\Pi_{v}^{T}\rho_{0}\Pi_{v}^{T}}{\tr(\Pi_v^T\rho_0)}.

(153)

Under ideal unitary evolution by
e^{-i\omega_{a}\overline{P}_{a}t_{i}}
, anticommutation gives

\expectationvalue{\overline T}_{\rho_{v}(t_{i})}=v\cos(\omega_a s_i),\qquad\expectationvalue{i\overline T\,\overline P_a}_{\rho_{v}(t_{i})}=v\sin(\omega_a s_i).

(154)

Here we used
s_{i}=2t_{i}
,
\expectationvalue{\overline T}_{\rho_{v}}=v
, and
\expectationvalue{\overline T\overline P_a}_{\rho_{v}}=0
. Thus the ideal complex statistic is

F_{\mathrm{ideal}}(s_{i})\coloneq v\left(\expectationvalue{\overline T}_{\rho_{v}(t_{i})}+i\expectationvalue{i\overline T\,\overline P_a}_{\rho_{v}(t_{i})}\right)=e^{i\omega_{a}s_{i}}.

We now account for shot noise. For each RFE iteration, we estimate the two Pauli observables
\overline{T}
and
i\overline{T}\,\overline{P}_{a}
using
N_{\mathrm{exp}}/2
samples each. Let
X_{\ell},Y_{\ell}\in[-1,1]
denote the corresponding measurement outcomes. By Hoeffding’s inequality,

\Pr\left(\left|\frac{2}{N_{\mathrm{exp}}}\sum_{\ell=1}^{N_{\mathrm{exp}}/2}X_{\ell}-\expectationvalue{\overline T}\right|\geq\frac{1}{4\sqrt{2}}\right)\leq 2\exp\left(-\frac{N_{\mathrm{exp}}}{128}\right),

(155)

and the same bound holds for the
Y_{\ell}
samples. Taking a union bound over
2L
observable estimates, it suffices to choose

N_{\mathrm{exp}}=128\ln\left(\frac{4L}{\delta_{\mathrm{coeff}}}\right)=\mathcal{O}\left(\log\left(\frac{1}{\delta_{\mathrm{coeff}}}\right)+\log\log\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)\right).

(156)

With probability at least
1-\delta_{\mathrm{coeff}}
, the shot-noise contribution to every complex statistic appearing in RFE is at most

E_{\mathrm{shot}}\leq\sqrt{\frac{1}{(4\sqrt{2})^{2}}+\frac{1}{(4\sqrt{2})^{2}}}=\frac{1}{4}.

(157)

We next bound the error from finite-step Lindbladian reshaping and from the residual Lindbladian. Let
\widetilde{B}_{t_{i}}
denote the implemented QEC reshaping plus partial logical twirl channel at physical time
t_{i}
, and let

B_{t_{i}}(\cdot)\coloneq e^{-i[\omega_{a}\overline{P}_{a},\cdot]t_{i}}(\cdot).

By
Section

C.4
,
\|\mathcal{L}\|_{\diamond},\|\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}\|_{\diamond}\leq 2M
, and Duhamel’s formula applied to
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}=-i[\omega_{a}\overline{P}_{a},\cdot]+\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{resid}}
,

\|\widetilde{B}_{t_{i}}-B_{t_{i}}\|_{\diamond}\leq\|\widetilde{B}_{t_{i}}-e^{t_{i}\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}}\|_{\diamond}+\|e^{t_{i}\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}}-B_{t_{i}}\|_{\diamond}\leq\frac{4M^{2}t_{i}^{2}}{N_{\mathrm{reshape}}(t_{i})}+t_{i}\|\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{resid}}\|_{\diamond}\leq\frac{4M^{2}t_{i}^{2}}{N_{\mathrm{reshape}}(t_{i})}+t_{i}R_{\diamond}.

Since
i<L
implies
t_{i}<\pi/(2\varepsilon)
and
R_{\diamond}\leq\varepsilon/(4\sqrt{2}\pi)
, the residual contribution is at most
1/(8\sqrt{2})
. Choosing

N_{\mathrm{reshape}}(t_{i})\geq 32\sqrt{2}\,M^{2}t_{i}^{2}

makes the finite-step contribution at most
1/(8\sqrt{2})
. Thus, for either
P_{\mathrm{obs}}\in\{\overline{T},i\overline{T}\,\overline{P}_{a}\}
,

\left|\tr\left(P_{\mathrm{obs}}(\widetilde{B}_{t_{i}}-B_{t_{i}})(\rho_{v})\right)\right|\leq\frac{1}{4\sqrt{2}}.

(158)

Combining the errors for both observables,

E_{\mathrm{reshape}}\leq\sqrt{\frac{1}{(4\sqrt{2})^{2}}+\frac{1}{(4\sqrt{2})^{2}}}=\frac{1}{4}.

(159)

Define the empirical statistic

\widehat{F}(s_{i})\coloneq v\left(\frac{2}{N_{\mathrm{exp}}}\sum_{\ell=1}^{N_{\mathrm{exp}}/2}X_{\ell}+i\frac{2}{N_{\mathrm{exp}}}\sum_{\ell=1}^{N_{\mathrm{exp}}/2}Y_{\ell}\right).

Combining Eq. (
157
) and Eq. (
159
), we obtain, for every RFE iteration simultaneously with probability at least
1-\delta_{\mathrm{coeff}}
,

|\widehat{F}(s_{i})-e^{i\omega_{a}s_{i}}|\leq E_{\mathrm{shot}}+E_{\mathrm{reshape}}\leq\frac{1}{2}.

Therefore the assumptions of
Section

E.3
hold for estimating
\omega_{a}
. After
L=\lceil\log_{3/2}(2/\varepsilon)\rceil
iterations, RFE returns an estimate
\widehat{\omega}_{a}
satisfying
|\widehat{\omega}_{a}-\omega_{a}|\leq\varepsilon/2
. The algorithm outputs
\widehat{h}_{a}\coloneq\widehat{\omega}_{a}
, and hence

|\widehat{h}_{a}-h_{a}|\leq|\widehat{\omega}_{a}-\omega_{a}|+|b_{a}|\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}+B_{\infty}\leq\varepsilon.

This proves the accuracy guarantee item
(1)
.

We now prove item
(2)
, the total evolution time. The total evolution time under
e^{\mathcal{L}t}
is

t_{\mathrm{tot}}=N_{\mathrm{exp}}\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}t_{i}=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\left[\log\left(\frac{1}{\delta_{\mathrm{coeff}}}\right)+\log\log\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)\right]\right),

where we used Eq. (
156
) and
\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}t_{i}=\mathcal{O}(1/\varepsilon)
.

The time resolution (item
(3)
) is dictated by the smallest reshaping step:

t_{\mathrm{res}}\coloneq\min_{i}\frac{t_{i}}{N_{\mathrm{reshape}}(t_{i})}=\Omega\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{M^{2}}\right),

using
t_{i}<\pi/(2\varepsilon)
and
N_{\mathrm{reshape}}(t_{i})=\mathcal{O}\left(M^{2}t_{i}^{2}\right)
.

For the total number of experiments (item
(4)
), each of the
L
RFE iterations uses
N_{\mathrm{exp}}
state preparations/evolutions, so

N_{\exp}^{\mathrm{(tot)}}=L\,N_{\mathrm{exp}}=\mathcal{O}\left(\log\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)\left[\log\left(\frac{1}{\delta_{\mathrm{coeff}}}\right)+\log\log\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)\right]\right).

The ancilla cost (item
(5)
) is due to the QEC ancilla padding and check measurement overhead.
Algorithm

6
uses the same QEC code as the structure-learning round in which
P_{a}
was sampled. Hence it inherits the QEC ancilla cost of
\mathcal{O}(\log M)
.

We now prove item
(6)
, the classical overhead, which consists of two parts. First, we need to pre-compute
\sum_{i}N_{\mathrm{reshape}}(t_{i})
different logical Paulis that we use for logical twirling. This corresponds to randomly sampling vectors in the symplectic binary picture from the null space of the stabilizer matrix (with an appended row corresponding to
P_{a}
) times
\Lambda
(see Appendix
B.4.4
). Let
s=\log(2M)
be the number of generators of the QEC from
Theorem

C.14
. Before sampling, there is a one-time pre-processing consisting of taking the matrix product with
\Lambda
(
\mathcal{O}\left(n^{2}s\right)
) and then performing Gaussian elimination (
\mathcal{O}\left(ns^{2}\right)
). After that, sampling in the null space is done by drawing
(2n-(s+1))
iid fair bits, then doing matrix-vector multiplication
\mathcal{O}\left(n^{2}\right)
. Therefore, the total classical computation needed to prepare logical Paulis from
\overline{\mathcal{K}}_{\overline{P}_{a}}
is

\mathcal{O}\left(n^{2}s+ns^{2}\right)+\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}N_{\mathrm{reshape}}(t_{i})\cdot\mathcal{O}\left(n^{2}\right)=\mathcal{O}\left(n^{2}\right)\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}N_{\mathrm{reshape}}(t_{i})=\mathcal{O}\left(n^{2}M^{2}\right)\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^{2i}=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{n^{2}M^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right).

Storing these logical Paulis takes
\mathcal{O}\left(n\cdot\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}N_{\mathrm{reshape}}(t_{i})\right)=\mathcal{O}\left(nM^{2}/\varepsilon^{2}\right)
classical space.

The second part is the real-time syndrome extraction, lookup-table decoding, and merging of the recovery unitary with the logical Pauli from
\overline{\mathcal{K}}_{\overline{P}_{a}}
. The real-time classical processing cost is

\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(n\right)\cdot\sum_{i=0}^{L-1}N_{\mathrm{reshape}}(t_{i})=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(nM^{2}/\varepsilon^{2}\right).

Hence the classical processing is dominated by preparing the logical Paulis from
\overline{\mathcal{K}}_{\overline{P}_{a}}
and equals

\mathrm{Classical\,overhead}=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(n^{2}M^{2}/\varepsilon^{2}\right).

∎

E.4
Full HDD hierarchical learning algorithm

In this section, we combine candidate identification (
Algorithm

5
) and coefficient learning (
Algorithm

6
) into the full Hamiltonian-Disjoint-from-Dissipator learning
Algorithm

7
according to Appendix
E.1
. Even though the algorithm closely follows
70
, the complexity is different due to the underlying QEC-based Lindbladian reshaping. We then summarize its guarantees in
Theorem

E.3
.

Inputs :

[label=(0), leftmargin=*, nosep]

1.

Access to
n
-qubit evolution
e^{t\mathcal{L}}
, where
\mathcal{L}
is
M
-sparse in the Pauli basis.

2.

Target accuracy
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and total failure probability
\delta\in(0,1)
.

3.

The
\eta
-heavy dissipator structure
\mathcal{S}_{D,\eta}
, chosen so that, for the QEC reshaping with
V_{\mathrm{corr}}=\mathcal{S}_{D,\eta}
, every successful code draw induces an effective generator
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}
(Eq. (
136
)) whose bias and residual parameters satisfy
B_{\infty}\leq\varepsilon/50
,
K_{\infty}\leq\varepsilon/50
and
R_{\diamond}\leq\varepsilon/(8\sqrt{2}\pi)
.

4.

Access to
n+\mathcal{O}\left(\log M\right)
noiseless ancillas (for Bell sampling, QEC code and checks).

Output :
An estimator
\widehat{h}_{\mathrm{HDD},\eta}
such that
\|\widehat{h}_{\mathrm{HDD},\eta}-h_{\mathrm{HDD},\eta}\|_{\infty}\leq\varepsilon
with probability at least
1-\delta
.

Schedule

L_{h}\leftarrow\left\lceil\log_{2}(1/\varepsilon)\right\rceil

//
# hierarchical rounds

\delta_{\mathrm{struct}},\delta_{\mathrm{coeff}}\leftarrow\frac{\delta}{2L_{h}}

//
per-round failure budgets

\widehat{h}_{\mathrm{HDD},\eta}\leftarrow\mathbf{0}

//
coefficient estimates (indexed by Pauli)

\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathrm{HDD{}},\eta}\leftarrow\emptyset

//
identified support

for

j\leftarrow 0

to

L_{h}-1

do

\widehat{\mathcal{Y}}_{j}\leftarrow\texttt{Hamilt\_Struct\_Sampling}(j,\,e^{t\mathcal{L}},\ \mathcal{S}_{D,\eta},\ \widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathrm{HDD{}},\eta},\ \delta_{\mathrm{struct}},\ \mathrm{ancillas})

//
candidate terms, alg
5

for

P\in\widehat{\mathcal{Y}}_{j}\setminus\mathfrak{D}_{\eta}

do

(S_{P},\ \mathcal{R}_{P})\leftarrow\text{reuse the }\mathrm{QEC}_{P}
from when the structure identification alg
5
. sampled
P

\delta_{P}\leftarrow\delta_{\mathrm{coeff}}/\max\{1,|\widehat{\mathcal{Y}}_{j}|\}

//
per-Pauli failure budget

\widehat{\mu}_{P}\leftarrow\texttt{Hamilt\_Coeff\_Learning}(P,\ \varepsilon/2,\ \delta_{P},\ (S_{P},\mathcal{R}_{P}),\ e^{\mathcal{L}t},\ \mathrm{ancillas})

//
learn coeff. alg.
6

if

\absolutevalue{\widehat\mu_P}>\varepsilon/2

then

\widehat{h}_{\mathrm{HDD},\eta}[P]\leftarrow\widehat{\mu}_{P}

//
record estimate

\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathrm{HDD{}},\eta}\leftarrow\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathrm{HDD{}},\eta}\cup\{P\}

//
update learned support

return
