\widehat{h}_{\mathrm{HDD},\eta}

Algorithm 7

Hamiltonian (HDD) learning

We now prove
Theorem

E.3
. We restate it here for convenience.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be an
n
-qubit
M
-sparse Lindbladian as in Eq. (
133
). Fix
\varepsilon,\delta\in(0,1)
and assume black-box access to
e^{t\mathcal{L}}
. Assume the
\eta
-heavy dissipator structure
\mathcal{S}_{D,\eta}
is given, and that it satisfies the following uniform small-bias/small-residual condition. For every successful QEC code draw used by
Algorithm

5
(and reused by
Algorithm

6
), the corresponding effective generator
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}
written in the form of Eq. (
136
) has bias and residual parameters from Eq. (
139
) satisfying

B_{\infty}\leq\varepsilon/50,\qquad K_{\infty}\leq\varepsilon/50,\qquad R_{\diamond}\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{8\sqrt{2}\pi}.

Then running
Algorithm

7
returns coefficient estimates
\widehat{h}_{\mathrm{HDD},\eta}
for the Hamiltonian-Disjoint-from-Dissipator Hamiltonian at scale
\eta
satisfying the following guarantees.

1.

(Accuracy)
With probability at least
1-\delta
,

\|\widehat{h}_{\mathrm{HDD},\eta}-h_{\mathrm{HDD},\eta}\|_{\infty}\leq\varepsilon.

2.

(Evolution time)
The total evolution time under
e^{t\mathcal{L}}
is

t_{\mathrm{tot}}=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{M^{2}}{\varepsilon}\right).

3.

(Time resolution)
The procedure only applies
e^{t\mathcal{L}}
for times at least

t_{\mathrm{res}}=\Omega\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{M^{3}}\right).

4.

(Number of experiments)
The total number of collected measurements is

\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(M^{3}\right),

dominated by Bell sampling during the candidate-identification stage.

5.

(Ancilla cost)
The procedure uses at most

n+\mathcal{O}\left(\log M\right)

noiseless ancilla qubits in addition to the
n
probe qubits.

6.

(Classical overhead)
The classical processing cost is

\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{M^{6}+nM^{5}+n^{2}M^{4}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right).

The first and second terms come from real-time QEC processing in the candidate-identification stage, while the last term comes from logical-Pauli sampling in coefficient learning.

Proof.

We first verify correctness. Since
2^{-j}\geq\varepsilon
for all rounds
j<L_{h}
, the uniform assumptions
B_{\infty}\leq\varepsilon/50
and
K_{\infty}\leq\varepsilon/50
imply the bias/residual assumptions required by
Theorem

E.7
.
B_{\infty}\leq\varepsilon/50
together with
R_{\diamond}\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{8\sqrt{2}\pi}
imply the assumptions of
Theorem

E.9
when coefficient learning is called with accuracy
\varepsilon/2
. Thus, in each round, candidate identification contains all terms in
\mathcal{Y}_{j}
not already learned, and coefficient learning estimates all candidates to accuracy
\varepsilon/2
. The strict threshold
|\widehat{\mu}_{P}|>\varepsilon/2
removes false positives with
h_{P}=0
, while any true candidate with
|h_{P}|>\varepsilon
is retained. Hence the round invariant Eq. (
135
) is maintained, and after
L_{h}=\lceil\log_{2}(1/\varepsilon)\rceil
rounds all Hamiltonian-Disjoint-from-Dissipator coefficients are estimated to accuracy at most
\varepsilon
. The union bound over the structure and coefficient-learning calls, with
\delta_{\mathrm{struct}}=\delta_{\mathrm{coeff}}=\delta/(2L_{h})
, gives success probability at least
1-\delta
.

We now prove the resource bounds. In round
j
, the evolution time is the sum of candidate-identification time and coefficient-learning time:

T_{j}=\mathcal{O}\left(2^{j}M^{2}\log(M/\delta_{\mathrm{struct}})\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{|\widehat{\mathcal{Y}}_{j}|}{\varepsilon}\left[\log\left(\frac{|\widehat{\mathcal{Y}}_{j}|}{\delta_{\mathrm{coeff}}}\right)+\log\log\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)\right]\right).

By
Theorem

E.7
, with high probability

|\widehat{\mathcal{Y}}_{j}|=\mathcal{O}\left(M^{2}\log(M/\delta_{\mathrm{struct}})\right)
. Therefore,

T_{j}=\mathcal{O}\left(2^{j}M^{2}\log(M/\delta_{\mathrm{struct}})\right)+\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{M^{2}}{\varepsilon}\right).

Summing over
j=0,\ldots,L_{h}-1
and using
\sum_{j}2^{j}=\mathcal{O}\left(1/\varepsilon\right)
, we obtain

t_{\mathrm{tot}}=\sum_{j=0}^{L_{h}-1}T_{j}=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{M^{2}}{\varepsilon}\right).

The total number of experiments in round
j
is

N_{\mathrm{exp}}^{(j)}=\mathcal{O}\left(M^{3}\log(M/\delta_{\mathrm{struct}})\right)+|\widehat{\mathcal{Y}}_{j}|\cdot\mathcal{O}\left(\log\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)\left[\log\left(\frac{|\widehat{\mathcal{Y}}_{j}|}{\delta_{\mathrm{coeff}}}\right)+\log\log\left(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\right)\right]\right).

Since
|\widehat{\mathcal{Y}}_{j}|=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(M^{2}\right)
, it is dominated by the candidate identification step and summing over all rounds gives

N_{\mathrm{exp}}=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(M^{3}\right),

The time resolution is bottlenecked by candidate identification. By
Theorem

E.7
, round
j
has resolution
\Omega\left(\frac{1}{M^{3}2^{j}}\right).

It is smallest in the final round, where
2^{j}=\Theta(1/\varepsilon)
, so

t_{\mathrm{res}}=\Omega\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{M^{3}}\right).

The ancilla bound follows directly from
Theorem

E.7
: candidate identification uses at most
n+5\lceil\log_{2}(2M)\rceil
noiseless ancillas in addition to the
n
probe qubits, while coefficient learning reuses the QEC code from the round in which the candidate was sampled. Hence the total ancilla cost is
n+\mathcal{O}\left(\log M\right)
.

The classical overhead is the sum of the candidate-identification and coefficient-learning contributions. From
Theorems

E.7
and
E.9
,

\mathrm{Classical\,overhead}=\sum_{j=0}^{L_{h}-1}\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left((n+M)M^{5}4^{j}\right)+\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(n^{2}M^{4}/\varepsilon^{2}\right)\right)=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{M^{6}+nM^{5}+n^{2}M^{4}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right).

This completes the proof.
∎

E.5
Removing the known dissipator structure assumption

The conditional HDD Hamiltonian learner in
Theorem

E.3
takes the
\eta
-heavy dissipator structure as input and assumes that, after setting
V_{\mathrm{corr}}=\mathcal{S}_{D,\eta}
in the QEC reshaping routines, the resulting effective generators have sufficiently small bias and residual parameters
B_{\infty}
,
K_{\infty}
and
R_{\diamond}
. We now remove this known-structure assumption by invoking the dissipator-structure learner from Appendix
D
. Thus, for a target Hamiltonian accuracy
\varepsilon
, the remaining question is how finely one must learn the dissipator—that is, how small
\eta
must be—to guarantee the required bounds on
B_{\infty}
,
K_{\infty}
and
R_{\diamond}
. This subsection answers that question by deriving the relevant choices of
\eta
: a worst-case choice that holds for arbitrary sparse Lindbladians, and a sharper choice that yields Heisenberg-limited scaling under an additional balanced-tail condition on the Kossakowski matrix.

The only subtlety is that the dissipator-structure learner returns a slightly enlarged estimate. When run at scale
\eta
,
Algorithm

4
outputs, with high probability, a set
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,\eta}
satisfying

\mathcal{S}_{D,\eta}\subseteq\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,\eta}\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{D,\eta/2}.

We then run
Algorithm

7
with
V_{\mathrm{corr}}=\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,\eta}
in place of the ideal input
\mathcal{S}_{D,\eta}
. This changes only the Hamiltonian sector being learned. Define the learned dissipator footprint

\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}_{\eta}\coloneq\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,\eta}\cup\{\operatorname{Herm}(PP^{\prime}):\ P,P^{\prime}\in\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,\eta},\ P\neq P^{\prime}\}.

Conditioned on the success of the dissipator-structure learner, the Hamiltonian learner estimates the coefficients in

\mathcal{S}_{H}\setminus\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}_{\eta}.

Thus, at finite accuracy, the phrase “Hamiltonian disjoint from the dissipator” is necessarily scale-dependent. Given a target accuracy
\varepsilon
, we choose a dissipator threshold
\eta=\eta(\varepsilon)
and learn the Hamiltonian outside the corresponding learned dissipator footprint. As
\varepsilon
decreases,
\eta(\varepsilon)
also decreases, and the learned sector approaches the Hamiltonian terms disjoint from the full dissipator footprint.

It remains to show that setting
V_{\mathrm{corr}}=\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,\eta}
results in effective generators satisfying the small-bias/small-residual assumptions of
Theorem

E.3
. The following lemma gives a worst-case bound using only positivity of the Kossakowski matrix.

Lemma E.10

(Worst-case bias and residual bounds from a learned dissipator structure)
.

Suppose the dissipator-structure learner succeeds at scale
\eta
, so that

\mathcal{S}_{D,\eta}\subseteq\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,\eta}\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{D,\eta/2}.

Set
V_{\mathrm{corr}}=\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,\eta}
in the QEC reshaping routines. Then, for both the candidate-identification effective generator from Eq. (
70
) and the coefficient-learning effective generator from Eq. (
74
), the residual and bias parameters from Eq. (
139
) satisfy

R_{\diamond}\leq 2M_{D}^{2}\eta,\qquad K_{\infty}\leq M_{D}\eta,\qquad B_{\infty}\leq\frac{M_{D}}{2}\sqrt{\eta}.

Consequently, there exists a universal constant
c>0
such that the small-bias/small-residual conditions of
Theorem

E.3
, i.e.
B_{\infty}\leq\varepsilon/50
,
K_{\infty}\leq\varepsilon/50
and
R_{\diamond}\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{8\sqrt{2}\pi}
, hold whenever

\eta\leq c\,\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{M_{D}^{2}}.

Proof.

We prove the bounds for the QEC-reshaped generator in Eq. (
70
); the coefficient-learning generator in Eq. (
74
) only keeps a subset of the same residual and bias contributions after the partial logical twirl, so the same bounds apply.

Since the dissipator-structure learner succeeds at scale
\eta
, every uncorrected dissipator Pauli
P_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}\setminus V_{\mathrm{corr}}
satisfies
a_{kk}<\eta
. By positivity of the Kossakowski matrix,
|a_{kj}|^{2}\leq a_{kk}a_{jj}
. Hence, for any two uncorrected dissipator Paulis
P_{k},P_{j}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}\setminus V_{\mathrm{corr}}
,

|a_{kj}|\leq\sqrt{a_{kk}a_{jj}}\leq\eta.

The residual Lindbladian consists only of uncorrected–uncorrected dissipator terms, as seen from Eq. (
70
) and Eq. (
74
). It has at most
M_{D}^{2}
nonzero Kossakowski entries, each of magnitude at most
\eta
. Applying
Section

B.3
gives

R_{\diamond}=\|\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{resid}}\|_{\diamond}\leq 2M_{D}^{2}\eta.

We next bound the no-jump residual coefficient scale. From the residual part of Eq. (
70
), the associated no-jump operator is

K_{\mathrm{resid}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P_{k},P_{j}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}\setminus V_{\mathrm{corr}}\\
r(P_{k})=r(P_{j})\end{subarray}}a_{kj}\,(Q_{P_{j}}^{\dagger}Q_{P_{k}}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P_{k},P_{j}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}\setminus V_{\mathrm{corr}}\\
r(P_{k})=r(P_{j})\end{subarray}}a_{kj}\,(P_{j}P_{k}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}).

Fix any resulting Pauli
Q
, including possibly
Q=I
. For each fixed uncorrected
P_{k}
, there is at most one uncorrected
P_{j}
such that
P_{j}P_{k}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}\propto Q
. Therefore the coefficient of any fixed Pauli
Q
is a sum of at most
M_{D}
terms, each of magnitude at most
\eta
. Hence

K_{\infty}=\|K_{\mathrm{resid}}\|_{\infty,\mathrm{coeff}}\leq M_{D}\eta.

We next bound the Hamiltonian bias. From Eq. (
70
), each bias coefficient is a sum of corrected–uncorrected dissipator cross terms with
P_{k}\in V_{\mathrm{corr}}
and
P_{j}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}\setminus V_{\mathrm{corr}}
. For a fixed resulting Pauli, the number of such contributing pairs is at most

\min\{|V_{\mathrm{corr}}|,M_{D}-|V_{\mathrm{corr}}|\}\leq\frac{M_{D}}{2}.

For every such pair,
P_{j}
is uncorrected and hence
a_{jj}<\eta
, while
a_{kk}\leq 1
by normalization. Therefore positivity gives

|a_{kj}|\leq\sqrt{a_{kk}a_{jj}}\leq\sqrt{\eta}.

(160)

Each corrected–uncorrected cross term contributes to a Hermitian Pauli bias coefficient with magnitude at most
|a_{kj}|
. Therefore

B_{\infty}=\|H_{\mathrm{bias}}\|_{\infty,\mathrm{coeff}}\leq\frac{M_{D}}{2}\sqrt{\eta}.

The same bound applies after partial logical twirling, since Eq. (
74
) only retains a subset of the corrected–uncorrected cross terms.

Finally, the required conditions are implied by

2M_{D}^{2}\eta\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{8\sqrt{2}\pi},\qquad M_{D}\eta\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{50},\qquad\frac{M_{D}}{2}\sqrt{\eta}\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{50}.

Since
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
and
M_{D}\geq 1
, all three inequalities hold for

\eta\leq c\,\frac{\varepsilon^{2}}{M_{D}^{2}}

with a sufficiently small universal constant
c>0
.
∎

Corollary E.11

(Ansatz-free HDD learning, worst case)
.

Run the dissipator-structure learner from
Algorithm

4
at a scale
\eta=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\varepsilon^{2}/M_{D}^{2}\right)
satisfying
Section

E.5
, and then run
Algorithm

7
with
V_{\mathrm{corr}}=\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,\eta}
. Conditioned on the success of the dissipator-structure learner, the algorithm learns all Hamiltonian coefficients in

\mathcal{S}_{H}\setminus\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}_{\eta}

to accuracy
\varepsilon
with the guarantees of
Theorem

E.3
. The total resource bounds are obtained by composing the dissipator-structure learning resources from
Theorem

D.1
with the conditional HDD learning resources from
Theorem

E.3
. In particular, because the worst-case choice of
\eta
scales quadratically in
\varepsilon
up to sparsity-dependent factors, the dissipator-structure preprocessing is standard-quantum-limited in the target Hamiltonian accuracy.

The worst-case SQL scaling comes from the PSD-allowed corrected–uncorrected cross term: a corrected heavy dissipator direction can couple to an uncorrected light direction with
|a_{kj}|=O(\sqrt{\eta})
, producing an
O(\sqrt{\eta})
Hamiltonian bias after QEC reshaping. Thus the PSD-only analysis forces the dissipator threshold to scale as
\eta\propto\varepsilon^{2}
in the target Hamiltonian accuracy, up to sparsity-dependent factors. The following balanced-tail condition rules out precisely this imbalanced coupling.

Assumption E.12

(Balanced Kossakowski tails at scale
\eta
)
.

For every pair
P_{k},P_{j}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}
with
j\neq k
, if

\min\{a_{kk},a_{jj}\}\leq\eta\qquad\text{and}\qquad a_{kk}a_{jj}\geq\eta^{2},

then

|a_{kj}|\leq\kappa\min\{a_{kk},a_{jj}\}

for some parameter
\kappa\geq 1
.

Lemma E.13

(Bias and residual bounds under balanced Kossakowski tails)
.

Suppose the dissipator-structure learner succeeds at scale
\eta
, so that

\mathcal{S}_{D,\eta}\subseteq\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,\eta}\subseteq\mathcal{S}_{D,\eta/2}.

Set
V_{\mathrm{corr}}=\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,\eta}
in the QEC reshaping routines, and assume
Section

E.5
. Then, for both the candidate-identification effective generator from Eq. (
70
) and the coefficient-learning effective generator from Eq. (
74
), the residual and bias parameters from Eq. (
139
) satisfy

R_{\diamond}\leq 2M_{D}^{2}\eta,\qquad K_{\infty}\leq M_{D}\eta,\qquad B_{\infty}\leq\frac{\kappa M_{D}}{2}\eta.

Consequently, there exists a universal constant
c>0
such that the small-bias/small-residual conditions of
Theorem

E.3
, i.e.
B_{\infty}\leq\varepsilon/50
,
K_{\infty}\leq\varepsilon/50
and
R_{\diamond}\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{8\sqrt{2}\pi}
, hold whenever

\eta\leq c\min\left\{\frac{\varepsilon}{M_{D}^{2}},\frac{\varepsilon}{\kappa M_{D}}\right\}.

Proof.

The residual bounds
R_{\diamond}\leq 2M_{D}^{2}\eta
and
K_{\infty}\leq M_{D}\eta
are exactly the same as in
Section

E.5
since they only depends on uncorrected–uncorrected dissipator cross terms.

It remains to improve the bias estimate. Under
Section

E.5
, every corrected–uncorrected pair contributing to
H_{\mathrm{bias}}
satisfies
|a_{kj}|\leq\kappa\eta
: if
a_{kk}a_{jj}<\eta^{2}
this follows from PSD, while if
a_{kk}a_{jj}\geq\eta^{2}
it follows from the balanced-tail assumption and the fact that the uncorrected term has diagonal weight
<\eta
. Replacing the worst-case bound
|a_{kj}|\leq\sqrt{\eta}
by
|a_{kj}|\leq\kappa\eta
in Eq. (
160
) gives

B_{\infty}\leq\frac{\kappa M_{D}}{2}\eta.

The stated sufficient choice of
\eta
follows immediately from requiring

2M_{D}^{2}\eta\leq\varepsilon/(8\sqrt{2}\pi)
and

\kappa M_{D}\eta/2\leq\varepsilon/50
.
∎

Combining
Section

E.5
with the dissipator-structure learner from Appendix
D
, it suffices under
Section

E.5
to learn the dissipator at a resolution whose dependence on the target Hamiltonian accuracy is linear in
\varepsilon
, up to sparsity-dependent factors. Since the dissipator-structure learner has total evolution time
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(1/\eta\right)
, this preprocessing is “Heisenberg-limited” in
\varepsilon
. Consequently, composing it with the conditional HDD learner from
Theorem

E.3
yields Heisenberg-limited Hamiltonian-Disjoint-from-Dissipator learning without prior knowledge of either the dissipator or Hamiltonian operator content.

Corollary E.14

(Heisenberg-limited ansatz-free HDD learning under balanced tails)
.

Assume
Section

E.5
holds with
\kappa\leq M_{D}
. Run the dissipator-structure learner from
Algorithm

4
at scale

\eta=\Theta\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{M_{D}^{2}}\right),

with the hidden constant chosen small enough to satisfy
Section

E.5
. Then run
Algorithm

7
with
V_{\mathrm{corr}}=\widehat{\mathcal{S}}_{D,\eta}
. Conditioned on the success of the dissipator-structure learner, the algorithm learns all Hamiltonian coefficients in

\mathcal{S}_{H}\setminus\widehat{\mathfrak{D}}_{\eta}

to accuracy
\varepsilon
with the guarantees of
Theorem

E.3
. The total resource bounds are obtained by composing the dissipator-structure learning resources from
Theorem

D.1
with the conditional HDD learning resources from
Theorem

E.3
. In particular, the black-box evolution time is

t_{\mathrm{tot}}=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{1}{\eta}\right)+\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{M^{2}}{\varepsilon}\right)=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{M^{2}}{\varepsilon}\right),

which is Heisenberg-limited in the target Hamiltonian accuracy. The time resolution is:

t_{\mathrm{res}}=\Omega\left({\min\left\{\frac{\eta}{M^{2}},\frac{\varepsilon}{M^{3}}\right\}}\right)=\Omega\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{M^{3}}\right),

where we used
M_{D}^{2}\leq M
and
\|\mathcal{L}\|_{\diamond}\leq 2M
from
Section

B.3
.

Let us unpack why
Section

E.5
is a natural condition. It does not constrain strong–strong couplings: if both diagonal entries are above the learned dissipator threshold, both Pauli directions are included in the correctable set, so their off-diagonal coupling does not produce a corrected–uncorrected bias. This covers standard coherent Pauli blocks such as amplitude damping when the block is above threshold: a jump operator proportional to
X+iY
produces an
X/Y
Kossakowski block whose diagonal and off-diagonal entries are all of the same order, and the two Pauli directions are learned together. The condition also does not constrain weak–weak couplings: if both directions are below threshold, then
a_{kk}a_{jj}<\eta^{2}
, so positivity already gives
|a_{kj}|<\eta
. Pauli channels satisfy the condition trivially because the Kossakowski matrix is diagonal.

The only excluded regime is an imbalanced weak–strong tail: one diagonal entry lies below the dissipator-learning threshold, the other is much larger, and the off-diagonal entry is large enough to behave like the PSD worst-case geometric mean. The balanced-tail condition rules this out by requiring that, in this intermediate regime, the off-diagonal entry is controlled by the smaller diagonal weight. Thus the assumption does not forbid off-diagonal Kossakowski structure; it only prevents a weak unlearned dissipator component from being coherently tied to a strong corrected component strongly enough to create the bias obstruction.

The condition is still permissive. If the two coupled diagonal weights obey

\frac{\max\{a_{kk},a_{jj}\}}{\min\{a_{kk},a_{jj}\}}\leq\kappa^{2},

then positivity alone implies

|a_{kj}|\leq\sqrt{a_{kk}a_{jj}}\leq\kappa\min\{a_{kk},a_{jj}\},

so
Section

E.5
is automatically satisfied for that pair. Moreover, by
Section

E.5
, it suffices to choose

\eta\leq c\min\left\{\frac{\varepsilon}{M_{D}^{2}},\frac{\varepsilon}{\kappa M_{D}}\right\}.

Thus, whenever
\kappa\leq M_{D}
, the residual term is the bottleneck and one may take
\eta=\Theta(\varepsilon/M_{D}^{2})
. In particular, diagonal-weight ratios as large as
\kappa^{2}\leq M_{D}^{2}
are compatible with the Heisenberg-limited composition. For a single jump operator proportional to
X+\alpha Y
, for example, this allows
|\alpha|\gtrsim 1/M_{D}
up to constants.

E.6
Deferred proofs for HDD Bell-sampling identification

Let
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}
be an
n^{\prime}
-qubit effective generator of the form in
Eq. (
136
), with bias and residual parameters
B_{\infty},K_{\infty}
as in
Eq. (
139
). Write it in the jump unraveling picture using Eq. (
139
) and Eq. (
138
):

\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}(\rho)=-iH_{\rm eff}\rho+i\rho H_{\rm eff}^{\dagger}+\sum_{w,m}\alpha_{wm}P_{w}\rho P_{m},\qquad H_{\rm eff}\coloneq H_{\rm tar}+H_{\rm bias}-\frac{i}{2}K_{\rm resid}.

Expand the target Hamiltonian in the Pauli basis as
H_{\mathrm{tar}}=\sum_{P_{m}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{H,tar}}}h_{m}P_{m}
, the bias as
H_{\mathrm{bias}}=\sum_{P_{m}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{H,bias}}}b_{m}P_{m}
and the residual non-Hermitian contribution as
K_{\mathrm{resid}}=\sum_{P_{m}\in\mathcal{S}_{K}}k_{m}P_{m}
, where

\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{H,tar}},\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{H,bias}}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{n^{\prime}}\setminus\{I\}
,
\mathcal{S}_{K}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{n^{\prime}}
and
\{h_{m}\},\{b_{m}\},\{k_{m}\}
are real coefficients.
Let
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{H,eff}}\coloneq\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{H,tar}}\cup\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{H,bias}}\cup\mathcal{S}_{K}
and assume
|\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{H,eff}}|\leq M
.

Fix an integer
j\geq 0
. Assume that
|h_{m}|\leq 2^{-j}
for every

P_{m}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{H,tar}}
and that a target Pauli

P_{r}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{H,tar}}
satisfies
|h_{r}|\geq 2^{-(j+1)}
. Assume that the bias and residual parameters satisfy
B_{\infty}\leq 2^{-(j+1)}/25
and
K_{\infty}\leq 2^{-(j+1)}/25.

Let
C
be a stabilizer code on
n^{\prime}
qubits with code projector
\Pi
and stabilizer subgroup

S\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{n^{\prime}}
. Let
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{eff}}\coloneq\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{H,eff}}\cup\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{D,res}}
and let
G_{\mathrm{eff}}\coloneq\langle\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{eff}}\rangle\subseteq\mathbb{P}_{n^{\prime}}
denote the subgroup generated by
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{eff}}
. Assume that all Paulis

P\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{eff}}
have zero syndrome
r(P)=0
with respect to
C
, and that no
nontrivial product of elements of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{eff}}
is proportional to a stabilizer; equivalently,
for every
Q\in G_{\mathrm{eff}}
,
Q\propto s\in S
implies
s=I_{n^{\prime}}
.

Let
\rho_{0}\coloneq\ket{\Phi_0}\bra{\Phi_0}
, where

\ket{\Phi_0}\coloneq\ket{\Phi_+}^{\otimes n^{\prime}}
and

\ket{\Phi_+}=(\ket{00}+\ket{11})/\sqrt{2}
. Let
\widetilde{\rho}
denote the state immediately before Bell-basis measurement,

\widetilde{\rho}\coloneq(e^{t\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}})\left(\frac{(\Pi\otimes I_{n^{\prime}})\rho_{0}(\Pi\otimes I_{n^{\prime}})}{\tr((\Pi\otimes I_{n'})\rho_0)}\right).

For any Pauli
P\in\mathbb{P}_{n^{\prime}}
, let

O_{P}\coloneq(P\otimes I_{n^{\prime}})\ket{\Phi_0}\bra{\Phi_0}(P^{\dagger}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}})

denote the corresponding Bell-measurement projector. Then, for

t=2^{j}/(2M)
, a single Bell-sampling experiment on
\widetilde{\rho}
satisfies

\displaystyle\Pr(\text{sample $P_r$ modulo stabilizers})

\displaystyle\coloneq\tr\left(\sum_{s\in S}O_{sP_{r}}\,\widetilde{\rho}\right)\geq\frac{1}{200M^{2}},

\displaystyle\Pr(\text{sample a stabilizer})

\displaystyle\coloneq\tr\left(\sum_{s\in S}O_{s}\,\widetilde{\rho}\right)\geq 1-\frac{1}{5M}.

Proof.

Since the code projector can be expressed as
\Pi=\frac{1}{|S|}\sum_{s\in S}s
(Eq. (
36
)), we have
\tr((\Pi\otimes I_{n'})\rho_0)=1/|S|
. Define

\rho_{\mathrm{code}}\coloneq\frac{(\Pi\otimes I_{n^{\prime}})\rho_{0}(\Pi\otimes I_{n^{\prime}})}{\tr((\Pi\otimes I_{n'})\rho_0)}=|S|(\Pi\otimes I_{n^{\prime}})\rho_{0}(\Pi\otimes I_{n^{\prime}}).

To obtain the lower bounds on the projection probabilities of this lemma, we use the quantum jump unraveling formalism. In particular, we compare the actual evolution of
\rho_{\mathrm{code}}
under
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}
to the non-hermitian Hamiltonian evolution generated by

H_{\mathrm{eff}}\coloneq H_{\mathrm{tar}}+H_{\mathrm{bias}}-\frac{i}{2}K_{\mathrm{resid}}=\sum_{P_{m}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{H,eff}}}\widetilde{h}_{m}P_{m},\qquad\widetilde{h}_{m}\coloneq h_{m}+b_{m}-\frac{i}{2}{k_{m}},

(161)

where
h_{m}
,
b_{m}
and
k_{m}
are extended by zero outside
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{H,tar}}
,
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{H,bias}}
and
\mathcal{S}_{K}
, respectively. Let
\mathcal{E}_{t}(\rho)\coloneq e^{-iH_{\mathrm{eff}}t}\rho e^{iH_{\mathrm{eff}}^{\dagger}t}
and
\mathcal{J}(\rho)\coloneq\sum_{P_{w},P_{m}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{D,res}}}\alpha_{wm}P_{w}\rho P_{m}
.
By decomposing
e^{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}t}
and using Duhamel’s formula:

e^{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}t}=\mathcal{E}_{t}+\underbrace{(e^{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}t}-\mathcal{E}_{t})}_{\Phi_{t}}=\mathcal{E}_{t}+\int_{s=0}^{t}e^{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}(t-s)}\circ\mathcal{J}\circ\mathcal{E}_{s}\,ds

Since
\mathcal{E}_{t}
is a conjugation map, it is completely positive (CP). Similarly,
\sum_{P_{w},P_{m}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{D,res}}}\alpha_{wm}\,P_{w}\rho P_{m}
is a CP map since one can diagonalize it by positivity of the Kossakowski matrix
\alpha
. Concretely, set
\alpha_{wm}\coloneq\sum_{x}\gamma_{wx}\gamma^{*}_{mx}
:

\sum_{P_{w},P_{m}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{D,res}}}\alpha_{wm}\,P_{w}\rho P_{m}=\sum_{x}\sum_{P_{w}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{D,res}}}\gamma_{wx}P_{w}\rho\sum_{P_{m}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{D,res}}}\gamma^{*}_{mx}P_{m}=\sum_{x}L_{x}\rho L_{x}^{\dagger}

where
L_{x}\coloneq\sum_{P_{w}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{D,res}}}\gamma_{wx}P_{w}
. Since the integrand is a composition of CP maps for every
s
, and the integral of CP maps is CP,
\Phi_{t}
is completely positive. Therefore,

\displaystyle\Pr(\text{sample $P_r$ modulo stabilizers})=\tr\left(\sum_{s\in S}O_{sP_{r}}\,\mathcal{E}_{t}(\rho_{\mathrm{code}})\right)+\tr\left(\sum_{s\in S}O_{sP_{r}}\,\Phi_{t}(\rho_{\mathrm{code}})\right)

(162)

\displaystyle\geq\tr\left(\sum_{s\in S}O_{sP_{r}}\,\mathcal{E}_{t}(\rho_{\mathrm{code}})\right)

The same holds for
\Pr(\text{sample a stabilizer})
by setting
P_{r}=I_{n^{\prime}}
. Thus moving forward, we focus on
\mathcal{E}_{t}(\rho)\coloneq e^{-iH_{\mathrm{eff}}t}\rho e^{iH_{\mathrm{eff}}^{\dagger}t}
. By the assumptions
|h_{m}|\leq 2^{-j}
,
B_{\infty}\leq\frac{2^{-j}}{50}
and
K_{\infty}\leq\frac{2^{-j}}{50}
and using Eq. (
161
), we have

\beta\coloneq\max_{P_{m}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{H,eff}}}|\widetilde{h}_{m}|\leq|h_{m}|+|b_{m}|+|k_{m}|\leq 1.04\cdot 2^{-j}.

(163)

Moreover, for the target Pauli
P_{r}
, since
|h_{r}|\geq 2^{-(j+1)}
,

|\operatorname{Re}(\widetilde{h}_{r})|\geq|h_{r}|-|b_{r}|\geq 0.96\cdot 2^{-(j+1)}.

Since every
P_{m}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{eff}}
has zero syndrome, every
P_{m}
commutes with
\Pi
, and hence
H_{\mathrm{eff}}
and
e^{-iH_{\mathrm{eff}}t}
commute with
\Pi
.

Let
\sigma\in S
be an arbitrary stabilizer. We first lower bound the probability to sample
\sigma P_{r}
and then combine the probability across all stabilizers. Using commutativity of
G_{\mathrm{eff}}
with
\Pi
,
\sigma\Pi=\Pi
and the hermiticity of
P_{r}
and
\sigma
, we get

\displaystyle p^{\mathrm{H}}_{(\sigma P_{r})}\coloneq\tr\left(O_{(\sigma P_{r})}(e^{-iH_{\mathrm{eff}}t}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}})\,\rho_{\mathrm{code}}\,(e^{iH_{\mathrm{eff}}^{\dagger}t}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}})\right)

\displaystyle=|S|\,\big|\bra{\Phi_0}(P_{r}^{\dagger}\sigma^{\dagger}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}})(e^{-iH_{\mathrm{eff}}t}\Pi\otimes I_{n^{\prime}})\ket{\Phi_0}\big|^{2}=

(164)

\displaystyle=|S|^{-1}\big|\bra{\Phi_0}(P_{r}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}})(\sum_{s\in S}s\,e^{-iH_{\mathrm{eff}}t}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}})\ket{\Phi_0}\big|^{2}.

Since no nontrivial product of elements of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{eff}}
is proportional to a stabilizer, by orthogonality of Bell-basis states, the only
s\in S
that contributes nonzero probability is
s=I_{n^{\prime}}
. Thus,

p^{\mathrm{H}}_{(\sigma P_{r})}=|S|^{-1}\big|\bra{\Phi_0}(P_{r}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}})(e^{-iH_{\mathrm{eff}}t}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}})\ket{\Phi_0}\big|^{2}.

(165)

Taylor-expanding
e^{-iH_{\mathrm{eff}}t}=I_{n^{\prime}}-iH_{\mathrm{eff}}t+\sum_{q\geq 2}\frac{(-iH_{\mathrm{eff}}t)^{q}}{q!}
, and using orthogonality of Bell-basis states, we obtain

\displaystyle p^{\mathrm{H}}_{(\sigma P_{r})}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|S|}\left|-it\widetilde{h}_{r}+\sum_{q\geq 2}\frac{(-it)^{q}}{q!}\bra{\Phi_0}(P_{r}H_{\mathrm{eff}}^{q}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}})\ket{\Phi_0}\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{|S|}\left(|\operatorname{Re}(\widetilde{h}_{r})|t-\left|\operatorname{Im}\left(\sum_{q\geq 2}\frac{(-it)^{q}}{q!}\bra{\Phi_0}(P_{r}H_{\mathrm{eff}}^{q}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}})\ket{\Phi_0}\right)\right|\right)^{2}

where the inequality follows from taking the imaginary part.

To account for the higher-order terms, we count the number of ordered tuples of Hamiltonian Paulis from
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{H,eff}}
whose product is proportional to
P_{r}
. For the
q
th order, this number is at most
M^{q-1}
. Therefore, using Eq. (
163
),

p^{\mathrm{H}}_{(\sigma P_{r})}\geq\frac{1}{|S|}\left(|\operatorname{Re}(\widetilde{h}_{r})|t-\sum_{q\geq 2}\frac{(t\beta)^{q}}{q!}M^{q-1}\right)^{2}.

Set
t\coloneq 2^{j}T
. Using
\beta\leq 1.04\cdot 2^{-j}
and
|\operatorname{Re}(\widetilde{h}_{r})|\geq 0.96\cdot 2^{-(j+1)}
, we get

p^{\mathrm{H}}_{(\sigma P_{r})}\geq\frac{1}{|S|}\left(0.96\cdot\frac{T}{2}-\frac{1}{M}\sum_{q\geq 2}\frac{(1.04\cdot TM)^{q}}{q!}\right)^{2}\geq\frac{1}{|S|M^{2}}\left(1.52TM-e^{1.04TM}+1\right)^{2}.

For
TM\in[0.2,0.52]
, this gives
p^{\mathrm{H}}_{(\sigma P_{r})}\geq\frac{1}{200|S|M^{2}}
. Therefore, using Eq. (
162
),

\Pr(\text{sample $P_r$ modulo stabilizers})\geq\tr\left(\sum_{s\in S}O_{sP_{r}}\,\mathcal{E}_{t}(\rho_{\mathrm{code}})\right)=\sum_{\sigma\in S}p^{\mathrm{H}}_{(\sigma P_{r})}\geq\frac{1}{200M^{2}}.

(166)

The calculation of
\Pr(\text{sample a stabilizer})
proceeds identically except with the replacement
P_{r}\to I
in Eq. (
165
). In this case the identity coefficient

-\frac{i}{2}k_{0}I
contributes at first order to the stabilizer amplitude. Hence, for any
\sigma\in S
and
TM\in[0.2,0.52]
we obtain

p^{\mathrm{H}}_{(\sigma)}\geq\frac{1}{|S|}\left(1-\frac{k_{0}t}{2}-\frac{1}{M}\sum_{q\geq 2}\frac{(1.04\cdot TM)^{q}}{q!}\right)^{2}.

Using
t=2^{j}T
and
k_{0}\leq K_{\infty}\leq 2^{-j}/50
, for
TM\in[0.2,0.52]
, this implies

p^{\mathrm{H}}_{(\sigma)}\geq\frac{1}{|S|}\left(1-\frac{1}{M}\left[\frac{TM}{100}+e^{1.04TM}-1-1.04TM\right]\right)^{2}\geq\frac{1}{|S|}\left(1-\frac{1}{5M}\right).

Therefore, using Eq. (
162
) and summing over all stabilizers, we get,

\Pr(\text{sample a stabilizer})\geq\tr\left(\sum_{s\in S}O_{s}\,\mathcal{E}_{t}(\rho_{\mathrm{code}})\right)=\sum_{\sigma\in S}p^{\mathrm{H}}_{(\sigma)}\geq 1-\frac{1}{5M}.

Since
t=2^{j}/(2M)
corresponds to
TM=0.5\in[0.2,0.52]
, this concludes the proof.
∎

The following corollary is useful for reducing the ancilla cost of the candidate identification in Appendix
E.2
.

Corollary E.15

(Chi-matrix entries lower bounds)
.

In the setting of
Section

E.2
, consider the chi-matrix representation of
e^{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}t}
:

e^{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}t}(\rho)=\sum_{P_{m},P_{w}\in G_{\mathrm{eff}}}\chi_{mw}(t)P_{m}\rho P_{w}.

In this representation, the sum over
G_{\mathrm{eff}}
is understood modulo global phases, with one Hermitian representative chosen for each phase class; we choose the representative of the target phase class to be
P_{r}
.

Then:

\displaystyle\chi_{rr}(t)

\displaystyle=\Pr(\text{sample $P_r$ modulo stabilizers})\geq\frac{1}{200M^{2}},

\displaystyle\chi_{00}(t)

\displaystyle=\Pr(\text{sample a stabilizer})\geq 1-\frac{1}{3M}.

Proof.

Fix
\sigma\in S
and write

Z_{r}\coloneq\sigma P_{r}
for the corresponding representative of the coset
SP_{r}
.
Using the chi-matrix representation of
e^{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}t}
and the definition of the Bell-measurements projector
O_{P}
, we get

\displaystyle\Pr(\text{sample $Z_r$})\coloneq\tr(O_{Z_r} e^{\LindbladEff t}(\rho_\mathrm{code}))

\displaystyle=|S|\sum_{P_{m},P_{w}\in G_{\mathrm{eff}}}\chi_{mw}(t)\bra{\Phi_0}(Z_{r}^{\dagger}P_{m}\Pi\otimes I_{n^{\prime}})\ket{\Phi_0}\bra{\Phi_0}(\Pi P_{w}Z_{r}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}})\ket{\Phi_0}=

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|S|}\sum_{P_{m}\in G_{\mathrm{eff}}}\chi_{mm}(t)\bigg|\bra{\Phi_0}(P_{r}P_{m}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}})\ket{\Phi_0}\bigg|^{2}=\frac{\chi_{rr}(t)}{|S|}.

where the second equality follows from
P_{r},P_{m}\in G_{\mathrm{eff}}
,
\Pi=\frac{1}{|S|}\sum_{s\in S}s
,
\Pi\sigma=\sigma\Pi=\Pi
and the assumption that no nontrivial product of elements of
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{eff}}
is proportional to a stabilizer. The last equality follows from the orthogonality of Bell states. Therefore, combining the probabilities across all representatives of the coset
SP_{r}
,

\Pr(\text{sample $P_r$ modulo stabilizers})=\tr\left(\sum_{\sigma\in S}O_{\sigma P_{r}}\,\widetilde{\rho}\right)=\sum_{\sigma\in S}\Pr(\text{sample $\sigma P_r$})=\chi_{rr}(t).

(167)

Similarly, by setting
P_{r}=I_{n^{\prime}}
, we get
\Pr(\text{sample a stabilizer})=\chi_{00}(t)
.

Combining the expressions for diagonal
\chi
matrix entries above with
Section

E.2
proves the remark.
∎

Appendix F
Dissipator (and Hamiltonian-Overlapping-Dissipator) Coefficients Learning

Recall the unknown
n
-qubit Lindbladian:

\mathcal{L}(\rho)=-i\sum_{P_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{H}}h_{k}\commutator{P_k}{\rho}+\sum_{P_{k},P_{j}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}}a_{kj}\left(P_{k}\rho P_{j}-\tfrac{1}{2}\anticommutator{P_j P_k}{\rho}\right).

(168)

Appendix
D
describes a learner for

\mathcal{S}_{D,\eta}\coloneq\{P_{k}:a_{kk}\geq\eta\}
, the dissipator structure at scale
\eta
. Appendix
E
then learns both the structure and the coefficients of the Hamiltonian disjoint from the dissipator,
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{HDD},\eta}\coloneq\mathcal{S}_{H}\setminus\mathcal{S}_{D,\eta}
, to accuracy
\varepsilon
. It remains to learn the dissipator coefficients and the Hamiltonian-Overlapping-Dissipator Hamiltonian coefficients of the Lindbladian to accuracy
\varepsilon
. In particular, this can be achieved by learning all Hamiltonian coefficients of Paulis in
\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{HOD},\eta}\coloneq\mathcal{S}_{H}\cap\mathfrak{D}_{\eta}
and all dissipator coefficients supported on the
\eta
-heavy dissipator footprint
\mathfrak{D}_{\eta}
(
Appendix

E
), for the choices of
\eta
specified in Appendix
E.5
. This section solves a generalized version of this task.

In this section, we describe a standard-quantum-limited learner (
Algorithm

8
) for specified Lindbladian coefficients that works for arbitrary locality and does not require linear-system inversion. To do this, we run the classical shadows protocol
71
on Choi states of the short-time Lindbladian evolution. Our main contribution is an explicit construction of
2n
-qubit low-rank observables whose first derivatives directly equal the desired coefficient values. We then measure these observables at different time nodes and use Chebyshev interpolation, similarly to
63
;
28
, to estimate their first derivatives and hence the Lindbladian coefficients to accuracy
\varepsilon
. Compared with the Hamiltonian learner of
28
, which also runs classical shadows protocol on Choi states, this gives a black-box evolution-time improvement from
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(n\|H\|_{\infty}^{3}/\varepsilon^{4}\right)
to
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\Lambda_{L}/\varepsilon^{2}\right)
, where
\Lambda_{L}\geq\|\mathcal{L}\|_{\diamond}
. Our approach parallels shadow process tomography
89
;
85
; however, by constructing explicit observables, it avoids the need to construct a full-rank linear system and hence avoids the associated numerical conditioning and inversion. It also matches the
\varepsilon
-scaling of the pseudo-Choi-state-based Hamiltonian learner of
29
, inviting further study of the comparison between these two resources.

F.1
Operator content of the Lindbladian Choi state

We work on
\mathcal{H}_{L}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{R}
with
n
Bell pairs,

\ket{\Phi_+}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\ket{00}+\ket{11}),\qquad\rho_{0}=\bigl(\ket{\Phi_+}\bra{\Phi_+}\bigr)^{\otimes n}.

Unless stated otherwise, operators act on the left register only, i.e.
A\equiv A\otimes I_{R}
.

Expanding the Lindbladian Choi state

\mathcal{E}_{t}(\rho_{0})\coloneqq(e^{t\mathcal{L}}\otimes I_{R})(\rho_{0})
to first order in time gives

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{t}(\rho_{0})

\displaystyle=\rho_{0}+t\mathcal{L}(\rho_{0})+\mathcal{O}\left(t^{2}\right)

(169)

\displaystyle=\rho_{0}\left(1-\sum_{P_{m}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}}a_{mm}t\right)-\sum_{P_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{H}}ih_{k}t\,\commutator{P_k}{\rho_0}+\sum_{P_{m}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}}a_{mm}t\,P_{m}\rho_{0}P_{m}

\displaystyle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P_{l},P_{m}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}\\
l>m\end{subarray}}\real(a_{lm})t\left(P_{l}\rho_{0}P_{m}+P_{m}\rho_{0}P_{l}-\frac{1}{2}\anticommutator{\acomm{P_m}{P_l}}{\rho_0}\right)

\displaystyle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P_{l},P_{m}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}\\
l>m\end{subarray}}i\imaginary(a_{lm})t\left(P_{l}\rho_{0}P_{m}-P_{m}\rho_{0}P_{l}-\frac{1}{2}\anticommutator{\comm{P_m}{P_l}}{\rho_0}\right)+\mathcal{O}\left(t^{2}\right).

In this expansion, we used the Hermiticity of the Kossakowski matrix,
a_{ml}=a_{lm}^{*}
, to collect the off-diagonal dissipator terms into real and imaginary parts. This motivates the following definition of observables:

Definition F.1

(Lindbladian Choi state observables)
.

Given the maximally entangled state
\rho_{0}=\bigl(\ket{\Phi_+}\bra{\Phi_+}\bigr)^{\otimes n}
and the Lindbladian superoperator in the Pauli basis

\mathcal{L}(\rho)=\mathcal{H}(\rho)+\mathcal{D}(\rho)=-\sum_{P_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{H}}ih_{k}\commutator{P_k}{\rho}+\sum_{P_{l},P_{m}\in\mathcal{S}_{D}}a_{lm}\left(P_{l}\rho P_{m}-\frac{1}{2}\anticommutator{P_mP_l}{\rho}\right),

we define the following Hilbert–Schmidt orthonormal set of Hermitian operators for
k,m,l\in\{1,2,\dots,4^{n}-1\}
:

\displaystyle O_{k}

\displaystyle\coloneq-\frac{i}{\sqrt{2}}\commutator{P_k}{\rho_0},

(170)

\displaystyle V_{m}

\displaystyle\coloneq P_{m}\rho_{0}P_{m},

(171)

\displaystyle R_{lm}

\displaystyle\coloneq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(P_{l}\rho_{0}P_{m}+P_{m}\rho_{0}P_{l}\right),\qquad l>m,

(172)

\displaystyle Q_{lm}

\displaystyle\coloneq\frac{i}{\sqrt{2}}\left(P_{l}\rho_{0}P_{m}-P_{m}\rho_{0}P_{l}\right),\qquad l>m.

(173)

Here
\rho_{0}
is a
2n
-qubit state, while the Pauli operators
P_{k}
are
n
-qubit Paulis acting nontrivially only on the left register.

Proof of Hilbert–Schmidt orthonormality.

Let
\rho_{0}=\ket{\Phi_0}\bra{\Phi_0}
, and for
k\geq 1
set
\ket{\Phi_k}=(P_{k}\otimes I_{R})\ket{\Phi_0}
. Then

\innerproduct{\Phi_0}{\Phi_k}=0,\qquad\innerproduct{\Phi_k}{\Phi_{k'}}=\delta_{kk^{\prime}}.

Moreover,

P_{\ell}\rho_{0}P_{m}=\ket{\Phi_\ell}\bra{\Phi_m},\qquad\commutator{P_k}{\rho_0}=\ket{\Phi_k}\bra{\Phi_0}-\ket{\Phi_0}\bra{\Phi_k}.

Using

\left\langle|a\rangle\!\langle b|,\ |c\rangle\!\langle d|\right\rangle_{\!HS}=\langle a|c\rangle\langle d|b\rangle,

we compute

\displaystyle\langle V_{m},V_{m^{\prime}}\rangle_{\!HS}

\displaystyle=\langle\Phi_{m}|\Phi_{m^{\prime}}\rangle\langle\Phi_{m^{\prime}}|\Phi_{m}\rangle=\delta_{mm^{\prime}},

\displaystyle\langle O_{k},O_{k^{\prime}}\rangle_{\!HS}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\langle\Phi_{k^{\prime}}|\Phi_{k}\rangle\langle\Phi_{0}|\Phi_{0}\rangle+\langle\Phi_{0}|\Phi_{0}\rangle\langle\Phi_{k}|\Phi_{k^{\prime}}\rangle\right)=\delta_{kk^{\prime}},

\displaystyle\langle O_{k},V_{m}\rangle_{\!HS}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\langle R_{lm},R_{l^{\prime}m^{\prime}}\rangle_{\!HS}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\delta_{ll^{\prime}}\delta_{mm^{\prime}}+\delta_{lm^{\prime}}\delta_{l^{\prime}m}+\delta_{ml^{\prime}}\delta_{m^{\prime}l}+\delta_{mm^{\prime}}\delta_{l^{\prime}l}\right)=\delta_{ll^{\prime}}\delta_{mm^{\prime}},

\displaystyle\langle Q_{lm},Q_{l^{\prime}m^{\prime}}\rangle_{\!HS}

\displaystyle=\delta_{ll^{\prime}}\delta_{mm^{\prime}},

\displaystyle\langle R_{lm},Q_{l^{\prime}m^{\prime}}\rangle_{\!HS}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\langle R_{lm},V_{p}\rangle_{\!HS}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\delta_{mp}\delta_{pl}+\delta_{lp}\delta_{pm}\right)=0,

\displaystyle\langle Q_{lm},V_{p}\rangle_{\!HS}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\langle O_{k},R_{lm}\rangle_{\!HS}

\displaystyle=\langle O_{k},Q_{lm}\rangle_{\!HS}=0.

Here the swapped-pair contribution in
\langle R_{lm},R_{l^{\prime}m^{\prime}}\rangle_{\!HS}
is ruled out by the ordering convention
l>m
and
l^{\prime}>m^{\prime}
, and the remaining cross terms vanish because they contain an overlap of the form
\langle\Phi_{0}|\Phi_{p}\rangle
with
p\geq 1
. Thus
\{O_{k}\}\cup\{V_{m}\}\cup\{R_{lm}\}\cup\{Q_{lm}\}
is Hilbert–Schmidt orthonormal.
∎

Importantly, the first derivatives of these observables at
t=0
directly give the Lindbladian coefficients.

Lemma F.2

(Derivatives of Lindbladian Choi state observables)
.

Let
h_{k}
and
a_{lm}
be the Hamiltonian coefficients and Kossakowski matrix elements of the Lindbladian generator in the Pauli basis. Then the first derivatives at
t=0
of the observables from
Section

F.1
satisfy

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\tr\bigl(O_{k}\,\mathcal{E}_{t}(\rho_{0})\bigr)\Big|_{t=0}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2}\,h_{k},

(174)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\tr\bigl(V_{m}\,\mathcal{E}_{t}(\rho_{0})\bigr)\Big|_{t=0}

\displaystyle=a_{mm},

(175)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\tr\bigl(R_{lm}\,\mathcal{E}_{t}(\rho_{0})\bigr)\Big|_{t=0}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2}\,\real(a_{lm}),\qquad l>m,

(176)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\tr\bigl(Q_{lm}\,\mathcal{E}_{t}(\rho_{0})\bigr)\Big|_{t=0}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2}\,\imaginary(a_{lm}),\qquad l>m.

(177)

Proof.

For any time-independent observable
O
,

\frac{d}{dt}\tr\bigl(O\,\mathcal{E}_{t}(\rho_{0})\bigr)\Big|_{t=0}=\tr\bigl(O\,\mathcal{L}(\rho_{0})\bigr),

(178)

so only the
t
-linear part matters. Let
\ket{\Phi_0}=\ket{\Phi_+}^{\otimes n}
,
\rho_{0}=\ket{\Phi_0}\bra{\Phi_0}
, and
\ket{\Phi_k}=P_{k}\ket{\Phi_0}
.

Using
\commutator{P_k}{\rho_0}=i\sqrt{2}\,O_{k}
and, for
l>m
,

P_{l}\rho_{0}P_{m}+P_{m}\rho_{0}P_{l}=\sqrt{2}\,R_{lm},\qquad P_{l}\rho_{0}P_{m}-P_{m}\rho_{0}P_{l}=-i\sqrt{2}\,Q_{lm},

the
t
-linear part of Eq. (
169
) gives

\mathcal{L}(\rho_{0})=\sqrt{2}\sum_{k}h_{k}O_{k}+\sum_{m}a_{mm}V_{m}+\sqrt{2}\sum_{l>m}\bigl(\real(a_{lm})R_{lm}+\imaginary(a_{lm})Q_{lm}\bigr)+\Xi,

(179)

where

\Xi\coloneq-\left(\sum_{m}a_{mm}\right)\rho_{0}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{l>m}\real(a_{lm})\anticommutator{\acomm{P_m}{P_l}}{\rho_0}-\frac{i}{2}\sum_{l>m}\imaginary(a_{lm})\anticommutator{\comm{P_m}{P_l}}{\rho_0}.

We now check that
\Xi
is orthogonal to all observables in
Section

F.1
. For any operator
X
on the left register,

\anticommutator{X}{\rho_0}=X\rho_{0}+\rho_{0}X=\ket{X\Phi_0}\bra{\Phi_0}+\ket{\Phi_0}\bra{X\Phi_0}.

Since
V_{m}
,
R_{lm}
, and
Q_{lm}
are linear combinations of
\ket{\Phi_r}\bra{\Phi_s}
with
r,s\geq 1
, we have

\displaystyle\tr(V_m\rho_0)

\displaystyle=\tr(R_{lm}\rho_0)=\tr(Q_{lm}\rho_0)=0,

(180)

\displaystyle\tr\bigl(V_{m}\anticommutator{X}{\rho_0}\bigr)

\displaystyle=\tr\bigl(R_{lm}\anticommutator{X}{\rho_0}\bigr)=\tr\bigl(Q_{lm}\anticommutator{X}{\rho_0}\bigr)=0.

(181)

It remains to check the Hamiltonian observables. Write
O_{k}=-\frac{i}{\sqrt{2}}\commutator{P_k}{\rho_0}
and let
X
be a nonidentity Pauli string, standing for either
\commutator{P_m}{P_l}
or
\anticommutator{P_m}{P_l}
when the corresponding expression is nonzero. Using the Hermiticity of
O_{k}
,

\tr\bigl(O_{k}\anticommutator{X}{\rho_0}\bigr)=-\frac{i}{\sqrt{2}}\tr\bigl(\commutator{P_k}{\rho_0}(X\rho_{0}+\rho_{0}X)\bigr).

Expanding and using
\rho_{0}^{2}=\rho_{0}
gives

\tr\bigl(\commutator{P_k}{\rho_0}(X\rho_{0}+\rho_{0}X)\bigr)=\tr(P_k\rho_0X\rho_0)+\tr(P_k\rho_0X)-\tr(P_kX\rho_0)-\tr(\rho_0P_k\rho_0X).

Using
\rho_{0}A\rho_{0}=\bra{\Phi_0}A\ket{\Phi_0}\rho_{0}
, the first and last terms vanish because
\bra{\Phi_0}P_{k}\ket{\Phi_0}=0
. The remaining terms are

\tr(P_k\rho_0X)-\tr(P_kX\rho_0)=\bra{\Phi_0}XP_{k}\ket{\Phi_0}-\bra{\Phi_0}P_{k}X\ket{\Phi_0}.

Each term can be nonzero only if
P_{k}X
is proportional to the identity, in which case the two terms cancel. Hence
\tr\bigl(O_{k}\anticommutator{\comm{P_m}{P_l}}{\rho_0}\bigr)=\tr\bigl(O_{k}\anticommutator{\acomm{P_m}{P_l}}{\rho_0}\bigr)=0
. Also,
\tr(O_k\rho_0)=0
. Therefore
O_{k}
is Hilbert–Schmidt orthogonal to every term in
\Xi
.

Taking the Hilbert–Schmidt inner product of Eq. (
179
) with each observable and using the orthonormality from
Section

F.1
, together with the orthogonality to
\Xi
above, gives

\displaystyle\tr\bigl(O_{k}\mathcal{L}(\rho_{0})\bigr)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2}\,h_{k},

\displaystyle\tr\bigl(V_{m}\mathcal{L}(\rho_{0})\bigr)

\displaystyle=a_{mm},

\displaystyle\tr\bigl(R_{lm}\mathcal{L}(\rho_{0})\bigr)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2}\,\real(a_{lm}),\qquad l>m,

\displaystyle\tr\bigl(Q_{lm}\mathcal{L}(\rho_{0})\bigr)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2}\,\imaginary(a_{lm}),\qquad l>m.

Combining these identities with Eq. (
178
) yields Eq. (
174
), Eq. (
175
), Eq. (
176
), and Eq. (
177
).
∎

F.2
Review of classical shadows

The key subroutine we use for learning the Lindbladian coefficients is the classical shadows protocol
(
71
)
. The goal of the classical shadows is to estimate properties of a quantum state
\rho
using a small number of single-copy measurements. The key step in the classical shadows algorithm involves creating a classical representation
\hat{\rho}
of
\rho
by applying random operations and performing measurements in the computational basis.

The version of classical shadows we consider requires applying a random Clifford operator
U_{i}\in\mathrm{Cl}(2^{n})
to the state
\rho
in each of
N
rounds. After applying
U_{i}
, one measures each qubit in the computational basis. The outcome is recorded as a bit string
\ket{b_i}
of length
n
, where the
j
th bit of
\ket{b_i}
records the measurement result of the
j
th qubit (i.e.,
\ket{b_i}_{j}=0
corresponds to measuring
\ket{0}
). The corresponding classical snapshot is
\sigma_{i}=U_{i}^{\dagger}\ket{b_i}\bra{b_i}U_{i}
. The classical shadow for random Clifford measurements is then defined as:

Definition F.3

(Classical Shadow (From Clifford Measurements))
.

Given
N
copies of an
n
-qubit quantum state
\rho
, the classical shadows procedure based on random Clifford measurements returns a classical shadow of
\rho
which is of the form

\hat{\rho}=\{\hat{\rho}_{i}\mid i\in\mathbb{Z}_{N}\}

where

\hat{\rho}_{i}=(2^{n}+1)(U_{i}^{\dagger}\ket{b_i}\bra{b_i}U_{i})-\mathbb{I}

The hat notation signifies that
\hat{\rho}
is an estimator.

The snapshots
\sigma_{i}
are stabilizer states and can be prepared efficiently using only Clifford operations starting from
\ket{0}^{\otimes n}
. Consequently, each snapshot requires only
\mathcal{O}(n^{2})
classical bits to store
1
. The full classical shadow of
\rho
consists of
N
such snapshots, requiring
N
copies of
\rho
.

We copy the informal version of the theorem here for completeness:

Theorem F.4

(Classical Shadows
71
)
.

Classical shadows of size
N
suffice to predict
M
arbitrary linear target functions

\tr(O_1 \rho),\ldots,\tr(O_M \rho)
up to additive error
\epsilon_{s}
with probability at least
1-\delta
given that

N\geq\text{(order)}\,\log(M/\delta)\,\max_{i}\norm{O_i}_{\text{shadow}}^{2}/\epsilon_{s}^{2}.

The definition of the norm
\norm{O_i}_{\text{shadow}}
depends on the ensemble of unitary
transformations used to create the classical shadow.

For the Clifford ensemble we use, Huang et al.
71
showed that
\norm{O_i}_{\text{shadow}}\leq\tr(O_i^2).
Hence, we can estimate
M
observables of constant Hilbert-Schmidt norm using
N=\mathcal{O}\left(\log(M/\delta)/\epsilon_{s}^{2}\right)
copies of
\rho
.

F.3
Standard-Quantum-Limited coefficient learning

We now formulate the coefficient-learning task for arbitrary specified Lindbladian coefficients. Let

\mathcal{Z}_{H}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{n}\setminus\{I\}
be the set of Hamiltonian Paulis whose coefficients we wish to estimate, and let

\mathcal{Z}_{D}\subseteq\{(P_{l},P_{m}):P_{l},P_{m}\in\mathcal{P}_{n}\setminus\{I\},\,l\geq m\}

be the set of Kossakowski matrix entries whose coefficients we wish to estimate. We write

\mathcal{Z}_{D}^{\mathrm{diag}}\coloneq\{P_{m}:(P_{m},P_{m})\in\mathcal{Z}_{D}\}
and

\mathcal{Z}_{D}^{\mathrm{off}}\coloneq\{(P_{l},P_{m})\in\mathcal{Z}_{D}:l>m\}
, and define the corresponding observable count

N_{\mathrm{obs}}\coloneq|\mathcal{Z}_{H}|+|\mathcal{Z}_{D}^{\mathrm{diag}}|+2|\mathcal{Z}_{D}^{\mathrm{off}}|.

The factor of
2
appears because each off-diagonal Kossakowski entry is reconstructed from separate observables for its real and imaginary parts. The algorithm below constructs the observables
O_{k}
,
V_{m}
,
R_{lm}
, and
Q_{lm}
associated with these specified sets, estimates their first derivatives from Choi-state classical shadows at Chebyshev time nodes, and converts the derivative estimates directly into the requested coefficients using
Section

F.1
.

Inputs :
Hamiltonian coefficient set
\mathcal{Z}_{H}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{n}\setminus\{I\}
;

Kossakowski coefficient set
\mathcal{Z}_{D}\subseteq\{(P_{l},P_{m}):P_{l},P_{m}\in\mathcal{P}_{n}\setminus\{I\},\,l\geq m\}
;

accuracy and confidence parameters
\epsilon,\delta_{\mathrm{coeff}}\in(0,1)
;

upper bound
\Lambda_{L}\geq\|\mathcal{L}\|_{\diamond}
; access to
e^{t\mathcal{L}}
;
n
noiseless ancillas.

Output :
Estimates
\{\widehat{h}_{k}:P_{k}\in\mathcal{Z}_{H}\}
and
\{\widehat{a}_{lm}:(P_{l},P_{m})\in\mathcal{Z}_{D}\}
to accuracy
\epsilon
with probability at least
1-\delta_{\mathrm{coeff}}
.

Schedule

\tau_{\max}\leftarrow\Theta(1/\Lambda_{L})
,
r\leftarrow\Theta(\log(\Lambda_L/\epsilon))
,
\epsilon_{s}\leftarrow\Theta(\epsilon/(\Lambda_{L}r^{3}))
,
\delta_{s}\leftarrow\Theta(\delta_{\mathrm{coeff}}/(r+1))

\{t_{q}\}_{q=1}^{r+1}\leftarrow
Chebyshev–Gauss nodes on
(0,\tau_{\max})
;
\{\alpha_{q}\}_{q=1}^{r+1}\leftarrow
first-derivative interpolation weights

\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{tar}}\leftarrow\{O_{k}:P_{k}\in\mathcal{Z}_{H}\}\cup\{V_{m}:(P_{m},P_{m})\in\mathcal{Z}_{D}\}\cup\{R_{lm},Q_{lm}:(P_{l},P_{m})\in\mathcal{Z}_{D},\ l>m\}

for

q\leftarrow 1

to

r+1

do

Prepare copies of
\rho(t_{q})\leftarrow(e^{t_{q}\mathcal{L}}\otimes I_{R})(\rho_{0})

//
prepare Choi states

Estimate all
\{\tr(O\rho(t_q)):O\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{tar}}\}
to accuracy
\epsilon_{s}
using global Clifford classical shadows with failure probability
\delta_{s}

foreach

O\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{tar}}

do

\widehat{\mu}^{(1)}_{O}\leftarrow\sum_{q=1}^{r+1}\alpha_{q}\,\widehat{\tr(O\rho(t_q))}

//
first-derivative estimation via Chebyshev interpolation

foreach

P_{k}\in\mathcal{Z}_{H}

do

\widehat{h}_{k}\leftarrow\widehat{\mu}^{(1)}_{O_{k}}/\sqrt{2}

foreach

(P_{m},P_{m})\in\mathcal{Z}_{D}

do

\widehat{a}_{mm}\leftarrow\widehat{\mu}^{(1)}_{V_{m}}

foreach

(P_{l},P_{m})\in\mathcal{Z}_{D}
with
l>m

do

\widehat{a}_{lm}\leftarrow\bigl(\widehat{\mu}^{(1)}_{R_{lm}}+i\widehat{\mu}^{(1)}_{Q_{lm}}\bigr)/\sqrt{2}

return

\{\widehat{h}_{k}:P_{k}\in\mathcal{Z}_{H}\}
and
\{\widehat{a}_{lm}:(P_{l},P_{m})\in\mathcal{Z}_{D}\}
.

Algorithm 8

Specified Lindbladian coefficient learning via Choi-state classical shadows

Theorem F.5

(Specified Lindbladian coefficient learning via Choi-state classical shadows)
.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be an
n
-qubit Lindbladian and assume
\Lambda_{L}\geq\max\{1,\|\mathcal{L}\|_{\diamond}\}
. Assume black-box access to
e^{t\mathcal{L}}
. Fix coefficient index sets

\mathcal{Z}_{H}\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{n}\setminus\{I\}
and

\mathcal{Z}_{D}\subseteq\{(P_{l},P_{m}):P_{l},P_{m}\in\mathcal{P}_{n}\setminus\{I\},\,l\geq m\}
.
Let

N_{\mathrm{obs}}\coloneq|\mathcal{Z}_{H}|+|\mathcal{Z}_{D}^{\mathrm{diag}}|+2|\mathcal{Z}_{D}^{\mathrm{off}}|,

where
\mathcal{Z}_{D}^{\mathrm{diag}}\coloneq\{P_{m}:(P_{m},P_{m})\in\mathcal{Z}_{D}\}
and

\mathcal{Z}_{D}^{\mathrm{off}}\coloneq\{(P_{l},P_{m})\in\mathcal{Z}_{D}:l>m\}
. Fix
\epsilon,\delta\in(0,1)
. Then running
Algorithm

8
returns coefficient estimates

\{\widehat{h}_{k}:P_{k}\in\mathcal{Z}_{H}\}
and

\{\widehat{a}_{lm}:(P_{l},P_{m})\in\mathcal{Z}_{D}\}
satisfying:

1.

(Accuracy) With probability
\geq 1-\delta
,

|\widehat{h}_{k}-h_{k}|\leq\epsilon,\qquad|\widehat{a}_{lm}-a_{lm}|\leq\epsilon

for all
P_{k}\in\mathcal{Z}_{H}
and all
(P_{l},P_{m})\in\mathcal{Z}_{D}
.

2.

(Evolution time) It applies
e^{\mathcal{L}t}
for total evolution time

t_{\mathrm{tot}}=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\Lambda_{L}}{\epsilon^{2}}\log\!\left(\frac{\Lambda_{L}}{\epsilon}\right)^{7}\log\!\left(\frac{N_{\mathrm{obs}}\log(\Lambda_L/\epsilon)}{\delta}\right)\right).

3.

(Time resolution) It only ever applies
e^{\mathcal{L}t_{q}}
for
t_{q}
such that

t_{q}=\Omega\!\left(\frac{1}{\Lambda_{L}\log(\Lambda_L/\epsilon)^{2}}\right)\quad\mbox{and}\quad\min_{1\leq q\leq r}|t_{q+1}-t_{q}|=\Omega\!\left(\frac{1}{\Lambda_{L}\log(\Lambda_L/\epsilon)^{2}}\right).

4.

(Number of experiments) It uses

N_{\exp}=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\Lambda_{L}^{2}}{\epsilon^{2}}\log\!\left(\frac{\Lambda_{L}}{\epsilon}\right)^{7}\log\!\left(\frac{N_{\mathrm{obs}}\log(\Lambda_L/\epsilon)}{\delta}\right)\right)

quantum circuits, each preparing
n
Bell pairs, evolving one register under
e^{\mathcal{L}t}
for some
t\in(0,\tau_{\max})
, applying global random Clifford measurements on the resulting
2n
-qubit Choi state, and measuring in the computational basis.

5.

(Classical overhead) A direct implementation using a phase-sensitive Clifford simulator has classical post-processing cost

\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(N_{\exp}(n^{3}+N_{\mathrm{obs}}n^{2})\right),

where
N_{\exp}
is the number of Choi-state classical-shadow snapshots in item
(4)
.

Proof.

If
N_{\mathrm{obs}}=0
, the algorithm returns empty estimate lists and the claim is trivial, so assume
N_{\mathrm{obs}}\geq 1
. Throughout this proof, we repeatedly use the classical shadows protocol from
Theorem

F.4
applied to the
e^{\mathcal{L}t}
Choi state and the unit-HS-norm
2n
-qubit observables from
Section

F.1
. The key observation we rely on is that the first derivatives of these observables are exactly the coefficients we wish to learn, as shown in
Section

F.1
. To estimate the first derivatives based on the observables at different time nodes, we use Chebyshev polynomial interpolation from
Theorem

B.10
.

We first form the target observable set

\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{tar}}\coloneq\{O_{k}:P_{k}\in\mathcal{Z}_{H}\}\cup\{V_{m}:(P_{m},P_{m})\in\mathcal{Z}_{D}\}\cup\{R_{lm},Q_{lm}:(P_{l},P_{m})\in\mathcal{Z}_{D},\ l>m\}.

By construction,
|\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{tar}}|=N_{\mathrm{obs}}
. For
O\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{tar}}
, define

f_{O}(t)\coloneq\tr\bigl(O\,(e^{t\mathcal{L}}\otimes I_{R})(\rho_{0})\bigr).

Since
\Lambda_{L}\geq\|\mathcal{L}\|_{\diamond}
, the functions

f_{O}(t)
satisfy the derivative-growth assumption required by

Section

B.5
. Indeed, fix

O\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{tar}}
and write

\rho_{t}\coloneq(e^{t\mathcal{L}}\otimes I_{R})(\rho_{0}).

Since
e^{t\mathcal{L}}
is CPTP for
t\geq 0
,
\rho_{t}
is a density
operator and hence
\norm{\rho_t}_{1}=1
. For every integer

\ell\geq 1
,

\displaystyle f_{O}^{(\ell)}(t)

\displaystyle=\tr\!\left(O\,(\mathcal{L}^{\ell}\otimes I_{R})(\rho_{t})\right),

and therefore, by Hölder’s inequality for Schatten norms,

|f_{O}^{(\ell)}(t)|\leq\norm{O}_{\infty}\norm{(\Lindblad^\ell\otimes I_R)(\rho_t)}_{1}\leq\norm{O}_{\infty}\norm{\Lindblad^\ell}_{\diamond}\norm{\rho_t}_{1}\leq\norm{O}_{\infty}\norm{\Lindblad}_{\diamond}^{\ell}.

(182)

All observables in
\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{tar}}
have

\norm{O}_{\infty}\leq 1
, so
|f_{O}^{(\ell)}(t)|\leq\Lambda_{L}^{\ell}
for all
\ell\geq 1
.

We now choose the Chebyshev–Gauss nodes

\{t_{q}\}_{q=1}^{r+1}\subset(0,\tau_{\max})
with the parameters from

Section

B.5
, applied with

B=1
and
\Lambda=\Lambda_{L}
. By

Theorem

B.10
and
Section

B.5
,
estimating each observable value
f_{O}(t_{q})
to accuracy
\epsilon_{s}

suffices to estimate
f_{O}^{\prime}(0)
to accuracy
\epsilon
. Thus, for
each
O\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{tar}}
, the Chebyshev estimator

\widehat{f}_{O}^{(1)}(0)\coloneq\sum_{q=1}^{r+1}\alpha_{q}\,\widehat{f}_{O}(t_{q})

satisfies

|\widehat{f}_{O}^{(1)}(0)-f_{O}^{\prime}(0)|\leq\epsilon

on the event that all nodewise estimates obey

|\widehat{f}_{O}(t_{q})-f_{O}(t_{q})|\leq\epsilon_{s}
.

At each Chebyshev node, we apply global Clifford classical shadows to the Choi state
(e^{t_{q}\mathcal{L}}\otimes I_{R})(\rho_{0})
and estimate all observables in
\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{tar}}
. By
Theorem

F.4
, estimating all
N_{\mathrm{obs}}
observables at one node to accuracy
\epsilon_{s}
with failure probability at most
\delta_{s}=\delta/(r+1)
requires

N_{\mathrm{node}}=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{\epsilon_{s}^{2}}\log\!\left(\frac{N_{\mathrm{obs}}}{\delta_{s}}\right)\right)=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\Lambda_{L}^{2}r^{6}}{\epsilon^{2}}\log\!\left(\frac{N_{\mathrm{obs}}r}{\delta}\right)\right)

Choi-state copies. A union bound over the
r+1
Chebyshev nodes guarantees that all observable values, and hence all first-derivative estimates, are simultaneously accurate with probability at least
1-\delta
.

We then convert derivatives to coefficients using
Section

F.1
. For each
P_{k}\in\mathcal{Z}_{H}
, set

\widehat{h}_{k}\coloneq\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\widehat{f}_{O_{k}}^{(1)}(0).

For each diagonal Kossakowski entry
(P_{m},P_{m})\in\mathcal{Z}_{D}
, set

\widehat{a}_{mm}\coloneq\widehat{f}_{V_{m}}^{(1)}(0).

For each off-diagonal Kossakowski entry
(P_{l},P_{m})\in\mathcal{Z}_{D}
with
l>m
, set

\widehat{a}_{lm}\coloneq\frac{\widehat{f}_{R_{lm}}^{(1)}(0)+i\,\widehat{f}_{Q_{lm}}^{(1)}(0)}{\sqrt{2}}.

Using Eq. (
174
), Eq. (
175
), Eq. (
176
), and Eq. (
177
), and decreasing the hidden constant in
\epsilon_{s}
by an absolute factor to account for the real and imaginary parts of off-diagonal entries, we obtain

|\widehat{h}_{k}-h_{k}|\leq\epsilon,\qquad|\widehat{a}_{lm}-a_{lm}|\leq\epsilon

for all specified coefficients. This proves item
(1)
.

The number of Choi-state experiments is

N_{\exp}=(r+1)N_{\mathrm{node}}=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\Lambda_{L}^{2}}{\epsilon^{2}}\log\!\left(\frac{\Lambda_{L}}{\epsilon}\right)^{7}\log\!\left(\frac{N_{\mathrm{obs}}\log(\Lambda_L/\epsilon)}{\delta}\right)\right),

which proves item
(4)
. Since every experiment uses evolution time at most
\tau_{\max}=\Theta(1/\Lambda_{L})
, the total black-box evolution time is

t_{\mathrm{tot}}\leq N_{\exp}\tau_{\max}=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\Lambda_{L}}{\epsilon^{2}}\log\!\left(\frac{\Lambda_{L}}{\epsilon}\right)^{7}\log\!\left(\frac{N_{\mathrm{obs}}\log(\Lambda_L/\epsilon)}{\delta}\right)\right),

which proves item
(2)
. Finally,
(3)
is satisfied by
Section

B.5
.

The classical overhead in item
(5)
is dominated by evaluating the observables in
\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{tar}}
on the classical-shadow snapshots from
Section

F.2
. Since
O_{k},V_{m},R_{lm},Q_{lm}
are rank-one or rank-two operators in the Bell basis, evaluating them requires phase-sensitive stabilizer amplitudes. We use a phase-sensitive Clifford simulator such as
24
. Equivalently, let
C_{\mathrm{Bell}}
be the fixed Clifford mapping computational-basis states to the Bell basis. For each
2n
-qubit snapshot, we first apply
C_{\mathrm{Bell}}^{\dagger}
classically, so that overlaps with Bell states reduce to computational-basis amplitudes. This fixed Bell-basis change uses
\mathcal{O}\left(n\right)
Hadamards and CNOTs; in the phase-sensitive simulator, the Hadamard updates dominate and cost
\mathcal{O}\left(n^{3}\right)
per snapshot. After this preprocessing, each observable evaluation only requires computing the relevant computational-basis stabilizer amplitudes, costing
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(n^{2}\right)
per observable. Thus a direct implementation has post-processing cost

\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(N_{\exp}n^{3}+N_{\exp}N_{\mathrm{obs}}n^{2}\right).

∎

Remark F.6

(Replacing
\Lambda_{L}
by the sparsity scale
M
)
.

The coefficient-learning guarantee above is stated in terms of an arbitrary available upper bound

\Lambda_{L}\geq\|\mathcal{L}\|_{\diamond}
. Under the coefficient normalization, we can prove a crude upper bound
\norm{\Lindblad}_{\diamond}\leq 2M
, as shown in
Section

B.3
. Thus, one can take
\Lambda_{L}=2M
. With this choice, the Chebyshev schedule in

Algorithm

8
becomes

\tau_{\max}=\Theta(1/M),\qquad r=\Theta(\log(M/\epsilon)),\qquad\epsilon_{s}=\Theta\!\left(\frac{\epsilon}{Mr^{3}}\right),

and the resource bounds in
Theorem

F.5

recover the
M
-dependent scaling stated in the main text.

Appendix G
Robustness to interface noise

Most previous work on quantum Hamiltonian learning has restricted analysis of implementation noise to the state-preparation-and-measurement (SPAM) model, arguing that
O(\epsilon^{-1})
Heisenberg scaling can be retained in the presence of errors at these stages. As has been made transparent in the metrological setting
148
;
40
, however, SPAM errors are not the primary obstacle for Heisenberg-limited quantum sensing: instead, ambient errors can relegate a sensing protocol to the SQL unless error-correction or other error mitigation strategies can be leveraged to distinguish signal from noise. Our work completely characterizes the power of quantum error-correction in this regard, as we provide the first algorithm to simultaneously learn the entire ambient dissipator while learning the superposed Hamiltonian in the Heisenberg limit.

However,
35
illustrates that error-correction of the ambient dissipator does not eliminate all barriers to learning an underlying Hamiltonian. In particular, their work points out that even while errors within a quantum device can be protected by quantum error-correction, the logical evolution by an ambient Lindbladian acts at the physical level, and absent additional structure, the coupling between signal and quantum computer cannot be assumed to be noiseless. This is laid bare in our algorithm: we allow probe qubits to evolve under the signal, then perform control including quantum error-correction and physical Pauli twirling on those
physical
degrees of freedom. In this section, we contend with these errors, demonstrating the inevitable tradeoff between Heisenberg-limited learning and the native error rate of quantum hardware.

For concreteness, we assume that the noisy interface between our hardware and the environment is modeled by a depolarizing channel described by the dissipator

\mathcal{L}_{\textnormal{dep}}^{\nu}(\rho)=\nu\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sum_{\sigma\in\{X,Y,Z\}}(\sigma^{(j)}\rho\sigma^{(j)}-\rho).

Here,
\nu
is a noise strength parameter governed by how noise-prone the hardware is, e.g. the relaxation times of the hardware qubits. While we work with depolarizing noise as it is a well-studied and standard noise model, we note that the following discussion can be easily extended to other noise models. Beyond parametrizing the noise strength within the generator, note that this Lindbladian will cause a non-infinitesimal error channel with error terms of various Pauli weights. The severity of these errors is governed by how long the channel evolves, which is in turn controlled by the speed of our quantum control. Operationally, let us suppose that each of our control steps (including twirling and quantum error correction) takes at most time
t_{c}
. Then
(\nu,t_{c})
are the two parameters governing the quality of our quantum device, and will control how well we are able to perform Heisenberg-limited Hamiltonian learning in the presence of implementation error.

Definition G.1

(Single-qubit depolarizing channel)
.

The single-qubit depolarizing channel with strength
\lambda\in[0,1]
is

\mathcal{D}_{\lambda}(\rho)=(1-\lambda)\rho+\lambda\,\frac{I}{2}

for any single-qubit density matrix
\rho
. The depolarizing channel is self-adjoint with respect to the Hilbert-Schmidt inner product, and on a general 2-by-2 matrix
A
, (the adjoint of)
\mathcal{D}
acts as

\mathcal{D}_{\lambda}(A)=(1-\lambda)A+\lambda\,\frac{\tr(A)\,I}{2}\,.

Note that
\exp(\mathcal{L}_{\textnormal{dep}}^\nu t_c)
and
\mathcal{D}_{\lambda}^{\otimes n}
are equivalent when
\lambda=1-\exp(-4\nu t_c)
. The following Lemma will capture the core effect of interface noise within our algorithm.

Lemma G.2
.

Let
\Psi
be any completely positive trace-nonincreasing map acting on
n
qubits, and
0\leq p\leq 1
. Moreover, let
\Phi
be a completely positive trace-nonincreasing map of the form

\Phi=(1-p)\Psi+p\Xi\ ,

where
\Xi
is any completely positive trace-nonincreasing map. Then for any integer
N>0
,

|\Psi^{N}-\Phi^{N}|_{\diamond}\leq 2pN\ .

Proof.

By telescoping,

\Psi^{N}-\Phi^{N}=\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}\Psi^{N-i-1}\circ(\Psi-\Phi)\circ\Phi^{i}\ .

Applying the triangle inequality and submultiplicativity of the diamond norm,

|\Psi^{N}-\Phi^{N}|_{\diamond}\leq\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}|\Psi^{N-i-1}|_{\diamond}|\Psi-\Phi|_{\diamond}|\Phi^{i}|_{\diamond}.

Since
\Psi
and
\Phi
are completely positive and trace-nonincreasing, their diamond norms are at most one, and hence

|\Psi^{N}-\Phi^{N}|_{\diamond}\leq N|\Psi-\Phi|_{\diamond}.

By definition of
\Phi
,
\Psi-\Phi=p(\Psi-\Xi)
and thus

|\Psi-\Phi|_{\diamond}=p|\Psi-\Xi|_{\diamond}\leq p\bigl(|\Psi|_{\diamond}+|\Xi|_{\diamond}\bigr)\leq 2p,

where we used that
\Psi
and
\Xi
are CPTNI. Combining the previous two displays completes the proof

|\Psi^{N}-\Phi^{N}|_{\diamond}\leq 2pN\ .

∎

Now recall that our algorithm interacts with the signal Lindbladian in three ways according to Eq. (
65
), Eq. (
67
), and Eq. (
66
). We define their noisy-interface versions here, using simplified indices
1,2,3
to denote the different maps:

•

QEC reshaping map:

A_{1}\coloneq\mathcal{R}\circ\bigl(e^{\mathcal{L}\tau}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}\bigr)\circ\mathcal{C}_{\Pi}
becomes

A_{1}^{\prime}\coloneq\mathcal{R}\circ e^{\mathcal{L}_{\textnormal{dep}}^{\nu}t_{c}}\circ\bigl(e^{\mathcal{L}\tau}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}\bigr)\circ e^{\mathcal{L}_{\textnormal{dep}}^{\nu}t_{c}}\circ\mathcal{C}_{\Pi}

•

Physical twirling + partial QEC reshaping:

A_{2}\coloneq\mathcal{R}\circ\frac{1}{4^{n}}\sum_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}\bigl(\mathcal{U}_{P}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}\bigr)\circ\bigl(e^{\mathcal{L}\tau}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}\bigr)\circ\bigl(\mathcal{U}_{P}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}\bigr)\circ\mathcal{C}_{\Pi}
becomes

A_{2}^{\prime}\coloneq\mathcal{R}\circ\frac{1}{4^{n}}\sum_{P\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}\bigl(\mathcal{U}_{P}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}\bigr)\circ e^{\mathcal{L}_{\textnormal{dep}}^{\nu}t_{c}}\circ\bigl(e^{\mathcal{L}\tau}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}\bigr)\circ e^{\mathcal{L}_{\textnormal{dep}}^{\nu}t_{c}}\circ\bigl(\mathcal{U}_{P}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}\bigr)\circ\mathcal{C}_{\Pi}

•

QEC reshaping + partial logical twirling:

A_{3}\coloneq\frac{1}{|\overline{\mathcal{K}}_{\overline{P}_{a}}|}\sum_{\overline{Q}\in\overline{\mathcal{K}}_{\overline{P}_{a}}}\Bigl(\mathcal{U}_{\overline{Q}}\circ\mathcal{R}\circ\bigl(e^{\mathcal{L}\tau}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}\bigr)\circ\mathcal{U}_{\overline{Q}}\Bigr)\circ\mathcal{C}_{\Pi}
becomes

A_{3}^{\prime}\coloneq\frac{1}{|\overline{\mathcal{K}}_{\overline{P}_{a}}|}\sum_{\overline{Q}\in\overline{\mathcal{K}}_{\overline{P}_{a}}}\Bigl(\mathcal{U}_{\overline{Q}}\circ\mathcal{R}\circ e^{\mathcal{L}_{\textnormal{dep}}^{\nu}t_{c}}\circ\bigl(e^{\mathcal{L}\tau}\otimes I_{n^{\prime}-n}\bigr)\circ e^{\mathcal{L}_{\textnormal{dep}}^{\nu}t_{c}}\circ\mathcal{U}_{\overline{Q}}\Bigr)\circ\mathcal{C}_{\Pi}

Note that
e^{\mathcal{L}_{\textnormal{dep}}^{\nu}t_{c}}
acts only on the
n
“probe” qubits as we may assume the remaining
n^{\prime}-n
qubits can be protected by quantum error-correction within the device. Next, we see that all three channels can be treated identically in the noisy case.

Lemma G.3
.

For each map
A_{i},i\in{1,2,3}
, and every integer
N>0
,

|A_{i}^{N}-A_{i}^{\prime N}|_{\diamond}\leq 2Np.

where
p\leq\left(1-e^{-8\nu t_{c}n}\right)
.

Proof.

Using
e^{\mathcal{L}_{\textnormal{dep}}^{\nu}t_{c}}=\mathcal{D}_{\lambda}^{\otimes n}
with
\lambda=1-e^{-4\nu t_{c}}
, a single interface-noise layer acts trivially on all
n
probe qubits with probability
(1-\lambda)^{n}
. Since each
A_{i}^{\prime}
contains two such layers, the probability that the composition of both layers acts trivially is at least

(1-\lambda)^{2n}=e^{-8\nu t_{c}n}.

On this branch, the noisy map coincides with
A_{i}
. All remaining branches define some completely positive trace-nonincreasing map
\Xi_{i}
. Hence

A_{i}^{\prime}=(1-p)A_{i}+p\Xi_{i},\qquad p\leq 1-(1-\lambda)^{2n}=1-e^{-8\nu t_{c}n}.

Applying
Appendix

G
with
\Psi=A_{i}
and
\Phi=A_{i}^{\prime}
gives

|A_{i}^{N}-A_{i}^{\prime N}|_{\diamond}\leq 2pN\leq 2N\left(1-e^{-8\nu t_{c}n}\right).

The averaging in
A_{2}
and
A_{3}
does not affect the argument, since the same convex decomposition is preserved under linear combinations.
∎

We are now prepared to state the effect of interface noise on the Heisenberg-limited sensitivity of our Hamiltonian learning algorithm.

Theorem G.4
.

In the presence of
(\nu,t_{c})
interface depolarizing noise,
Algorithm

7
succeeds in total evolution time
t=O(M^{2}/\epsilon)
when the following condition is satisfied:

\epsilon>c_{1}M^{2}\sqrt{\nu nt_{c}}.

where
c_{1}
is a universal constant.

Proof.

From
Section

C.4
, we have

\big\|\,A_{\tau}^{\,N}-e^{\,t\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}}\,\big\|_{\diamond}\ \leq\ \frac{t^{2}\,\|\mathcal{L}\|_{\diamond}^{2}}{N},\qquad\big\|\,\widetilde{A}_{\tau}^{\,N}-e^{\,t\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{eff}}}\,\big\|_{\diamond}\ \leq\ \frac{t^{2}\,\|\mathcal{L}\|_{\diamond}^{2}}{N}\,.

By Lemmas
G
,
G
and the triangle inequality, we thus have

\big\|\,A_{\tau}^{\prime\,N}-e^{\,t\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}}\,\big\|_{\diamond}\ \leq\ \frac{t^{2}\,\|\mathcal{L}\|_{\diamond}^{2}}{N}+2pN,\qquad\big\|\,\widetilde{A}_{\tau}^{\prime\,N}-e^{\,t\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{\mathrm{eff}}}\,\big\|_{\diamond}\ \leq\ \frac{t^{2}\,\|\mathcal{L}\|_{\diamond}^{2}}{N}\,+2pN.

Under these bounds, the error does not strictly decrease as
N
is increased. Minimizing the RHS gives

N_{\rm opt}=\frac{t\,\|\mathcal{L}\|_{\diamond}}{\sqrt{2p}}

In both cases, we thus obtain minimum
N
-round channel error bounded by
2\sqrt{2}t\|\mathcal{L}\|_{\diamond}\sqrt{p}
.

The HDD Hamiltonian learner sensitivity to the interface noise is bottle-necked by the candidate identification step, i.e.
Algorithm

5
. By Eq. (
147
), we want the interface error to be at most
\mathcal{O}\left(1/M^{2}\right)
. Therefore,

t\|\mathcal{L}\|_{\diamond}\sqrt{p}<\mathcal{O}\left(1/M^{2}\right)\ .

Hence, under the longest evolution time
t=\mathcal{O}\left((M\epsilon)^{-1}\right)
of
Algorithm

5
, we can only do so when
\epsilon>cM^{2}\sqrt{p}
. Noting that for small
\nu,t_{c}
, we have
p_{\rm int}=O(\nu qt_{c})
, which concludes the proof of the theorem.
∎

Appendix H
Reducing the ancilla cost of candidate identification

The dissipator structure learner
Algorithm

4
and the HDD Hamiltonian learner
Algorithm

7
use Bell sampling for candidate identification in
Algorithms

2
and
5
. In this section, we show that Bell sampling can be replaced by population recovery, following the construction of
70
. This substitution reduces the ancilla cost from
n+\mathcal{O}\left(\log M\right)
to
\mathcal{O}\left(\log M\right)
at the cost of increasing the total evolution time by
\mathcal{O}\left(M^{2}\log n\right)
as stated in
Theorem

H.2
.

Before invoking the population-recovery theorem of
50
, we first record the identities that make this replacement possible. The key observation is that the Bell-sampling probabilities coincide with the diagonal
\chi
-rates of the effective logical channel; see Eq. (
127
) and Eq. (
167
). Let
\overline{\mathcal{P}}=N(S)/S
denote the logical Pauli group on the code space
\Pi\mathcal{H}_{n^{\prime}}
of a stabilizer code with stabilizer group
S
. We write the effective logical channel (Eq. (
70
) and Eq. (
72
)) in the logical Pauli basis as

e^{\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{eff}}t}(\rho_{c})=\sum_{m,k}\overline{\chi}_{mk}(t)\,\overline{Q}_{m}\rho_{c}\overline{Q}_{k},

(183)

where
\rho_{c}\in\mathfrak{A}_{c}
is an arbitrary code state and the sum ranges over logical Paulis
\overline{Q}_{m},\overline{Q}_{k}\in\overline{\mathcal{P}}
. By Eq. (
127
) and Eq. (
167
):

\Pr(\text{sample $Q_r$ modulo stabilizers})=\Pr(\text{sample $\overline{Q}_r$})=\overline{\chi}_{rr}(t).

For dissipator-structure identification, Eq. (
117
) gives

\overline{\chi}_{rr}=\Pr(\text{sample $\overline Q_r$})=\Pr(\text{sample $Q_r$ modulo stabilizers})\geq\frac{1}{4e\,2^{j}\,\widehat{\Gamma}_{j}}\geq\frac{1}{4eM_{D}}.

(184)

The final inequality uses the sparsity bound and the round-
j
hierarchy,
\widehat{\Gamma}_{j}\leq 2^{-j}M_{D}
, matching the main-text exposition. The same reasoning applies to the desparsified version.

Similarly, for HDD candidate identification, Eq. (
148
) gives

\overline{\chi}_{rr}=\Pr(\text{sample $\overline Q_r$})=\Pr(\text{sample $P_r$ modulo stabilizers}\mid A_{P_r}\wedge E_{\mathrm{tar}})\geq\frac{1}{300M^{2}}.

(185)

Thus, in both hierarchical candidate-identification routines, the relevant logical Pauli rates are lower bounded by sparsity-dependent constants, independent of the final target accuracy
\varepsilon
and threshold
\eta
. Consequently, population recovery can replace Bell sampling without changing the
\varepsilon
- or
\eta
-dependence of the guarantees in
Algorithms

7
and
4
.

We now import the Pauli channel estimation algorithm by
50
.

Theorem H.1

(Learning Pauli error rates via population recovery
50
)
.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be an
n
-qubit channel with diagonal Pauli
\chi
-rates
\{\chi_{ii}\}
.
For any
0<\epsilon,\delta<1
, there exists an ancilla-free procedure that uses

m\;=\;\mathcal{O}\!\left(\epsilon^{-2}\log\frac{n}{\epsilon\delta}\right)

(186)

applications of
\mathcal{E}
and prepares/measures only product single-qubit Pauli eigenstates.
With probability at least
1-\delta
, it outputs a hypothesis
\widehat{\chi}
such that

\max_{i}|\widehat{\chi}_{ii}-\chi_{ii}|\;\leq\;\epsilon.

(187)

The hypothesis
\widehat{\chi}
is supported on a set
\widehat{S}\subseteq\{0,\dots,4^{n}-1\}

of size
|\widehat{S}|\;\leq\;\frac{4}{\epsilon}
(i.e.,
\widehat{\chi}_{ii}=0
for
i\notin\widehat{S}
)

The classical post-processing runs in time
\mathcal{O}\!\left(mn/\epsilon\right)=\widetilde{O}(n/\epsilon^{3})
, where
\widetilde{O}(\cdot)
omits the
\operatorname{polylog}()\,
factors in
(n,1/\epsilon,1/\delta)
.

The same statement applies to the logical
\chi
-matrix representation in Eq. (
183
): one simply runs population recovery using logical Pauli eigenstate preparations and logical Pauli-basis measurements.

Therefore, for dissipator candidate identification,
Theorem

H.1
with
\epsilon=\mathcal{O}\left(1/M_{D}\right)
from Eq. (
184
) implies that

m_{\mathrm{diss,PR}}=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(M_{D}^{2}\log n\right)

population-recovery experiments suffice for candidate identification, compared with

m_{\mathrm{diss,Bell}}=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(M_{D}\right)
Bell-sampling experiments. Thus replacing logical Bell sampling by logical population recovery incurs an additional factor
M_{D}\log n
in the dissipator-structure learner of
Theorem

D.1
(in the desparsified version, the corresponding factor is
2^{j}\widehat{\Gamma}_{j}\log n
). This increases to the total evolution time of the full dissipator structure learner from
Theorem

D.1
to

t_{\mathrm{diss,PR}}=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{M_{D}\log n}{\eta}\right),

(188)

while the ancilla cost is reduced from
n+\mathcal{O}\left(\log M\right)
to
\mathcal{O}\left(\log M\right)
.

Similarly, for Hamiltonian candidate identification,
Theorem

H.1
with
\epsilon=\mathcal{O}\left(1/M^{2}\right)
from Eq. (
185
) implies that:

m_{\mathrm{Hamilt,PR}}=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(M^{4}\log n\right)

population-recovery experiments suffice for candidate identification, compared with

m_{\mathrm{Hamilt,Bell}}=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(M^{2}\right)
Bell-sampling experiments. Therefore replacing logical Bell sampling by logical population recovery incurs an additional factor
M^{2}\log n
in the Hamiltonian structure learner of
Theorem

E.7
. This increases to the total evolution time of the HDD Hamiltonian learner from
Theorem

E.3
to

t_{\mathrm{Hamilt,PR}}=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{M^{4}\log n}{\varepsilon}\right),

(189)

while the ancilla cost is reduced from
n+\mathcal{O}\left(\log M\right)
to
\mathcal{O}\left(\log M\right)
.

Combining the population-recovery total evolution times Eq. (
189
) and Eq. (
188
) with
\eta=\varepsilon/M_{D}^{2}
from
Section

E.5
yields an ancilla-reduced version of
Result

1
:

Theorem H.2

(Ancilla-reduced Ansatz-free HDD Hamiltonian Learning)
.

There exists a quantum algorithm that, under the balanced Kossakowski tail condition (
Section

E.5
with
\kappa\leq M_{D}
), outputs an estimator of the true Hamiltonian-Disjoint-from-Dissipator coefficients such that with high probability:

|\widehat{h_{k}}-h_{k}|\leq\varepsilon,\qquad\forall P_{k}\in\mathcal{S}_{\mathrm{HDD},\varepsilon}

The total evolution time under
e^{\mathcal{L}t}
is Heisenberg-limited:

t_{\mathrm{tot}}=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{M^{4}\log n}{\epsilon}\right).

(190)

The algorithm employs only Clifford operations, uses
\mathcal{O}\left(\log M\right)
noiseless ancillary qubits, and has classical processing cost
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\operatorname{poly}(n,M,1/\epsilon)\right)
.

This replacement, however, does not improve the ancilla cost of
Result

2
, since the full Lindbladian learner remains bottlenecked by the SQL coefficient-learning stage of Appendix
F
, which uses
n
ancillary qubits to form the Choi state. For local or sparsely interacting Lindbladians, SQL coefficient learning can be performed efficiently without ancillas
74
;
125
. Extending such ancilla-reduced SQL Lindbladian learning to arbitrary-locality generators remains an open question.
